There exists a QPT algorithm
\mathpzc{Ext}
that satisfies the following.
Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
\epsilon\in[0,1/2]
, and
\mathpzc{A}
be a quantum algorithm with a quantum auxiliary input
\mathpzc{aux}
such that

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathpzc{A}(\mathpzc{aux},r)\rightarrow x\cdot r~\middle|~r\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}\right]\geq\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon.

(17)

Then, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathpzc{Ext}([\mathpzc{A}],\mathpzc{aux})\rightarrow x\right]\geq 4\epsilon^{2}.

(18)

where
[\mathpzc{A}]
means the description of
\mathpzc{A}
.

3
Public Key Encryption with Secure Key Leasing

In this section, we define the notion of public key encryption with secure key leasing (PKE-SKL) and its various security notions. Then we show several general relationships among those security notions.

3.1
Definitions

The syntax of PKE-SKL is defined as follows.

Definition 3.1 (PKE with Secure Key Leasing).

A PKE-SKL scheme
\mathsf{SKL}
is a tuple of four algorithms
(\mathpzc{KG},\mathsf{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Vrfy})
.
Below, let
\mathcal{X}
be the message space of
\mathsf{SKL}
.

\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{ek},\mathpzc{dk},\mathsf{vk})
:

The key generation algorithm takes a security parameter
1^{\lambda}
, and outputs an encryption key
\mathsf{ek}
, a decryption key
\mathpzc{dk}
, and a verification key
\mathsf{vk}
.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{m})\rightarrow\mathsf{ct}
:

The encryption algorithm takes an encryption key
\mathsf{ek}
and a message
\mathsf{m}\in\mathcal{X}
, and outputs a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
.

\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathpzc{dk},\mathsf{ct})\rightarrow\tilde{\mathsf{m}}
:

The decryption algorithm takes a decryption key
\mathpzc{dk}
and a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
, and outputs a value
\tilde{\mathsf{m}}
.

\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}})\rightarrow\top/\bot
:

The verification algorithm takes a verification key
\mathsf{vk}
and a (possibly malformed) decryption key
\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}
, and outputs
\top
or
\bot
.

Decryption correctness:

For every
\mathsf{m}\in\mathcal{X}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathpzc{dk},\mathsf{ct})\allowbreak=\mathsf{m}\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{ek},\mathpzc{dk},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{m})\end{array}\right]=1-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Verification correctness:

We have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{dk})=\top\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{ek},\mathpzc{dk},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})\end{array}\right]=1-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Remark
3.2.

We can assume without loss of generality that a decryption key of a PKE-SKL scheme is reusable, i.e., it can be reused to decrypt (polynomially) many ciphertexts. In particular, we can asusme that
for honestly generated
\mathsf{ct}
and
\mathpzc{dk}
, if we decrypt
\mathsf{ct}
by using
\mathpzc{dk}
, the state of the decryption key after the decryption is negligibly close to that before the decryption in terms of trace distance.
This is because the output of the decryption is almost deterministic by decryption correctness, and thus such an operation can be done without almost disturbing the input state by the gentle measurement lemma
[
Win99
]
.
A similar remark applies to all variants of PKE-SKL (IBE, ABE, and FE with SKL) defined in this paper.

Remark
3.3.

Though we are the first to define PKE with secure key leasing, SKFE with secure key leasing was already defined by Kitagawa and Nishimaki
[
KN22a
]
.
The above definition is a natural adaptation of their definition with the important difference that we do not require classical certificate of deletion.

We define several security notions for PKE-SKL.
The first is a natural indistinguishability security definition, which is our primary taget.

Definition 3.4 (IND-KLA Security).

We say that a PKE-SKL scheme
\mathsf{SKL}
with the message space
\mathcal{X}
is IND-KLA secure, if it satisfies the following requirement, formalized from the experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{SKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})
between an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
and a challenger
\mathpzc{C}
:

1.

\mathpzc{C}
runs
(\mathsf{ek},\mathpzc{dk},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})
and sends
\mathsf{ek}
and
\mathpzc{dk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

Throughout the experiment,
\mathpzc{A}
can access the following (stateful) verification oracle
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}
where
V
is initialized to be
\bot
:

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}(\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}})
:

It runs
d\leftarrow\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}})
and returns
d
.
If
V=\bot
and
d=\top
, it updates
V\coloneqq\top
.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
sends
(\mathsf{m}_{0}^{*},\mathsf{m}_{1}^{*})\in\mathcal{X}^{2}

to
\mathpzc{C}
. If
V=\bot
,
\mathpzc{C}
output
0
as the final output of this experiment. Otherwise,
\mathpzc{C}
generates
\mathsf{ct}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{m}_{\mathsf{coin}}^{*})
and sends
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs a guess
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
for
\mathsf{coin}
.
\mathpzc{C}
outputs
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
as the final output of the experiment.

For any QPT
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{SKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[\expb{\PKESKL,\qA}{ind}{kla} (1^\secp,0) \ra 1] - \Pr[\expb{\PKESKL,\qA}{ind}{kla} (1^\secp,1) \ra 1] }\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(22)

We say that
\mathsf{SKL}
is 1-query IND-KLA secure if the above holds for any QPT
\mathpzc{A}
that makes at most one query to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}
.

Remark
3.5.

When we consider a 1-query adversary, we can assume that its query is made before receiving the challenge ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
without loss of generality. This is because otherwise the experiment always outputs
0
.

Remark
3.6.

By a standard hybrid argument, one can show that IND-KLA security implies multi-challenge IND-KLA security where the adversary is allowed to request arbitrarily many challenge ciphertexts. Thus, if we have an IND-KLA secure PKE-SKL scheme for single-bit messages, we can extend the plaintext length to an arbitrary polynomial by bit-by-bit encryption.

We also define the one-way variant of the above security.

Definition 3.7 (OW-KLA Security).

We say that a PKE-SKL scheme
\mathsf{SKL}
with the message space
\mathcal{X}
is OW-KLA secure, if it satisfies the following requirement, formalized from the experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{SKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ow}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda})
between an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
and a challenger
\mathpzc{C}
:

1.

\mathpzc{C}

runs
(\mathsf{ek},\mathpzc{dk},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})
and sends
\mathsf{ek}
and
\mathpzc{dk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

Throughout the experiment,
\mathpzc{A}
can access the following (stateful) verification oracle
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}
where
V
is initialized to be
\bot
:

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}(\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}})
:

It runs
d\leftarrow\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}})
and returns
d
.
If
V=\bot
and
d=\top
, it updates
V\coloneqq\top
.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
sends
\mathsf{RequestChallenge}

to
\mathpzc{C}
. If
V=\bot
,
\mathpzc{C}
outputs
0
as the final output of this experiment. Otherwise,
\mathpzc{C}

chooses
\mathsf{m}^{*}\leftarrow\mathcal{X}
,
generates
\mathsf{ct}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{m}^{*})
and sends
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs
\mathsf{m}
.
\mathpzc{C}
outputs
1
if
\mathsf{m}=\mathsf{m}^{*}
and otherwise outputs
0
as the final output of the experiment.

For any QPT
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{SKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ow}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{SKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ow}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow 1]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(23)

We say that
\mathsf{SKL}
is 1-query OW-KLA secure if the above holds for any QPT
\mathpzc{A}
that makes at most one query to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}
.

Similar to normal PKE, IND-KLA security implies OW-KLA security if
|\mathcal{X}|
is super-polynomial in
\lambda
.

Finally, we define a security notion which we call one-more unreturnability (OMUR), which requires that an adversary given a single copy of the decryption key cannot pass the verification more than once. Though this does not seem very meaningful by itself, this is a useful intermediate tool for our final goal of constructing IND-KLA secure scheme.

Definition 3.8 (One-More Unreturnability).

We say that a PKE-SKL scheme
\mathsf{SKL}
with the message space
\mathcal{X}
satisfies One-More UnReturnability (OMUR), if it satisfies the following requirement, formalized from the experiment
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathsf{SKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{omur}}(1^{\lambda})
between an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
and a challenger
\mathpzc{C}
:

1.

\mathpzc{C}

runs
(\mathsf{ek},\mathpzc{dk},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})
and sends
\mathsf{ek}
and
\mathpzc{dk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

Throughout the experiment,
\mathpzc{A}
can access the following (stateful) verification oracle
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}
where
\mathsf{count}
is initialized to be
0
:

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}(\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}})
:

It runs
d\leftarrow\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}})
and
returns
d
.
It updates
\mathsf{count}\coloneqq\mathsf{count}+1
if
d=\top
.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
sends
\mathsf{Finish}

to
\mathpzc{C}
. If
\mathsf{count}\geq 2
,
\mathpzc{C}
outputs
1
and
0
otherwise
as the final output of this experiment.

For any QPT
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{SKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{omur}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\Pr[\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathsf{SKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{omur}}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow 1]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(24)

3.2
Relationships among Security Notions

We show several relationships among different security notions for PKE-SKL. In particular,
we show the following theorem.

Theorem 3.9.

If there exists a 1-query OW-KLA secure PKE-SKL scheme, there exists an IND-KLA secure PKE-SKL scheme.

This theorem simplifies our task: For constructing a (
{\mathrm{poly}}
-query) IND-KLA secure scheme, it suffices to construct a 1-query OW-KLA secure scheme.
We construct a 1-query OW-KLA secure scheme in
Section

5
.

We prove
Theorem

3.9
in the following three steps.

1.

Give a conversion to add OMUR to any 1-query OW-KLA secure scheme (
Lemma

3.10
).

2.

Convert a 1-query OW-KLA secure scheme that satisfies OMUR to a 1-query IND-KLA secure scheme that satisfies OMUR (
Lemma

3.12
).

3.

Show that any 1-query IND-KLA secure scheme that satisfies OMUR is IND-KLA secure (
Lemma

3.14
).

It is clear that
Theorem

3.9
follows from
Lemmas

3.10
,
3.12
and
3.14
.
We prove them in the following.

Lemma 3.10.

If there exists a 1-query OW-KLA secure PKE-SKL scheme, then there exists a 1-query OW-KLA secure PKE-SKL scheme that satisfies OMUR.

Remark
3.11.

This lemma is actually not needed for the purpose of this paper since our construction of a 1-query OW-KLA secure PKE-SKL scheme in
Section

5
already satisfies OMUR as mentioned in
Remark

5.7
. We include this lemma in the paper because this general reduction may be useful in future works.

Proof of
Lemma

3.10
.

Let
\mathsf{OW}=(\mathsf{OW}.\mathpzc{KG},\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{OW}.\mathpzc{Dec},\mathsf{OW}.\mathpzc{Vrfy})
be a 1-query OW-KLA secure PKE-SKL scheme with the message space
\mathcal{X}
.
We assume that a decryption key of
\mathsf{OW}
is reusable in the sense of
Remark

3.2

and
\mathsf{vk}
contains
\mathsf{ek}

without loss of generality.
Then we consider a modified PKE-SKL scheme
\mathsf{OW}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{OW}^{\prime}.\mathpzc{KG},\mathsf{OW}^{\prime}.\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{OW}^{\prime}.\mathpzc{Dec},\mathsf{OW}^{\prime}.\mathpzc{Vrfy})
with the same message space
\mathcal{X}
defined as follows.
The algorithms
\mathsf{OW}^{\prime}.\mathpzc{KG}
,
\mathsf{OW}^{\prime}.\mathsf{Enc}
, and
\mathsf{OW}^{\prime}.\mathpzc{Dec}
are identical to
\mathsf{OW}.\mathpzc{KG}
,
\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{Enc}
, and
\mathsf{OW}.\mathpzc{Dec}
, respectively. The algorithm
\mathsf{OW}^{\prime}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}
works as follows:

\mathsf{OW}^{\prime}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}})
:

On input a verification key
\mathsf{vk}
and a (possibly malformed) decryption key
\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}
, do the following:

Decryptability verification:

Choose
\mathsf{m}\leftarrow\mathcal{X}
and run
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{m})
and
\mathsf{m}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{Dec}(\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}},\mathsf{ct})
. If
\mathsf{m}^{\prime}\neq\mathsf{m}
, return
\bot
.

Original verification:

Otherwise, let
\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}^{\prime}
be the state of the decryption key after running the decryption algorithm.
Run
\mathsf{OW}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}^{\prime})
and return whatever
\mathsf{OW}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}
returns.

Correctness.

The decryption correctness of
\mathsf{OW}^{\prime}
follows from that of
\mathsf{OW}
because the only difference between these schemes is the verification algorithm, which is irrelevant to the decryption correctness.
The verification correctness of
\mathsf{OW}^{\prime}
follows from that of
\mathsf{OW}
because we assume that
\mathsf{OW}
has reusable decryption keys and thus
\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{OW}^{\prime}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}
has a negligible trace distance from
\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}
, which passes
\mathsf{OW}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}
except for a negligible probability by the verification correctness of
\mathsf{OW}
.

1-query OW-SKL security.

The 1-query OW-SKL security of
\mathsf{OW}^{\prime}
follows from that of
\mathsf{OW}
by a straightforward reduction. Specifically, let
\mathpzc{A}
be an QPT adversary that breaks the 1-query OW-SKL security of
\mathsf{OW}^{\prime}
. Then, we construct a QPT adversary
\mathpzc{B}
that breaks the 1-query OW-SKL security of
\mathsf{OW}
as follows:

\mathpzc{B}(\mathsf{ek},\mathpzc{dk})
:

Run
\mathpzc{A}(\mathsf{ek},\mathpzc{dk})
until
\mathpzc{A}
makes a verification query
\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}
. For simulating the verification oracle to
\mathpzc{A}
,
choose
\mathsf{m}\leftarrow\mathcal{X}
, run
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{m})
and
\mathsf{m}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{Dec}(\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}},\mathsf{ct})
, and let
\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}^{\prime}
be the state of the decryption key after running the decryption algorithm.
If
\mathsf{m}^{\prime}\neq\mathsf{m}
, output
0
and immediately halt. Otherwise, query
\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}^{\prime}
to its own verification oracle, and forward the response to
\mathpzc{A}
.
When
\mathpzc{A}
sends
\mathsf{RequestChallenge}
, forward it to the external challenger to receive
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
and forward it to
\mathpzc{A}
. Run
\mathpzc{A}
until it halts and output whatever
\mathpzc{A}
outputs.

We can see that the experiment which
\mathpzc{B}
plays outputs
1
if and only if the (simulated) experiment which
\mathpzc{A}
plays outputs
1
. Therefore,
\mathpzc{B}
breaks the 1-query OW-SKL security of
\mathsf{OW}
. Thus, the 1-query OW-SKL security of
\mathsf{OW}^{\prime}
follows from that of
\mathsf{OW}
.

OMUR.

In the following, we show that
\mathsf{OW}^{\prime}
satisfies OMUR. Let
\mathpzc{A}
be a QPT adversary against the OMUR of
\mathsf{OW}^{\prime}
that makes
Q={\mathrm{poly}}(\lambda)
verification queries. Then we consider the following sequence of hybrids.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
:

This is identical to the experiment
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathsf{OW}^{\prime},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{omur}}(1^{\lambda})
as defined in
Definition

3.8
.

Note that we have

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]=\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{OW}^{\prime},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{omur}}(\lambda).

(25)

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
:

This is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
except that the challenger uniformly chooses integers
1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}\leq Q
at the beginning of the experiment and outputs
1
if and only if
i_{1}
-th and
i_{2}
-th verification queries are the first two queries to which the verification oracle returned
\top
.

Whenever
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
returns
1
, there are at least
2
verification queries accepted by the verification oracle.
Therefore, when we uniformly choose
1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}\leq Q
, the probability that
i_{1}
-th and
i_{2}
-th queries are the first two queries to be accepted is
{\binom{Q}{2}}^{-1}=\frac{2}{Q(Q-1)}
. Therefore we have

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]=\frac{2}{Q(Q-1)}\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1].

(26)

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
:

This is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
except that the verification oracle just returns
\bot
without running the verification algorithm
to
i
-th query for all
i\in[i_{2}-1]\setminus\{i_{1}\}
and the experiment halts right after running the verification oracle for the
i_{2}
-th query where it outputs
1
if and only if the verification oracle returned
\top
to both
i_{1}
-th and
i_{2}
-th queries.

When
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
returns
1
, the verification oracle returns
\bot
to
i
-th query for all
i\in[i_{2}-1]\setminus\{i_{1}\}
since otherwise
i_{1}
-th and
i_{2}
-th queries cannot be the first
2
queries to be accepted. Therefore, these hybrids are identical until
\mathpzc{A}
makes
i_{2}
-th query when
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
returns
1
.
9
9

9

Note that there is a superficial difference that the verification oracle of
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
runs the verification algorithm to
i
-th query for all
i\in[i_{2}-1]\setminus\{i_{1}\}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
but it does not in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
. However since these query registers are not used at all for generating the output of
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
, the difference of if measurements are applied on them cannot affect the probability to output
1
.

Moreover,
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
outputs
1
whenever
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
outputs
1
if we run the rest of
\mathpzc{A}
to complete
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]\geq\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1].

(27)

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
:

This is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
except that the experiment outputs
1
if and only if
i_{1}
-th query is accepted and
i_{2}
-th query passes the “Decryptability verification" part of
\mathsf{OW}^{\prime}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}
, i.e.,
\mathsf{m}=\mathsf{m}^{\prime}
in the notation of the description of
\mathsf{OW}^{\prime}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}
.

Since the condition to output
1
is just relaxed, we have

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]\geq\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1].

(28)

Below, we prove

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(29)

To prove this, we consider the following QPT adversary
\mathpzc{B}
against the 1-query OW-SKL security of
\mathsf{OW}
that works as follows:

\mathpzc{B}(\mathsf{ek},\mathpzc{dk})
:

Uniformly choose integers
1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}\leq Q
and run
\mathpzc{A}(\mathsf{ek},\mathpzc{dk})
until it makes
i_{2}
-th query where the response by the verification oracle to
\mathpzc{A}
’s
i
-th query for
i\in[i_{2}-1]
is simulated as follows: If
i\neq i_{1}
, return
\bot
as the response from the verification oracle. If
i=i_{1}
, forward the query to its own verification oracle and forward the response to
\mathpzc{A}
. Let
\mathpzc{dk}_{i_{2}}
be
\mathpzc{A}
’s
i_{2}
-th verification query.
Send
\mathsf{RequestChallenge}
to the external challenger to receive
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
. Run
\mathsf{m}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{ct}^{*},\mathpzc{dk})
and output
\mathsf{m}^{\prime}
.

By the definitions of
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
and
\mathpzc{B}
, we can see that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{OW},\mathpzc{B}}^{\mathsf{ow}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(\lambda)=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1].

(30)

Thus, we have
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
by the 1-query OW-SKL security of
\mathsf{OW}
.

Combining the above, we have
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{OW}^{\prime},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{omur}}(\lambda)={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
, which means that
\mathsf{OW}
satisfies OMUR. This completes the proof of
Lemma

3.10
.
∎

Lemma 3.12.

If there exists a 1-query OW-KLA secure PKE-SKL scheme, then there exists a 1-query IND-KLA secure PKE-SKL scheme. Moreover, if the base scheme satisfies OMUR, then then the resulting scheme satisfies OMUR.

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{OW}=(\mathsf{OW}.\mathpzc{KG},\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{OW}.\mathpzc{Dec},\mathsf{OW}.\mathpzc{Vrfy})
be a 1-query OW-KLA secure PKE-SKL scheme with the message space
\{0,1\}^{n}
that satisfies OMUR. Then, we construct an IND-KLA secure PKE-SKL scheme
\mathsf{IND}=(\mathsf{IND}.\mathpzc{KG},\mathsf{IND}.\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{IND}.\mathpzc{Dec},\mathsf{IND}.\mathpzc{Vrfy})
with the message space
\{0,1\}
as follows.

\mathsf{IND}.\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{ek},\mathpzc{dk},\mathsf{vk})
:

On input the security parameter
1^{\lambda}
,
run
(\mathsf{ek},\mathpzc{dk},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathsf{OW}.\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})
and output
(\mathsf{ek},\mathpzc{dk},\mathsf{vk})
.

\mathsf{IND}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{m})\rightarrow\mathsf{IND}.\mathsf{ct}
:

On input an encryption key
\mathsf{ek}
and a message
\mathsf{m}\in\{0,1\}
,
choose
r,x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
,
generate
\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},x)
,
set
b\coloneqq(x\cdot r)\oplus\mathsf{m}
,
and output a ciphertext
\mathsf{IND}.\mathsf{ct}\coloneqq(\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{ct},r,b)
.

\mathsf{IND}.\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathpzc{dk},\mathsf{IND}.\mathsf{ct})\rightarrow\tilde{\mathsf{m}}
:

On input a decryption key
\mathpzc{dk}
and a ciphertext
\mathsf{IND}.\mathsf{ct}=(\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{ct},r,b)
,
compute
\tilde{x}\leftarrow\mathsf{OW}.\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathpzc{dk},\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{ct})

and output
\tilde{\mathsf{m}}\coloneqq(\tilde{x}\cdot r)\oplus b
.

\mathsf{IND}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}})\rightarrow\top/\bot
:

On input a verification key
\mathsf{vk}
and a (possibly malformed) decryption key
\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}
, run
\mathsf{OW}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}})
and
output whatever
\mathsf{OW}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}
outputs.

The decryption correctness and verification correctness of
\mathsf{IND}
immediately follow from those of
\mathsf{OW}
.
The OMUR of
\mathsf{IND}
immediately follows from that of
\mathsf{OW}
since their key generation and verification algorithms are identical and the definition of OMUR only depends on these algorithms.
In the following, we prove that
\mathsf{IND}
is IND-KLA secure assuming that
\mathsf{OW}
is OW-KLA secure.
Toward contradiction, suppose that
\mathsf{IND}
is not IND-KLA secure. Then, there is a QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
such that
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{IND},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(\lambda)
is non-negligible. Without loss of generality, we assume that

\displaystyle\Pr_{\mathsf{coin}\leftarrow\{0,1\}}[\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{IND},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})\rightarrow\mathsf{coin}]\geq 1/2+\epsilon(\lambda)

(31)

for a non-negligible
\epsilon(\lambda)
. Since
\mathsf{IND}
is a bit encryption, we assume that the challenge message pair
(\mathsf{m}_{0},\mathsf{m}_{1})
is
(0,1)
without loss of generality.
We divide
\mathpzc{A}
into the following two stages
\mathpzc{A}_{0}
and
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
:

\mathpzc{A}_{0}^{O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}}(\mathsf{ek},\mathpzc{dk})\rightarrow\mathpzc{st}{A}
:

Upon receiving
(\mathsf{ek},\mathpzc{dk})
from
\mathpzc{C}
, makes a single query to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}
and outputs a quantum state
\mathpzc{st}{A}
.

\mathpzc{A}_{1}(\mathpzc{st}{A},\mathsf{IND}.\mathsf{ct})\rightarrow\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
:

Upon receiving the state
\mathpzc{st}_{\mathcal{A}}
from
\mathpzc{A}_{0}
and
\mathsf{IND}.\mathsf{ct}=(\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{ct},r,b)
from
\mathpzc{C}
, output
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
.

We remark that we can assume that
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
does not make any query to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}
without loss of generality by
Remark

3.5
.

We have

\displaystyle\Pr_{\mathsf{coin}\leftarrow\{0,1\}}[\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{IND},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})\rightarrow\mathsf{coin}]

(32)

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\mathsf{coin}\leftarrow\{0,1\}}[\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{IND},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})\rightarrow\mathsf{coin}\wedge V=\top]

(33)

\displaystyle+\Pr_{\mathsf{coin}\leftarrow\{0,1\}}[\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{IND},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})\rightarrow\mathsf{coin}\wedge V=\bot]

(34)

\displaystyle=\Pr[V=\top]\cdot\Pr_{\mathsf{coin}\leftarrow\{0,1\}}[\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{IND},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})\rightarrow\mathsf{coin}\mid V=\top]

(35)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}(1-\Pr[V=\top]).

(36)

By
Equations

31
and
36
, we have
10
10

10

We can assume
\Pr[V=\top]\neq 0
since otherwise
Equation

31
cannot be satisfied.

\displaystyle\Pr_{\mathsf{coin}\leftarrow\{0,1\}}[\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{IND},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})\rightarrow\mathsf{coin}\mid V=\top]\geq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\epsilon(\lambda)}{\Pr[V=\top]}.

(37)

Then, we construct an adversary
\mathpzc{B}=(\mathpzc{B}_{0},\mathpzc{B}_{1})
against OW-KLA security of
\mathsf{OW}
that works as follows.

\mathpzc{B}_{0}^{O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}}(\mathsf{ek},\mathpzc{dk})\rightarrow\mathpzc{st}{A}
:

This is identical to
\mathpzc{A}_{0}
. Specifically, run
\mathpzc{st}{A}\leftarrow\mathpzc{A}_{0}^{O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}}(\mathsf{ek},\mathpzc{dk})
and output
\mathpzc{st}{A}
.

\mathpzc{B}_{1}(\mathpzc{st}{A},\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{ct})\rightarrow x
:

Upon receiving

\mathpzc{st}{A}
from
\mathpzc{B}_{0}
, send
\mathsf{RequestChallenge}
to
\mathpzc{C}
and receive

\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{ct}
from
\mathpzc{C}
.
Then set
\mathpzc{aux}\coloneqq(\mathpzc{st}{A},\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{ct})
and define an algorithm
\mathpzc{A}^{\prime}
as follows.

\mathpzc{A}^{\prime}(\mathpzc{aux},r)
:

On input
\mathpzc{aux}=(\mathpzc{st}{A},\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{ct})
and
r\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, choose
b\leftarrow\{0,1\}
,
set
\mathsf{IND}.\mathsf{ct}=(\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{ct},r,b)
,
run
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathpzc{A}_{1}(\mathpzc{st}{A},\mathsf{IND}.\mathsf{ct})
, and output
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}\oplus b
.

Run
x\leftarrow\mathpzc{Ext}([\mathpzc{A}^{\prime}],\mathpzc{aux})
, and output
x
where

\mathpzc{Ext}
is the algorithm as in
Lemma

2.22
and

[\mathpzc{A}^{\prime}]
is the description of
\mathpzc{A}^{\prime}
.

In the following, we show that
\mathpzc{B}
breaks OW-KLA security of
\mathsf{OW}
.
Let
\mathpzc{G}
be an algorithm that works as follows.

\mathpzc{G}(1^{\lambda})
:

Generate
(\mathsf{ek},\mathpzc{dk},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathsf{OW}.\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})
,

\mathpzc{st}{A}\leftarrow\mathpzc{A}_{0}^{O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}}(\mathsf{ek},\mathpzc{dk})
,

x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
, and

\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},x)
.
Let
V\coloneqq\top
if the response to
\mathpzc{A}_{0}
’s query (which is assumed to be made once) is
\top
and
V\coloneqq\bot
otherwise.
Output
(V,\mathpzc{st}{A},\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{ct},x)
.

By
Equation

37
and a standard averaging argument,
for at least
\frac{\epsilon(\lambda)}{2\Pr[V=\top]}
-fraction of
(V,\mathpzc{st}{A},\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{ct},x)
generated by
\mathpzc{G}(1^{\lambda})
conditioned on
V=\top
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathpzc{A}_{1}(\mathpzc{st}{A},\mathsf{IND}.\mathsf{ct})\rightarrow\mathsf{coin}\right]\geq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\epsilon(\lambda)}{2\Pr[V=\top]}\geq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\epsilon(\lambda)}{2}

(38)

where
\mathsf{coin}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
,

r\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
,

b\coloneqq(x\cdot r)\oplus\mathsf{coin}
, and

\mathsf{IND}.\mathsf{ct}=(\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{ct},r,b).

Therefore, for at least
\frac{\epsilon(\lambda)}{2}
-fraction of
(V,\mathpzc{st}{A},\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{ct},x)
generated by
\mathpzc{G}(1^{\lambda})
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[V=\top\wedge\mathpzc{A}_{1}(\mathpzc{st}{A},\mathsf{IND}.\mathsf{ct})\rightarrow\mathsf{coin}\right]\geq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\epsilon(\lambda)}{2}

(39)

where
\mathsf{coin}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
,

r\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
,

b\coloneqq(x\cdot r)\oplus\mathsf{coin}
, and

\mathsf{IND}.\mathsf{ct}=(\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{ct},r,b).

For such
(V,\mathpzc{st}{A},\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{ct},x)
, if we let
\mathpzc{aux}=(\mathpzc{st}{A},\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{ct})
,
Equation

39
directly implies

\displaystyle\Pr_{r\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}}\left[\mathpzc{A}^{\prime}(\mathpzc{aux},r)\rightarrow x\cdot r\right]\geq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\epsilon(\lambda)}{2}.

(40)

Therefore, by
Lemma

2.22
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathpzc{Ext}([\mathpzc{A}^{\prime}],\mathpzc{aux})\rightarrow x\right]\geq\epsilon(\lambda)^{2}.

(41)

Since
Equation

41

and

V=\top
hold at the same time for at least
\frac{\epsilon(\lambda)}{2}
-fraction of
(V,\mathpzc{st}{A},\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{ct},x)
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr_{(\mathsf{v},\mathpzc{st}{A},\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{ct},x)\leftarrow\mathpzc{G}(1^{\lambda})}\left[V=\top\wedge\mathpzc{B}_{1}(\mathpzc{st}{A},\mathsf{OW}.\mathsf{ct})\rightarrow x\right]\geq\frac{\epsilon(\lambda)^{3}}{2}.

(42)

By the definitions of
\mathpzc{B}=(\mathpzc{B}_{0},\mathpzc{B}_{1})
and
\mathpzc{G}
and the assumption that
\epsilon(\lambda)
is non-negligible, this implies that
\mathpzc{B}
breaks OW-KLA security of
\mathsf{OW}
.
∎

Remark
3.13 (On Multiple-Query Case).

In the above reduction, it is important that
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
can be assumed to not make any verification query because otherwise we cannot apply the quantum Goldreich-Levin theorem (
Lemma

2.22
). In the 1-query setting, this can be assumed without loss of generality by
Remark

3.5
. In the multiple-query setting, we cannot assume it in general. If we assume that the base scheme satisfies OMUR, we can assume it without loss of generality because post-challenge verification queries are useless for such schemes. However, we do not know how to resolve the issue in the multiple-query setting without relying on OMUR.

Lemma 3.14.

If a PKE-SKL scheme is 1-query IND-KLA secure and satisfies OMUR, then it is IND-KLA secure.

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{SKL}=(\mathpzc{KG},\mathsf{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Vrfy})
be an IND-KLA secure PKE-SKL scheme that satisfies OMUR.
For a QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
against IND-KLA security of
\mathsf{SKL}
that makes
Q={\mathrm{poly}}(\lambda)
verification queries and
\mathsf{coin}\in\{0,1\}
, we consider the following sequence of hybrids.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}^{\mathsf{coin}}
:

This is identical to
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{SKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})
.

Note that our goal is to prove

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}^{0}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}^{1}=1]\right|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(43)

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{\mathsf{coin}}
:

This is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}^{\mathsf{coin}}
except that the verification oracle returns
\bot
to all queries made after it returns
\top
once.

By the OMUR of
\mathsf{SKL}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{\mathsf{coin}}\rightarrow 1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}^{\mathsf{coin}}\rightarrow 1]\right|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)

(44)

for
\mathsf{coin}\in\{0,1\}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}^{\mathsf{coin}}
:

This is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{\mathsf{coin}}
except that the challenger chooses
i^{*}\leftarrow[Q]
at the beginning of the game, the verification oracle just returns
\bot
without running the verification algorithm to
i
-th query for
i\neq i^{*}
, and the experiment returns
0
if the verification oracle returns
\bot
to
i^{*}
-th query.

Note that there is exactly one verification query to be accepted in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{\mathsf{coin}}
whenever it returns
1
. If
i^{*}
is the correct guess for such query, which occurs with probability
\frac{1}{Q}
, then
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}^{\mathsf{coin}}
is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{\mathsf{coin}}
.
11
11

11

A similar remark to
Footnote

9
applies here.

Moreover,
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}^{\mathsf{coin}}
outputs
0
when the guess is incorrect.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}^{\mathsf{coin}}\rightarrow 1]=\frac{1}{Q}\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{\mathsf{coin}}\rightarrow 1].

(45)

Below, we prove

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}^{0}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}^{1}=1]\right|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(46)

To prove this, we consider a QPT adversary
\mathpzc{B}
against
1
-query IND-KLA security of
\mathsf{SKL}
that works as follows.

\mathpzc{B}(\mathsf{ek},\mathpzc{dk})
:

Choose
i^{*}\leftarrow[Q]
and
run
\mathpzc{A}(\mathsf{ek},\mathpzc{dk})
where the
i^{*}
-th query is forwarded to its own verification oracle and responded according to the response from the oracle while all the other queries are responded by
\bot
.
When
\mathpzc{A}
sends
(\mathsf{m}_{0}^{*},\mathsf{m}_{1}^{*})
, forward it to the external challenger, receive
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
from the challenger, and forward it to
\mathpzc{A}
.
Finally, output whatever
\mathpzc{A}
outputs.

By the definitions of
\mathpzc{B}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}^{\mathsf{coin}}
, one can see that

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{SKL},\mathpzc{B}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})\rightarrow 1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}^{\mathsf{coin}}=1]

(47)

for
\mathsf{coin}\in\{0,1\}
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hybi{2}^{0}= 1] - \Pr[\hybi{2}^{1}= 1]}

(48)

\displaystyle=\absolutevalue{\Pr[\expb{\PKESKL,\qB}{ind}{kla} (1^\secp,0) \ra 1] - \Pr[\expb{\PKESKL,\qB}{ind}{kla} (1^\secp,1) \ra 1]}

(49)

\displaystyle={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)

(50)

by the
1
-query IND-SKL security of
\mathsf{SKL}
.

Combining the above, we have

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}^{0}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}^{1}=1]\right|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(51)

This completes the proof of
Lemma

3.14
.
∎

4
Public Key Encryption with CoIC-KLA Security

In this section, we introduce a new security notion called CoIC-KLA security for PKE, and construct a PKE scheme that satisfies it based on any IND-CPA secure PKE scheme. Looking ahead, it is used as a building block of our construction of PKE-SKL in
Section

5
.

4.1
Tools

We first introduce some tools used in this section.

Measurement Implementation.

We review some notions related to measurement implementations used in the definition and the security proof of CoIC-KLA security.

Definition 4.1 (Projective Implementation).

Let:

•

\mathcal{D}
be a finite set of distributions over an index set
\mathcal{I}
.

•

\mathcal{P}=\{\bm{P}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
be a positive operator valued measure (POVM).

•

\mathcal{E}=\{\bm{E}_{D}\}_{D\in\mathcal{D}}
be a projective measurement with index set
\mathcal{D}
.

We consider the following measurement procedure.

1.

Measure under the projective measurement
\mathcal{E}
and obtain a distribution
D
.

2.

Output a random sample from the distribution
D
.

We say
\mathcal{E}
is the projective implementation of
\mathcal{P}
, denoted by
\mathsf{ProjImp}(\mathcal{P})
, if the measurement process above is equivalent to
\mathcal{P}
.

Theorem 4.2 (
[
Zha20
, Lemma 1]
).

Any binary outcome POVM
\mathcal{P}=(\bm{P},\bm{I}-\bm{P})
has a unique projective implementation
\mathsf{ProjImp}(\mathcal{P})
.

Definition 4.3 (Shift Distance).

For two distributions
D_{0},D_{1}
, the shift distance with parameter
\epsilon
, denoted by
\Delta_{\mathsf{Shift}}^{\epsilon}(D_{0},D_{1})
, is the smallest quantity
\delta
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle\Pr[D_{0}\leq x]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr[D_{1}\leq x+\epsilon]+\delta,

\displaystyle\Pr[D_{0}\geq x]\leq\Pr[D_{1}\geq x-\epsilon]+\delta,

(52)

\displaystyle\Pr[D_{1}\leq x]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr[D_{0}\leq x+\epsilon]+\delta,

\displaystyle\Pr[D_{1}\geq x]\leq\Pr[D_{0}\geq x-\epsilon]+\delta.

(53)

For two real-valued measurements
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
over the same quantum system, the shift distance between
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
with parameter
\epsilon
is

\Delta_{\mathsf{Shift}}^{\epsilon}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})\coloneqq\sup_{\ket{\psi}}\Delta_{\mathsf{Shift}}^{\epsilon}(\mathcal{M}(\ket{\psi}),\mathcal{N}(\ket{\psi})).

(54)

Definition 4.4 (Mixture of Projetive Measurement
[
Zha20
]
).

Let
D:\mathcal{R}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}
where
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{I}
are some sets.
Let
\{(\bm{P}_{i},\bm{Q}_{i})\}_{\in\mathcal{I}}
be a collection of binary projective measurement.
The mixture of projective measurements associated to
\mathcal{R}
,
\mathcal{I}
,
D
, and
\{(\bm{P}_{i},\bm{Q}_{i})\}_{\in\mathcal{I}}
is the binary POVM
\mathcal{P}_{D}=(\bm{P}_{D},\bm{Q}_{D})
defined as follows.

\displaystyle\bm{P}_{D}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\Pr[i\leftarrow D(R)]\bm{P}_{i}

\displaystyle\bm{Q}_{D}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\Pr[i\leftarrow D(R)]\bm{Q}_{i},

(55)

where
R
is uniformly distributed in
\mathcal{R}
.

Theorem 4.5 (
[
Zha20
,
KN22b
]
).

Let
D
be any probability distribution and
\mathcal{P}=\{(\Pi_{i},\bm{I}-\Pi_{i})\}_{i}
be a collection of binary outcome projective measurements. For any
0<\epsilon,\delta<1
, there exists an algorithm of measurement
\mathpzc{API}_{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{D}}^{\epsilon,\delta}
that satisfies the following.

•

\Delta_{\mathsf{Shift}}^{\epsilon}(\mathpzc{API}_{\mathcal{P},D}^{\epsilon,\delta},\mathsf{ProjImp}(\mathcal{P}_{D}))\leq\delta
.

•

\mathpzc{API}_{\mathcal{P},D}^{\epsilon,\delta}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-almost projective in the following sense. For any quantum state
\ket{\psi}
, we apply
\mathpzc{API}_{\mathcal{P},D}^{\epsilon,\delta}
twice in a row to
\ket{\psi}
and obtain measurement outcomes
x
and
y
, respectively. Then,
\Pr[\absolutevalue{x-y}\leq\epsilon]\geq 1-\delta
.

•

\mathpzc{API}_{\mathcal{P},D}^{\epsilon,\delta}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-reverse almost projective in the following sense. For any quantum state
\ket{\psi}
, we apply
\mathpzc{API}_{\mathcal{P},D}^{\epsilon,\delta}
and
\mathpzc{API}_{\mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{rev}},D}^{\epsilon,\delta}
in a row to
\ket{\psi}
and obtain measurement outcomes
x
and
y
, respectively, where
\mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{rev}}=\{(\bm{I}-\Pi_{i},\Pi_{i})\}_{i}
. Then,
\Pr[\absolutevalue{(1-x)-y}\leq\epsilon]\geq 1-\delta
.

•

The expected running time of
\mathpzc{API}_{\mathcal{P},D}^{\epsilon,\delta}
is
T_{\mathcal{P},D}\cdot{\mathrm{poly}}(1/\epsilon,\log(1/\delta))
where
T_{\mathcal{P},D}
is the combined running time of
D
, the procedure mapping
i\rightarrow(\bm{P}_{i},\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{i})
, and the running time of measurement
(\bm{P}_{i},\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{i})
.

Theorem 4.6 (
[
Zha20
, Corollary 1]
).

Let
\mathpzc{q}
be an efficiently constructible, potentially mixed state, and
D_{0},D_{1}
efficiently sampleable distributions.
If
D_{0}
and
D_{1}
are computationally indistinguishable, for any inverse polynomial
\epsilon
and any function
\delta
, we have
\Delta_{\mathsf{Shift}}^{3\epsilon}(\mathpzc{API}_{\mathcal{P},D_{0}}^{\epsilon,\delta}(\mathpzc{q}),\mathpzc{API}_{\mathcal{P},D_{1}}^{\epsilon,\delta}(\mathpzc{q}))\leq 2\delta+{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

Definition 4.7 (Quantum Program with Classical Inputs and Outputs
[
ALL+21
]
).

A quantum program with classical inputs is a pair of quantum state
\mathpzc{q}
and unitaries
\{\bm{U}_{x}\}_{x\in[N]}
where
[N]
is the domain, such that the state of the program evaluated on input
x
is equal to
\bm{U}_{x}\mathpzc{q}\bm{U}_{x}^{\dagger}
. We measure the first register of
\bm{U}_{x}\mathpzc{q}\bm{U}_{x}^{\dagger}
to obtain an output. We say that
\{\bm{U}_{x}\}_{x\in[N]}
has a compact classical description
\bm{U}
when applying
\bm{U}_{x}
can be efficiently computed given
\bm{U}
and
x
.

Ciphertext-Policy Functional Encryption.

We review the definition of ciphertext-policy functional encryption (CPFE) that we use as the building block of our CoIC-KLA secure PKE scheme.

Definition 4.8 (Ciphertext-Policy Functional Encryption).

A CPFE scheme for the circuit space
\mathcal{C}
and the input space
\mathcal{X}
is a tuple of algorithms
(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KG},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
.

•

The setup algorithm
\mathsf{Setup}
takes as input a security parameter
1^{\lambda}
, and outputs a master public key
\mathsf{MPK}
and master secret key
\mathsf{MSK}
.

•

The key generation algorithm
\mathsf{KG}
takes as input the master secret key
\mathsf{MSK}
and
x\in\mathcal{X}
, and outputs a decryption key
\mathsf{sk}_{x}
.

•

The encryption algorithm
\mathsf{Enc}
takes as input the master public key
\mathsf{MPK}
and
C\in\mathcal{C}
, and outputs a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
.

•

The decryption algorithm
\mathsf{Dec}
takes as input a functional decryption key
\mathsf{sk}_{x}
and a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
, and outputs
y
.

Decryption Correctness:

We require
\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{MSK},x),\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},C))\allowbreak=C(x)
for every
C\in\mathcal{C}
,
x\in\mathcal{X}
, and
\left(\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{MSK}\right)\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

Next, we introduce
1
-bounded security for CPFE schemes.

Definition 4.9 (
1
-Bounded Security).

Let
\mathsf{CPFE}
be a CPFE scheme. We define the game
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{A},\mathsf{CPFE}}^{\mathsf{1\textrm{-}bounded}}(\lambda,\mathsf{coin})
as follows.

1.

The challenger generates
(\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and sends
\mathsf{MPK}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

\mathpzc{A}
sends
x\in\mathcal{X}
to the challenger. The challenger generates
\mathsf{sk}_{x}\leftarrow\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{MSK},x)
and sends
\mathsf{sk}_{x}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs
(C_{0},C_{1})
such that
C_{0}(x)=C_{1}(x)
and
C_{0}
and
C_{1}
have the same size. The challenger picks
\mathsf{coin}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
, generates
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},C_{\mathsf{coin}})
, and sends
\mathsf{ct}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
.

We say that
\mathsf{CPFE}
is
1
-bounded secure if for every QPT
\mathpzc{A}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathpzc{A},\mathsf{CPFE}}^{\mathsf{1\textrm{-}bounded}}(\lambda)=2\absolutevalue{\Pr[
\expt{\qA,\CPFE}{1\textrm{-}bounded}(\secp)=1
] -\frac{1}{2}}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(56)

Theorem 4.10 (
[
GVW12
]
).

If there exists IND-CPA secure PKE, there exists
1
-bounded secure CPFE for
\mathsf{P/poly}
.
12
12

12

Though
[
GVW12
]
present their construction as KPFE instead of CPFE, it is easy to see that they implicitly give CPFE.

4.2
Definitions of CoIC-KLA Security

We introduce definitions of CoIC-KLA security.
In addition to normal CoIC-KLA security needed to realize our PKE-SKL, we also define what we call strong CoIC-KLA security.
We can prove that strong CoIC-KLA security implies CoIC-KLA security.
The reason we introduce strong CoIC-KLA is that it is more compatible to our construction strategy in
Section

4.3
that uses watermarking technique by Kitagawa and Nishimaki
[
KN22b
]
.

Definition 4.11 (CoIC-KLA Security).

We say that a PKE scheme
\mathsf{PKE}
with the message space
\mathcal{X}
is CoIC-KLA secure, if it satisfies the following requirement, formalized from the experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{PKE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{coic}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda})
between an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
and a challenger
\mathpzc{C}
:

1.

\mathpzc{C}
runs
(\mathsf{ek}_{0},\mathsf{dk}_{0})\leftarrow\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})
and
(\mathsf{ek}_{1},\mathsf{dk}_{1})\leftarrow\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})
, and generates
\mathpzc{dk}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{0}\ket{\dk_0}+\ket{1}\ket{\dk_1})
.
\mathpzc{C}
sends
\mathsf{ek}_{0}
,
\mathsf{ek}_{1}
, and
\mathpzc{dk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
\mathpzc{A}
can get access to the following oracle only once.

\mathcal{O}(\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}})
:

On input a possibly malformed decryption key
\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}
, it applies a binary-outcome measurement
(\bm{I}-\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}},\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}})
, where
\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}
is the projection to the right decryption key, i.e.,

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}\coloneqq\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\ket{0}\ket{\dk_{0}}+\ket{1}\ket{\dk_{1}}\right)\right)\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\bra{0}\bra{\dk_{0}}+\bra{1}\bra{\dk_{1}}\right)\right).

(57)

It returns the measurement outcome (indicating whether the state was projected onto
\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}
or not).

2.

\mathpzc{A}
sends
(\mathsf{m}_{0}^{*},\mathsf{m}_{1}^{*})\in\mathcal{X}^{2}
to
\mathpzc{C}
.
\mathpzc{C}
generates
a,b\leftarrow\{0,1\}
and generates
\mathsf{ct}_{0}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek}_{0},\mathsf{m}_{a}^{*})
and
\mathsf{ct}_{1}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek}_{1},\mathsf{m}_{a\oplus b}^{*})
.
\mathpzc{C}
sends
\mathsf{ct}_{0}^{*}
and
\mathsf{ct}_{1}^{*}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs a guess
b^{\prime}
for
b
.
\mathpzc{C}
outputs
1
if
b=b^{\prime}
and
0
otherwise as the final output of the experiment.

For any QPT
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{PKE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{coic}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(\lambda)\coloneqq 2\cdot\absolutevalue{\Pr[\expb{\PKE,\qA}{coic}{kla} (1^\secp) \ra 1] - \frac{1}{2} }\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(58)

Definition 4.12 (Strong CoIC-KLA Security).

We say that a PKE scheme
\mathsf{PKE}
with the message space
\mathcal{X}
is
\epsilon
-strong CoIC-KLA secure, if it satisfies the following requirement, formalized from the experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{PKE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{s}\mbox{-}\mathsf{coic}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda},\epsilon)
between an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
and a challenger
\mathpzc{C}
:

1.

\mathpzc{C}
runs
(\mathsf{ek}_{0},\mathsf{dk}_{0})\leftarrow\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})
and
(\mathsf{ek}_{1},\mathsf{dk}_{1})\leftarrow\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})
, and generates
\mathpzc{dk}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{0}\ket{\dk_0}+\ket{1}\ket{\dk_1})
.
\mathpzc{C}
sends
\mathsf{ek}_{0}
,
\mathsf{ek}_{1}
, and
\mathpzc{dk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}
sends
(\mathsf{m}_{0}^{*},\mathsf{m}_{1}^{*})\in\mathcal{X}^{2}
and a quantum circuit
\mathpzc{D}=(\mathpzc{q},\bm{U})
, where
\mathpzc{D}
is a quantum program with classical inputs and one-bit outputs and
\bm{U}
is a compact classical description of
\{\bm{U}_{\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}}\}_{\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}}
to
\mathpzc{C}
.

3.

Let
D
be the following distribution.

D
:

Generate
a,b\leftarrow\{0,1\}
and
\mathsf{ct}_{0}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek}_{0},\mathsf{m}_{a})
and
\mathsf{ct}_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek}_{1},\mathsf{m}_{a\oplus b})
. Output
(b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1})
.

We also let
\mathcal{P}=(\bm{P}_{b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}},\bm{Q}_{b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}})_{b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}}
be a collection of binary outcome projective measurements, where

\displaystyle\bm{P}_{b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}}=\bm{U}_{\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}}^{\dagger}(\ket{b}\bra{b}\otimes\bm{I})\bm{U}_{\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}}\textrm{~~~~and~~~~}\bm{Q}_{b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}}=\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}}.

(59)

Moreover, we let
\mathcal{M}_{D}=(\bm{P}_{D},\bm{Q}_{D})
be binary outcome POVMs, where

\displaystyle\bm{P}_{D}=\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\calR}}\bm{P}_{D(r)}\textrm{~~~~and~~~~}\bm{Q}_{D}=\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{D}.

(60)

Note that
\mathcal{R}
is the random coin space of
D
and
\bm{P}_{D(r)}=\bm{P}_{b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}}
, where
(b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1})\leftarrow D(r)
.
13
13

13

The random coin
r
for
D
consists of random bits
a,b
and encryption coins of two ciphertexts.

\mathpzc{C}
applies the measurement
\mathsf{ProjImp}(\mathcal{M}_{D})
to
\mathpzc{q}
, and obtain a value
p
.

\mathpzc{C}
outputs
1
if
p\geq\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon
and
0
otherwise.

For any QPT
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{PKE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{s}\mbox{-}\mathsf{coic}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{SKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{s}\mbox{-}\mathsf{coic}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda},\epsilon)\rightarrow 1]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(61)

Theorem 4.13.

If
\mathsf{PKE}
is
\epsilon
-strong CoIC-KLA secure for any inverse polynomial
\epsilon
, then
\mathsf{PKE}
is CoIC-KLA secure.

Proof.

Assume there exists
\mathpzc{A}
that breaks CoIC-KLA security of
\mathsf{PKE}
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
\mathpzc{A}
correctly guesses the bit
b
with probability
\frac{1}{2}+\gamma
for some inverse polynomial
\gamma
.
Then, consider the following experiment using
\mathpzc{A}
.

1.

Execute
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{PKE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{coic}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda})
until the point
\mathpzc{A}
outputs
(\mathsf{m}_{0}^{*},\mathsf{m}_{1}^{*})
.

2.

Construct a quantum program with classical inputs and outputs
\mathpzc{D}=(\mathpzc{q},\bm{U})
, where
\mathpzc{q}
is the inner quantum state of
\mathpzc{A}
and
\bm{U}
is a compact description of
\{\bm{U}_{\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}}\}_{\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}}
and
\bm{U}_{\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}}
is a unitary that performs the rest of
\mathpzc{A}
’s computations on input
(\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1})
.

3.

Obtain
p
by applying
\mathsf{ProjImp}(\mathcal{M}_{D})
to
\mathpzc{q}
, where the measurement
\mathcal{M}_{D}
and the distribution
D
are defined in
Definition

4.12
.

Then, from the definition of
\mathsf{ProjImp}
and the fact that
\mathpzc{A}
’s advantage is
\frac{1}{2}+\gamma
, we have
E[p]=\frac{1}{2}+\gamma
.
By the averaging argument, we obtain
\Pr[p\geq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}]\geq\frac{\gamma}{2}
.
Consider the following adversary
\mathpzc{B}
that attacks
\frac{\gamma}{2}
-strong CoIC-KLA security of
\mathsf{PKE}
.

1.

Given,
\mathsf{ek}_{0}
,
\mathsf{ek}_{1}
, and
\mathpzc{dk}
,
\mathpzc{B}
executes
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{PKE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{coic}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda})
until the point
\mathpzc{A}
outputs
(\mathsf{m}_{0}^{*},\mathsf{m}_{1}^{*})
. When
\mathpzc{A}
makes a query to
\mathcal{O}
,
\mathpzc{B}
returns a random bit.

2.

\mathpzc{B}
constructs a quantum program with classical inputs and outputs
\mathpzc{D}=(\mathpzc{q},\bm{U})
, where
\mathpzc{q}
is the inner quantum state of
\mathpzc{A}
,
\bm{U}
is a compact description of
\{\bm{U}_{\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}}\}_{\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}}
, and
\bm{U}_{\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}}
is a unitary that performs the rest of
\mathpzc{A}
’s computations on input
(\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1})
.

\mathpzc{B}
outputs
(\mathsf{m}_{0}^{*},\mathsf{m}_{1}^{*})
and
\mathpzc{D}
.

\mathpzc{B}
correctly answers to
\mathpzc{A}
’s query to
\mathcal{O}
and correctly simulates
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{PKE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{coic}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}
for
\mathpzc{A}
with probability
\frac{1}{2}
.
14
14

14

\mathpzc{B}
does not apply the verification procedure to the queried state differently from
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{PKE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{coic}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}
. This is not a problem since from the view of
\mathpzc{A}
, the experiment simulated by
\mathpzc{B}
is the same as the experiment where the verification process is applied to the queried state, but the result is ignored and a random bit is returned.

Moreover, from the above discussion, under the condition that
\mathpzc{B}
correctly answers to
\mathpzc{A}
’s query to
\mathcal{O}
,
\mathpzc{B}
wins with probability
\frac{\gamma}{2}
.
Overall,
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{PKE},\mathpzc{B}}^{\mathsf{s}\mbox{-}\mathsf{coic}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(\lambda)\geq\frac{\gamma}{4}
, which contradicts
\frac{\gamma}{2}
-strong CoIC-KLA security of
\mathsf{PKE}
.
This completes the proof.
∎

4.3
Strong
CoIC-KLA
Secure PKE from CPFE

We construct a strong
CoIC-KLA
secure PKE
\mathsf{PKE}=(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
using a CPFE scheme
\mathsf{CPFE}=(\mathsf{CPFE}.\mathsf{Setup},\allowbreak\mathsf{CPFE}.\mathsf{KG},\mathsf{CPFE}.\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{CPFE}.\mathsf{Dec})
as a building block.

\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
:

•

Generate
(\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{CPFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

•

Generate
x\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and
\mathsf{sk}_{x}\leftarrow\mathsf{CPFE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{MSK},x)
.

•

Output
\mathsf{ek}\coloneqq\mathsf{MPK}
and
\mathsf{dk}:=\mathsf{sk}_{x}
.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{m})
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{ek}=\mathsf{MPK}
.

•

Let
C[\mathsf{m}]
be a constant circuit that outputs
\mathsf{m}
on any input.
C
is padded so that it has the same size as the circuit
C^{*}
appeared in the security proof.

•

Output
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{CPFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},C[\mathsf{m}])
.

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{dk},\mathsf{ct})
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{dk}=\mathsf{sk}_{x}
.

•

Output
\mathsf{m}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{CPFE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{x},\mathsf{ct})
.

The decryption correctness of
\mathsf{PKE}
follows from that of
\mathsf{CPFE}
.
We also have the following theorems.

Theorem 4.14.

If
\mathsf{CPFE}
is a
1
-bounded secure CPFE scheme, then
\mathsf{PKE}
is a
\epsilon
-strong
CoIC-KLA
secure PKE scheme for any inverse polynomial
\epsilon
.

Proof.

We show that if there exists a QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
that breaks
\epsilon
-strong
CoIC-KLA
security for some inverse polynomial
\epsilon
, then we can construct a QPT adversary
\mathpzc{B}
that contradicts the following lemma.

Lemma 4.15.

Consider the following experiment
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathsf{CPFE},\mathpzc{B}}^{\mathsf{BZ}}(1^{\lambda})
between an adversary
\mathpzc{B}
and a challenger
\mathpzc{C}
.

1.

\mathpzc{C}
generates
(\mathsf{MPK}_{0},\mathsf{MSK}_{0})\leftarrow\mathsf{CPFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
,
(\mathsf{MPK}_{1},\mathsf{MSK}_{1})\leftarrow\mathsf{CPFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
,
x_{0},x_{1}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
,
\mathsf{sk}_{x_{0}}\leftarrow\mathsf{CPFE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{MSK}_{0},x_{0})
, and
\mathsf{sk}_{x_{1}}\leftarrow\mathsf{CPFE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{MSK}_{1},x_{1})
.
\mathpzc{C}
gives
\mathsf{MPK}_{0}
,
\mathsf{MPK}_{1}
, and
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{0}\ket{\sk_{x_0}}+\ket{1}\ket{\sk_{x_1}})
to
\mathpzc{B}
.

2.

\mathpzc{B}
outputs
x_{0}^{\prime}
and
x_{1}^{\prime}
.
\mathpzc{C}
outputs
1
if
x_{0}^{\prime}=x_{0}
and
x_{1}^{\prime}=x_{1}
and
0
otherwise.

Then, for any QPT adversary
\mathpzc{B}
, we have
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{CPFE},\mathpzc{B}}^{\mathsf{BZ}}(1^{\lambda})=\Pr[\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathsf{CPFE},\mathpzc{B}}^{\mathsf{BZ}}(1^{\lambda})=1]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

Proof.

This lemma directly follows from
Lemma

2.21
.
∎

Let
\epsilon
be some inverse polynomial. Assume there exits a QPT
\mathpzc{A}
such that
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{PKE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{s}\mbox{-}\mathsf{coic}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(\lambda,\epsilon)=\gamma
for some inverse polynomial
\gamma
.
We construct the following adversary
\mathpzc{B}
.

1.

Given
\mathsf{MPK}_{0}
,
\mathsf{MPK}_{1}
, and
\mathpzc{dk}
,
\mathpzc{B}
sets
\mathsf{ek}_{0}:=\mathsf{MPK}_{0}
and
\mathsf{ek}_{1}:=\mathsf{MPK}_{1}
.
\mathpzc{B}
sends
\mathsf{ek}_{0}
,
\mathsf{ek}_{1}
, and
\mathpzc{dk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

When
\mathpzc{A}
outputs
(\mathsf{m}_{0}^{*},\mathsf{m}_{1}^{*})
and
\mathpzc{D}=(\mathpzc{q},\bm{U})
,
\mathpzc{B}
outputs
(x_{0}^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Extract}(\mathsf{MPK}_{0},\mathsf{MPK}_{1},\mathsf{m}_{0}^{*},\mathsf{m}_{1}^{*},\mathpzc{D},\epsilon)
, where
\mathpzc{Extract}
is described below.

\mathpzc{Extract}(\mathsf{MPK}_{0},\mathsf{MPK}_{1},\mathsf{m}_{0}^{*},\mathsf{m}_{1}^{*},\mathpzc{D},\epsilon)
:

•

Let
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon/8\lambda
and
\delta^{\prime}=2^{-\lambda}
.

•

Parse
(\mathpzc{q},\bm{U})\leftarrow\mathpzc{D}
.

•

Let
\mathcal{P}
be defined in the same way as that in
Definition

4.12
and
D_{0,i}
and
D_{1,i}
be the following distributions for every
i\in[\lambda]
.

D_{0,i}
:

Generate
a,b\leftarrow\{0,1\}
. Generate
\mathsf{ct}_{0}\leftarrow\mathsf{CPFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK}_{0},C^{*}[a,b,\mathsf{m}_{0},\mathsf{m}_{1},i])
, where
C^{*}[a,b,\mathsf{m}_{0},\mathsf{m}_{1},i]
is a circuit that takes
x
as input and outputs
\mathsf{m}_{a\oplus b\oplus x[i]}
. Generate
\mathsf{ct}_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{CPFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK}_{1},C[\mathsf{m}_{a}])
. Output
(b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1})
.

D_{1,i}
:

Generate
a,b\leftarrow\{0,1\}
. Generate
\mathsf{ct}_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{CPFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK}_{0},C^{*}[a,b,\mathsf{m}_{0},\mathsf{m}_{1},i])
, where
C^{*}[a,b,\mathsf{m}_{0},\mathsf{m}_{1},i]
is a circuit that takes
x
as input and outputs
\mathsf{m}_{a\oplus b\oplus x[i]}
. Generate
\mathsf{ct}_{0}\leftarrow\mathsf{CPFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK}_{1},C[\mathsf{m}_{a}])
. Output
(b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1})
.

•

Let
\mathsf{D}
be the distribution defined in the same way as that in
Definition

4.12
. Compute
\tilde{p}_{0}\leftarrow\mathpzc{API}_{\mathcal{P},D}^{\epsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}}(\mathpzc{q})
. If
\tilde{p}_{0}<\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon-4\epsilon^{\prime}
, return
\bot
. Otherwise, let
\mathpzc{q}_{0,0}
be the post-measurement state, go to the next step.

•

For all
i\in[\lambda]
, do the following.

1.

Compute
\tilde{p}_{0,i}\leftarrow\mathpzc{API}_{\mathcal{P},D_{0,i}}^{\epsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}}(\mathpzc{q}_{0,i-1})
. Let
\mathpzc{q}_{0,i}
be the post-measurement state.

2.

If
\tilde{p}_{0,i}>\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon-4(i+1)\epsilon^{\prime}
, set
x_{0}^{\prime}[i]=0
. If
\tilde{p}_{0,i}<\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon+4(i+1)\epsilon^{\prime}
, set
x_{0}^{\prime}[i]=1
. Otherwise, exit the loop and output
\bot
.

•

Let
\mathpzc{q}_{1,0}
be
\mathpzc{q}_{0,\lambda}
. For all
i\in[\lambda]
, do the following.

1.

Compute
\tilde{p}_{1,i}\leftarrow\mathpzc{API}_{\mathcal{P},D_{1,i}}^{\epsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}}(\mathpzc{q}_{1,i-1})
. Let
\mathpzc{q}_{1,i}
be the post-measurement state.

2.

If
\tilde{p}_{1,i}>\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon-4(\lambda+i+1)\epsilon^{\prime}
, set
x_{1}^{\prime}[i]=0
. If
\tilde{p}_{1,i}<\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon+4(\lambda+i+1)\epsilon^{\prime}
, set
x_{1}^{\prime}[i]=1
. Otherwise, exit the loop and output
\bot
.

•

Output
x_{0}^{\prime}=x_{0}^{\prime}[1]\|\cdots\|x_{0}^{\prime}[\lambda]
and
x_{1}^{\prime}=x_{1}^{\prime}[1]\|\cdots\|x_{1}^{\prime}[\lambda]
.

We will estimate
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{CPFE},\mathpzc{B}}^{\mathsf{BZ}}(1^{\lambda})
.
We define the events
\mathsf{BadDec}
, and
\mathsf{BadExt}_{0,i}
and
\mathsf{BadExt}_{1,i}
for every
i\in[\lambda]
.

\mathsf{BadDec}
:

When
\mathpzc{B}
runs
\mathpzc{Extract}(\mathsf{MPK}_{0},\mathsf{MPK}_{1},\mathsf{m}_{0}^{*},\mathsf{m}_{1}^{*},\mathpzc{D},\epsilon)
,
\tilde{p}_{0}<\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon-4\epsilon^{\prime}
holds.

\mathsf{BadExt}_{0,i}
:

When
\mathpzc{B}
runs
\mathpzc{Extract}(\mathsf{MPK}_{0},\mathsf{MPK}_{1},\mathsf{m}_{0}^{*},\mathsf{m}_{1}^{*},\mathpzc{D},\epsilon)
, the following conditions hold.

•

\tilde{p}_{0}\geq\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon-4\epsilon^{\prime}
holds.

•

x_{0}^{\prime}[j]=x_{0}[j]
holds for every
j\in[i-1]
.

•

x_{0}^{\prime}[i]\neq x_{0}[i]
holds.

\mathsf{BadExt}_{1,i}
:

When
\mathpzc{B}
runs
\mathpzc{Extract}(\mathsf{MPK}_{0},\mathsf{MPK}_{1},\mathsf{m}_{0}^{*},\mathsf{m}_{1}^{*},\mathpzc{D},\epsilon)
, the following conditions hold.

•

\tilde{p}_{0}\geq\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon-4\epsilon^{\prime}
holds.

•

x_{0}^{\prime}[j]=x_{0}[j]
holds for every
j\in[\lambda]
.

•

x_{1}^{\prime}[j]=x_{1}[j]
holds for every
j\in[i-1]
.

•

x_{1}^{\prime}[i]\neq x_{1}[i]
holds.

From the assumption that
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{PKE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{s}\mbox{-}\mathsf{coic}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(\lambda)=\gamma
, for
\tilde{p}_{0}
computed in
\mathpzc{Extract}
,
\tilde{p}_{0}\geq\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon-\epsilon^{\prime}
holds with probability
\gamma-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
due to the first item of
Theorem

4.5
.
This means that
\Pr[\mathsf{BadDec}]\leq 1-\gamma+{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{CPFE},\mathpzc{B}}^{\mathsf{BZ}}(1^{\lambda})

\displaystyle\geq 1-\left(\Pr[\mathsf{BadDec}]+\sum_{i\in[\lambda]}\Pr[\mathsf{BadExt}_{0,i}]+\sum_{i\in[\lambda]}\Pr[\mathsf{BadExt}_{1,i}]\right)

(62)

\displaystyle\geq\gamma-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)-\left(\sum_{i\in[\lambda]}\Pr[\mathsf{BadExt}_{0,i}]+\sum_{i\in[\lambda]}\Pr[\mathsf{BadExt}_{1,i}]\right).

(63)

Estimation of
\Pr[\mathsf{BadExt}_{0,i}]
for every
i\in[\lambda]
.

We first estimate
\Pr[\mathsf{BadExt}_{0,1}]
.
We first consider the case of
x_{0}[1]=0
.
From the first item of the event, we have
\tilde{p}_{0}>\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon-4\epsilon^{\prime}
.
Let
\tilde{p}^{\prime}_{0}\leftarrow\mathpzc{API}_{\mathcal{P},D}^{\epsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}}(\mathpzc{q}_{0,0})
.
From the almost-projective property of
\mathpzc{API}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr[\tilde{p}^{\prime}_{0}>\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon-4\epsilon^{\prime}-\epsilon^{\prime}]\geq 1-\delta^{\prime}.

(64)

Lemma 4.16.

When
x_{0}[1]=0
,
D_{0,1}
is computationally indistinguishable from
D
.

Proof.

The difference between
D_{0,1}
and
D
is that
\mathsf{ct}_{0}
is generated as
\mathsf{ct}_{0}\leftarrow\mathsf{CPFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK}_{0},C^{*}[a,b,\mathsf{m}_{0},\mathsf{m}_{1},1])
in
D_{0,1}
and it is generated as
\mathsf{ct}_{0}\leftarrow\mathsf{CPFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK}_{0},C[\mathsf{m}_{a\oplus b}])
in
D
.
From the condition that
x_{0}[1]=0
, we have
C^{*}[a,b,\mathsf{m}_{0},\mathsf{m}_{1},1](x_{0})=C[\mathsf{m}_{a\oplus b}](x_{0})=\mathsf{m}_{a\oplus b}
.
Thus, from the
1
-bounded security of
\mathsf{CPFE}
,
D_{0,1}
and
D
are computationally indistinguishable when
x_{0}[1]=0
.
∎

Thus, from
Theorem

4.6
and
Lemma

4.16
, we have

\displaystyle 1-\delta^{\prime}\leq\Pr[\tilde{p}^{\prime}_{0}>\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon-5\epsilon^{\prime}]\leq\Pr[\tilde{p}_{0,1}>\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon-8\epsilon^{\prime}]+{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(65)

This means that
\Pr[\mathsf{BadExt}_{0,1}]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
when
x_{0}[1]=0
.
We next consider the case of
x_{0}[1]=1
.
We define the following distribution
D^{\mathtt{rev}}
.

D^{\mathtt{rev}}
:

Generate
(b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1})\leftarrow D
. Output
(1\oplus b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1})
.

That is, the first bit of the output is flipped from
D
.
Then, for any random coin
r
, we have
(\bm{P}_{D^{\mathtt{rev}}(r)},\bm{Q}_{D^{\mathtt{rev}}(r)})=(\bm{Q}_{D(r)},\bm{P}_{D(r)})
.
This is because we have
\bm{Q}_{b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}}=\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}}=\bm{P}_{1\oplus b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}}
for any tuple
(b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1})
.
Therefore,
\mathpzc{API}_{\mathcal{P},D^{\mathtt{rev}}}^{\epsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}}
is exactly the same process as
\mathpzc{API}_{\mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{rev}},D}^{\epsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}}
, where
\mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{rev}}=(\bm{Q}_{b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}},\bm{P}_{b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}})_{b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}}
.
Let
\tilde{p}^{\prime}_{0}\leftarrow\mathpzc{API}_{\mathcal{P},D^{\mathtt{rev}}}^{\epsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}}(\mathpzc{q}_{0,0})
.
From, the reverse-almost-projective property of
\mathpzc{API}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr[\tilde{p}^{\prime}_{0}<\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon+4\epsilon^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime}]\geq 1-\delta^{\prime}.

(66)

Lemma 4.17.

When
x_{0}[1]=1
,
D_{0,1}
is computationally indistinguishable from
D^{\mathtt{rev}}
.

Proof.

We see that
D^{\mathtt{rev}}
is identical to the following distribution.

•

Generate
a,b\leftarrow\{0,1\}
and
\mathsf{ct}_{0}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek}_{0},\mathsf{m}_{a})
and
\mathsf{ct}_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{m}_{a\oplus 1\oplus b})
. Output
(b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1})
.

Then, the difference between
D_{0,1}
and
D^{\mathtt{rev}}
is that
\mathsf{ct}_{0}
is generated as
\mathsf{ct}_{0}\leftarrow\mathsf{CPFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK}_{0},C^{*}[a,b,\mathsf{m}_{0},\mathsf{m}_{1},1])
in
D_{0,1}
and it is generated as
\mathsf{ct}_{0}\leftarrow\mathsf{CPFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK}_{0},C[\mathsf{m}_{a\oplus 1\oplus b}])
in
D^{\mathtt{rev}}
.
From the condition that
x_{0}[1]=1
, we have
C^{*}[a,b,\mathsf{m}_{0},\mathsf{m}_{1},1](x_{0})=C[\mathsf{m}_{a\oplus 1\oplus b}](x_{0})=\mathsf{m}_{a\oplus 1\oplus b}
.
Thus, from the
1
-bounded security of
\mathsf{CPFE}
,
D_{0,1}
and
D^{\mathtt{rev}}
are computationally indistinguishable when
x_{0}[1]=1
.
∎

Thus, from
Theorem

4.6
and
Lemma

4.17
, we have

\displaystyle 1-\delta^{\prime}\leq\Pr[\tilde{p}^{\prime}_{0}<\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon+5\epsilon^{\prime}]\leq\Pr[\tilde{p}_{0,1}<\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon+8\epsilon^{\prime}]+{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(67)

This means that
\Pr[\mathsf{BadExt}_{0,1}]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
when
x_{0}[1]=1
.

Overall,
\Pr[\mathsf{BadExt}_{0,1}]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
regardless of the value of
x_{0}
.
We can similarly show that
\Pr[\mathsf{BadExt}_{0,i}]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
for
i\in\{2,\cdots,\lambda\}
using the fact that
D_{0,i}
is computationally indistinguishable from
D
if
x_{0}[i]=0
and it is computationally indistinguishable from
D^{\mathtt{rev}}
if
x_{0}[i]=1
.
We omit the details.

Estimation of
\Pr[\mathsf{BadExt}_{1,i}]
for every
i\in[\lambda]
.

We estimate
\Pr[\mathsf{BadExt}_{1,1}]
.
We first consider the case of
x_{0}[\lambda]=0
and
x_{1}[1]=0
.
From the second item of the event, we have
\tilde{p}_{0,\lambda}>\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon-4(\lambda+1)\epsilon^{\prime}
.
Let
\tilde{p}^{\prime}_{0,\lambda}\leftarrow\mathpzc{API}_{\mathcal{P},D_{0,\lambda}}^{\epsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}}(\mathpzc{q}_{0,\lambda})
.
From, the almost-projective property of
\mathpzc{API}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr[\tilde{p}^{\prime}_{0,\lambda}>\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon-4(\lambda+1)\epsilon^{\prime}-\epsilon^{\prime}]\geq 1-\delta^{\prime}.

(68)

Lemma 4.18.

When
x_{0}[\lambda]=x_{1}[1]=0
,
D_{0,\lambda}
and
D_{1,1}
are computationally indistinguishable.

Proof.

We can show that
D_{0,\lambda}
is computationally indistinguishable from
D
when
x_{0}[\lambda]=0
similarly to
Lemma

4.16
.
We see that
D
is identical to the following distribution.

•

Generate
a,b\leftarrow\{0,1\}
and
\mathsf{ct}_{0}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek}_{0},\mathsf{m}_{a\oplus b})
and
\mathsf{ct}_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{m}_{a})
. Output
(b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1})
.

Then, the difference between
D_{1,1}
and
D
is that
\mathsf{ct}_{1}
is generated as
\mathsf{ct}_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{CPFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK}_{1},C^{*}[a,b,\mathsf{m}_{0},\mathsf{m}_{1},1])
in
D_{1,1}
and it is generated as
\mathsf{ct}_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{CPFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK}_{1},C[\mathsf{m}_{a\oplus b}])
in
D
.
From the condition that
x_{1}[1]=0
, we have
C^{*}[a,b,\mathsf{m}_{0},\mathsf{m}_{1},1](x_{1})=C[\mathsf{m}_{a\oplus b}](x_{1})=\mathsf{m}_{a\oplus b}
.
Thus, from the
1
-bounded security of
\mathsf{CPFE}
,
D_{1,1}
and
D
are computationally indistinguishable when
x_{0}[1]=0
.
This means that
D_{0,\lambda}
and
D_{1,1}
are computationally indistinguishable when
x_{0}[\lambda]=x_{1}[1]=0
.
∎

Thus, from
Theorem

4.6
and
Lemma

4.18
, we have

\displaystyle 1-\delta^{\prime}\leq\Pr[\tilde{p}^{\prime}_{0,\lambda}>\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon-(4\lambda+5)\epsilon^{\prime}]\leq\Pr[\tilde{p}_{1,1}>\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon-4(\lambda+2)\epsilon^{\prime}]+{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(69)

This means that
\Pr[\mathsf{BadExt}_{1,1}]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
when
x_{0}[\lambda]=0
and
x_{1}[1]=0
.
We next consider the case of
x_{0}[\lambda]=0
and
x_{1}[1]=1
.
We define the following distribution
D^{\mathtt{rev}}_{0,\lambda}
.

D^{\mathtt{rev}}_{0,\lambda}
:

Generate
(b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1})\leftarrow D_{0,\lambda}
. Output
(1\oplus b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1})
.

That is, the first bit of the output is flipped from
D_{0,\lambda}
.
Then, for any random coin
r
, we have
(\bm{P}_{D^{\mathtt{rev}}_{0,\lambda}(r)},\bm{Q}_{D^{\mathtt{rev}}_{0,\lambda}(r)})=(\bm{Q}_{D_{0,\lambda}(r)},\bm{P}_{D_{0,\lambda}(r)})
.
(Again, this is because we have
\bm{Q}_{b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}}=\bm{I}-\bm{P}_{b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}}=\bm{P}_{1\oplus b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}}
for any tuple
(b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1})
.)
Therefore,
\mathpzc{API}_{\mathcal{P},D^{\mathtt{rev}}_{0,\lambda}}^{\epsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}}
is exactly the same process as
\mathpzc{API}_{\mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{rev}},D_{0,\lambda}}^{\epsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}}
, where
\mathcal{P}^{\mathtt{rev}}=(\bm{Q}_{b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}},\bm{P}_{b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}})_{b,\mathsf{ct}_{0},\mathsf{ct}_{1}}
.
Let
\tilde{p}^{\prime}_{0,\lambda}\leftarrow\mathpzc{API}_{\mathcal{P},D^{\mathtt{rev}}_{0,\lambda}}^{\epsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}}(\mathpzc{q}_{0,\lambda})
.
From, the reverse-almost-projective property of
\mathpzc{API}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr[\tilde{p}^{\prime}_{0,\lambda}<\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon+4(\lambda+1)\epsilon^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime}]\geq 1-\delta^{\prime}.

(70)

Lemma 4.19.

When
x_{0}[\lambda]=0
and
x_{1}[1]=1
,
D^{\mathtt{rev}}_{0,\lambda}
and
D_{1,1}
are computationally indistinguishable.

Proof.

We can show that both
D^{\mathtt{rev}}_{0,\lambda}
and
D_{1,1}
are computationally indistinguishable from
D^{\mathtt{rev}}
when
x_{0}[\lambda]=0
and
x_{1}[1]=1
.
The proof is similarly to those for
Lemmas

4.16
,
4.17
and
4.18
, thus we omit the details.
∎

Thus, from
Theorem

4.6
and
Lemma

4.19
, we have

\displaystyle 1-\delta^{\prime}\leq\Pr[\tilde{p}^{\prime}_{0,\lambda}<\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon+(4\lambda+5)\epsilon^{\prime}]\leq\Pr[\tilde{p}_{1,1}<\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon+4(\lambda+2)\epsilon^{\prime}]+{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(71)

This means that
\Pr[\mathsf{BadExt}_{1,1}]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
when
x_{0}[\lambda]=0
and
x_{1}[1]=1
.

Similarly, we can show that
\Pr[\mathsf{BadExt}_{1,1}]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
holds when
(x_{0}[\lambda],x_{1}[1])=(1,0)
and
(x_{0}[\lambda],x_{1}[1])=(1,1)
.
Moreover, we can show that
\Pr[\mathsf{BadExt}_{1,i}]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
holds for
i\in\{2,\cdots,\lambda\}
.

From the above discussion, we have
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{CPFE},\mathpzc{B}}^{\mathsf{BZ}}(1^{\lambda})\geq\gamma-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
for some inverse polynomial
\gamma
, which contradicts
Lemma

4.15
.
This completes the proof of
Theorem

4.14
.
∎

5
Construction of PKE with Secure Key Leasing

In this section, we prove the following theorem:

Theorem 5.1.

If there is an IND-CPA secure PKE scheme, then there is an IND-KLA secure PKE-SKL scheme.

By
Theorem

3.9
, it suffices to construct 1-query OW-KLA secure PKE-SKL scheme. In the rest of this section, we construct such a scheme. To build our scheme, we rely on a PKE scheme satisfying
CoIC-KLA
security, which is constructed from any IND-CPA secure PKE scheme in Section
4
.

Let
\mathsf{cPKE}=(\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{KG},\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{Dec})
be a PKE scheme satisfying
CoIC-KLA
security with message space
\{0,1\}^{\ell}
where
\ell=\omega(\log\lambda)
. We note that
CoIC-KLA
security implies OW-CPA security when
\ell=\omega(\log\lambda)
. (See
Appendix

B
for the proof.) Then, we construct a PKE-SKL scheme
(\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{KG},\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Dec},\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Vrfy})
with message space
\{0,1\}^{\lambda\ell}
as follows.

\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})
:

•

Generate
(\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i,b},\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{dk}_{i,b})\leftarrow\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})
for
i\in[\lambda]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
.

•

Output an encryption key

\mathsf{ek}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}},

(72)

a decryption key

\mathpzc{dk}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{i\in[\lambda]}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\ket{0}\ket{\cPKE.\dk_{i,0}}+\ket{1}\ket{\cPKE.\dk_{i,1}}\right),

(73)

and a verification key

\mathsf{vk}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{dk}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}.

(74)

For convenience, we write
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{DK}_{i}}}
to mean the registers of
\mathpzc{dk}
that contains
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\ket{0}\ket{\cPKE.\dk_{i,0}}+\ket{1}\ket{\cPKE.\dk_{i,1}}\right)
for
i\in[\lambda]
.

\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{m})
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{ek}=\{\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
and
\mathsf{m}=\mathsf{m}_{1}\|\ldots\|\mathsf{m}_{\lambda}
where
\mathsf{m}_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
for each
i\in[\lambda]
.

•

Generate

\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,b}\leftarrow\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i,b},\mathsf{m}_{i})
for
i\in[\lambda]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
.

•

Output
\mathsf{ct}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
.

\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathpzc{dk},\mathsf{ct})
:

•

Parse
\mathpzc{dk}=\bigotimes_{i\in[\lambda]}\mathpzc{dk}_{i}
and
\mathsf{ct}=\{\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
.

•

Let
U_{\mathrm{dec}}
be a unitary such that for all
\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{dk}^{\prime}
,
\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{\prime}_{0}
, and
\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{\prime}_{1}
:

\displaystyle\ket{b}\ket{\cPKE.\dk'}\ket{\cPKE.\ct'_0,\cPKE.\ct'_1}\ket{0}

(75)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{U_{\mathrm{dec}}}

\displaystyle\ket{b}\ket{\cPKE.\dk'}\ket{\cPKE.\ct'_0,\cPKE.\ct'_1}\ket{\cPKE.\Dec(\cPKE.\dk',\cPKE.\ct'_b)}

(76)

Note that such a unitary can be computed in quantum polynomial-time since we assume that
\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{Dec}
is a deterministic classical polynomial-time algorithm.

•

For all
i\in[\lambda]
,
generate

U_{\mathrm{dec}}\left(\mathpzc{dk}_{i}\otimes\ket{\cPKE.\ct_{i,0},\cPKE.\ct_{i,1}}\bra{\cPKE.\ct_{i,0},\cPKE.\ct_{i,1}}\otimes\ket{0}\bra{0}\right)U_{\mathrm{dec}}^{\dagger},

(77)

measure the rightmost register, and let
\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{i}
be the measurement outcome.

•

Output
\mathsf{m}^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{1}\|\ldots\|\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{\lambda}
.

\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}})
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{vk}=\{\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{dk}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
.

•

Apply a binary-outcome measurement
(\bm{I}-\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}^{\mathsf{vk}},\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}^{\mathsf{vk}})
on
\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}
where
\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}^{\mathsf{vk}}
is the projection onto the right decryption key, i.e.,

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}^{\mathsf{vk}}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{i\in[\lambda]}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\ket{0}\ket{\cPKE.\dk_{i,0}}+\ket{1}\ket{\cPKE.\dk_{i,1}}\right)\right)\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\bra{0}\bra{\cPKE.\dk_{i,0}}+\bra{1}\bra{\cPKE.\dk_{i,1}}\right)\right).

(78)

If the measurement outcome is
1
(indicating that the state was projected onto
\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}^{\mathsf{vk}}
), output
\top
and otherwise output
\bot
.

The correctness of
\mathsf{SKL}
easily follows from that of

\mathsf{cPKE}
.
Below, we show that
\mathsf{SKL}
is 1-query OW-KLA secure.

Theorem 5.2.

If
\mathsf{cPKE}
is
CoIC-KLA
secure, then
\mathsf{SKL}
is 1-query OW-KLA secure.

Proof.

Let
\mathpzc{A}
be a QPT adversary against 1-query OW-KLA security of
\mathsf{SKL}
.
By
Remark

3.5
,
we assume that
\mathpzc{A}
makes the verification query before receiving the challenge ciphertext without loss of generality.
We consider the following sequence of hybrids.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{SKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ow}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda})
. More specifically, it works as follows.

1.

The challenger generates
(\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i,b},\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{dk}_{i,b})\leftarrow\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})
for
i\in[\lambda]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
,
sets

\mathsf{ek}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
and

\mathpzc{dk}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{i\in[\lambda]}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\ket{0}\ket{\cPKE.\dk_{i,0}}+\ket{1}\ket{\cPKE.\dk_{i,1}}\right)
,
and sends
\mathsf{ek}
and
\mathpzc{dk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}
queries
\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}

to the verification oracle.
The challenger applies a binary-outcome measurement
(\bm{I}-\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}^{\mathsf{vk}},\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}^{\mathsf{vk}})
on
\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}
where
\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}^{\mathsf{vk}}
is the projection defined in the description of
\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}
. If the measurement outcome is
0
(indicating that the state was projected onto
\bm{I}-\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}^{\mathsf{vk}}
), the challenger outputs
0
as the final outcome of this experiment.
15
15

15

In the description of the OW-KLA experiment in
Definition

3.7
, the oracle returns
\bot
even if the decryption key does not pass the verification. However, in the 1-query setting, if the first (and only) query is rejected, the experiment finally outputs
0
. Thus, we terminate the experiment at this point when the query is rejected.

Otherwise, the challenger returns
\top
to
\mathpzc{A}
as the response from the oracle.

3.

The challenger chooses
\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
for
i\in[\lambda]
,
generates

\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{i,b}\leftarrow\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i,b},\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i})
for
i\in[\lambda]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
, and sends
\mathsf{ct}^{*}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
16
16

16

Since
\mathpzc{A}
makes only one verification query, we can assume that
\mathpzc{A}
requests the challenge ciphertext immediately after finishing the first verification query without loss of generality.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs
\mathsf{m}^{\prime}=\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{1}\|\ldots\|\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{\lambda}
. The challenger outputs
1
if
\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{i}=\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i}
for all
i\in[\lambda]
and otherwise
0
as the final outcome of this experiment.

Note that we have
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]=\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{SKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ow}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda})
. Our goal is to prove
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
:

This is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
except for the following modifications:

•

The challenger chooses
\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,b}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
for
i\in[\lambda]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
(instead of choosing
\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
for
i\in[\lambda]
) and
a_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
for
i\in[\lambda]
.

•

\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{i,b}
is generated as

\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{i,b}\leftarrow\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i,b},\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,a_{i}})
for
i\in[\lambda]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
. We emphasize that
\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\underline{a_{i}}}
is encrypted for both cases of
b=0
and
b=1
and
\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,a_{i}\oplus 1}
is not used in this step.

•

In Step
4
, the challenger outputs
1
if
\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{i}\in\{\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,0},\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,1}\}
for all
i\in[\lambda]
.

By considering
\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,a_{i}}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
as
\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
, these hybrids are identical from the view of
\mathpzc{A}
except that the winning condition (i.e., the condition that the challenger returns
1
) is just relaxed in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
. Therefore, we trivially have
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]\leq\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
:

This is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
except that
\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{i,b}
is generated as

\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{i,b}\leftarrow\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i,b},\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,a_{i}\oplus b})
for
i\in[\lambda]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
. We remark that the way of generating
\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{i,1}
is changed but that of
\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{i,0}
is unchanged (because
a_{i}\oplus 0=a_{i}
).

By the CoIC-KLA security of
\mathsf{cPKE}
and a standard hybrid argument, we have

\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hybi{1}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{2}=1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
. See
Lemma

5.3
for the detail.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
:

This is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
except that
the challenger quits choosing
a_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
for
i\in[\lambda]
and

\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{i,b}
is generated as

\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{i,b}\leftarrow\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i,b},\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,b})
for
