i\in[\lambda]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
.

This modification is just conceptual and we have

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
:

This is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
except for a conceptual modification that the measurement of the returned key
\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}
is deferred until the end of the experiment. For clarity, we give the full description of this experiment.

1.

The challenger generates
(\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i,b},\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{dk}_{i,b})\leftarrow\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})
for
i\in[\lambda]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
,
sets

\mathsf{ek}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
and

\mathpzc{dk}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{i\in[\lambda]}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\ket{0}\ket{\cPKE.\dk_{i,0}}+\ket{1}\ket{\cPKE.\dk_{i,1}}\right)
,
and sends
\mathsf{ek}
and
\mathpzc{dk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}
queries
\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}

to the verification oracle. The challenger returns
\top
to
\mathpzc{A}
as the response from the oracle.

3.

The challenger chooses
\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,b}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
for
i\in[\lambda]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
generates

\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{i,b}\leftarrow\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i,b},\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,b})
for
i\in[\lambda]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
, and sends
\mathsf{ct}^{*}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs
\mathsf{m}^{\prime}=\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{1}\|\ldots\|\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{\lambda}
.
The challenger outputs
0
as the final outcome of this experiment if
\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{i}\notin\{\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,0},\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,1}\}
for some
i\in[\lambda]
.

5.

Otherwise,
the challenger applies a binary-outcome measurement
(\bm{I}-\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}^{\mathsf{vk}},\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}^{\mathsf{vk}})
on
\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}
where
\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}^{\mathsf{vk}}
is the projection defined in the description of
\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}
.
the challenger outputs the outcome of the measurement as the final outcome of this experiment.

By the deferred measurement principle, we have
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=1]
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
:

This is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
except that
the challenger measures the returned key
\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}
in the
computational basis
instead of applying the projective measurement
(\bm{I}-\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}^{\mathsf{vk}},\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}^{\mathsf{vk}})
in Step
5
, and the condition to output
1
is modified as follows:

•

Let
\{\widetilde{b}_{i},\mathsf{cPKE}.\widetilde{\mathsf{dk}}_{i}\}_{i\in[\lambda]}
be the outcome of the measurement of
\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}
in the computational basis.
If there is
i\in[\lambda]
such that
\mathsf{cPKE}.\widetilde{\mathsf{dk}}_{i}\neq\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{dk}_{i,\widetilde{b}_{i}}
, the challenger outputs
0
as the final outcome of this experiment.
Otherwise, define
\mathbf{b}=b_{1}\|\ldots\|b_{\lambda}\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
in such a way that
\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{i}=\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,b_{i}}
for
i\in[\lambda]
. Note that such
\mathbf{b}
must exist since this step is invoked only when the challenger does not output
0
in Step
4
.
17
17

17

If
\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,0}=\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,1}
(which happens with a negligible probability), then we
set
b_{i}\coloneqq 0
.

If there is
i\in[\lambda]
such that
\widetilde{b}_{i}\neq b_{i}
, the challenger outputs
1
and otherwise
0
as the final output of the experiment.

We prove that
if
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}=1]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
, then it holds that
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=1]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.
The intuition is as follows: If we have
\widetilde{b}_{i}=b_{i}
with overwhelming probability, then

\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}
has a negligible amplitude on
\bigotimes_{i\in[\lambda]}\ket{b'_i}\ket{\cPKE.\widetilde{\dk}_{b'_i}}
for all
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\neq\mathbf{b}
.
In this case, the probability that
\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}
is projected onto
\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}^{\mathsf{vk}}
is negligible since the right key
\mathpzc{dk}
has an exponentially small amplitude on
\bigotimes_{i\in[\lambda]}\ket{b_i}\ket{\cPKE.\widetilde{\dk}_{b_i}}
.
See
Lemma

5.4
for the detail.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
:

This is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
except that the challenger chooses
i^{*}\leftarrow[\lambda]
at the beginning of the experiment and the condition to output
1
is modified to that

\widetilde{b}_{i^{*}}\neq b_{i^{*}}
holds for the a priori chosen
i^{*}
instead of for some
i\in[\lambda]
.

Whenever there is
i
such that
\widetilde{b}_{i}\neq b_{i}
, the probability that
i^{*}\leftarrow[\lambda]
satisfies
\widetilde{b}_{i^{*}}\neq b_{i^{*}}
is at least
\frac{1}{\lambda}
. Thus, we have
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}=1]\geq\frac{1}{\lambda}\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}=1]
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}
:

This is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
except that challenger measures the register
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{DK}_{i^{*}}}}
of the decryption key
\mathpzc{dk}
in the computational basis before giving
\mathpzc{dk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
. (See the description of
\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{KG}
for the definition of register
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{DK}_{i^{*}}}}
.)

Note that the measurement of
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{DK}_{i^{*}}}}
in the computational basis yields either
(0,\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{dk}_{i^{*},0})
or
(1,\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{dk}_{i^{*},1})
. In particular, there are only two possible outcomes.
Thus, by
Lemma

2.21
,
we have
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}=1]\geq\frac{1}{2}\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}=1]
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}
:

This is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}
except that the collapsing caused by measuring
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{DK}_{i^{*}}}}
is simulated by classical randomness. That is, the challenger chooses
b^{*}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
at the beginning and sets

\mathpzc{dk}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{i\in[\lambda]\setminus\{i^{*}\}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\ket{0}\ket{\cPKE.\dk_{i,0}}+\ket{1}\ket{\cPKE.\dk_{i,1}}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{DK}_{i}}}}\otimes(\ket{b^*}\ket{\cPKE.\dk_{i^*,b^*}})_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{DK}_{i^{*}}}}}.

(79)

It is easy to see that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}
are identical from the view of
\mathpzc{A}
, and thus we have
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}=1]
. In
Lemma

5.6
, we prove that
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}=1]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
by using the OW-CPA security (which is implied by CoIC-KLA security) of
\mathsf{SKL}
.

By combining the above, we have
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
. This means that
\mathsf{SKL}
is
OW-KLA
secure.
We are left to prove
Lemmas

5.3
,
5.4
and
5.6

Lemma 5.3.

It holds that
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hybi{1}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{2}=1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
if
\mathsf{cPKE}
is
CoIC-KLA
secure.

Proof.

We define additional hybrids
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.j}
for
j\in[\lambda+1]
as follows.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.j}
:

This is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
except that
\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{i,b}
is generated as

\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{i,b}\leftarrow\begin{cases}\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i,b},\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,a_{i}\oplus b})&i<j\\
\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i,b},\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,a_{i}})&i\geq j\end{cases}

(80)

for
i\in[\lambda]
.

Clearly, we have
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.1}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.\lambda+1}
. Thus, it suffices to prove that

\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.j+1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.j}=1]\right|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Remark that the only difference between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.j+1}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.j}
is the way of generating
\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{j,1}
.
To show that
\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.j+1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.j}=1]\right|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
,
we construct
\mathpzc{B}
against
CoIC-KLA
security of
\mathsf{cPKE}
as follows.

\mathpzc{B}(\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}^{*}_{0},\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}^{*}_{1},\mathpzc{dk}^{*})
:

It works as follows.

1.

Generate
(\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i,b},\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{dk}_{i,b})\leftarrow\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})
for
i\in[\lambda]\setminus\{j\}
and
b\in\{0,1\}
and
set

\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{j,b}\coloneqq\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}^{*}_{b}

for
b\in\{0,1\}
.
Set

\mathsf{ek}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
and

\mathpzc{dk}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{i\in[\lambda]\setminus\{j\}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\ket{0}\ket{\cPKE.\dk_{i,0}}+\ket{1}\ket{\cPKE.\dk_{i,1}}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{DK}_{i}}}}\otimes\mathpzc{dk}^{*}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{DK}_{j}}}}.

(81)

This implicitly defines
\mathsf{vk}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{dk}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
where
\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{dk}_{j,b}
is the decryption key corresponding to
\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{dk}_{j,b}
chosen by the external challenger for
b\in\{0,1\}
(but
\mathpzc{B}
cannot know
\mathsf{vk}
).

2.

Send
\mathsf{ek}
and
\mathpzc{dk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
and receives the verification query
\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}
from
\mathpzc{A}
.

3.

Apply a binary-outcome measurement
(\bm{I}-\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}^{\mathsf{vk}},\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}^{\mathsf{vk}})
on
\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}
.
This is possible by simulating the projection on
\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{DK}_{i}}}\}_{i\neq j}
by itself while forwarding
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{DK}_{j}}}
to its own verification oracle.
If the outcome is
0
, output
0
. Otherwise, return
\top
to
\mathpzc{A}
as the response from the oracle.

4.

Choose
\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,b}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
for
i\in[\lambda]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
and
a_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
for
i\in[\lambda]\setminus\{j\}
, send
(\mathsf{m}^{*}_{j,0},\mathsf{m}^{*}_{j,1})
to the external challenger, and receive
(\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{0},\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{1})
from the challenger.
This implicitly defines
a_{j}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
and
\beta\leftarrow\{0,1\}
where the challenger generates

\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{0}\coloneqq\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{j},m^{*}_{j,a_{j}})

and

\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{1}\coloneqq\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{j},m^{*}_{j,a_{j}\oplus\beta})
(but
\mathpzc{B}
cannot know
a_{j}
or
\beta
).
18
18

18

Here,
\beta
plays the role of
b
in the experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{cPKE},\mathpzc{B}}^{\mathsf{coic}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda})
in
Definition

4.11
. This is because
b
is used in another meaning in this section.

5.

Generate

\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{i,b}\leftarrow,\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i,b},\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,a_{i}\oplus b})
for
i\in[\lambda]\setminus\{j\}
and
b\in\{0,1\}
, set

\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{j,b}\coloneqq\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{b}
for
b\in\{0,1\}
,
send
\mathsf{ct}^{*}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
to
\mathpzc{A}
, and receive
\mathsf{m}^{\prime}=\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{1}\|\ldots\|\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{\lambda}
from
\mathpzc{A}
.

6.

Output
1
if
\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{i}\in\{\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,0},\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,1}\}
for all
i\in[\lambda]
and otherwise output
0
.

We have

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{cPKE},\mathpzc{B}}^{\mathsf{coic}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(\lambda)

(82)

\displaystyle=2\left|\Pr[\mathpzc{B}(\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}^{*}_{0},\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}^{*}_{1},\mathpzc{dk}^{*})=\beta]-\frac{1}{2}\right|

(83)

\displaystyle=\left|\Pr[\mathpzc{B}(\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}^{*}_{0},\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}^{*}_{1},\mathpzc{dk}^{*})=1|\beta=0]-\Pr[\mathpzc{B}(\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}^{*}_{0},\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}^{*}_{1},\mathpzc{dk}^{*})=1|\beta=1]\right|

(84)

\displaystyle=\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.j+1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.j}=1]\right|

(85)

where
(\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}^{*}_{0},\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{dk}^{*}_{0})\leftarrow\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})
,

(\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}^{*}_{1},\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{dk}^{*}_{1})\leftarrow\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})
, and

\mathpzc{dk}^{*}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{\cPKE.\dk^*_0}+\ket{\cPKE.\dk^*_1})
.
Thus,
\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.j+1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.j}=1]\right|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
by the
CoIC-KLA
security of
\mathsf{cPKE}
.
This completes the proof of
Lemma

5.3
.
∎

Lemma 5.4.

If
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}=1]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
, then it holds that
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=1]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

Proof.

Let
\epsilon\coloneqq\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=1]
.
For

\mathsf{vk}=\{\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{dk}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
and

\mathbf{b}=b_{1}\|\ldots\|b_{\lambda}\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
, let
\mathsf{E}_{\mathbf{b}}^{\mathsf{vk}}
be the event that

\mathsf{vk}
is chosen as a verification key and

\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{i}=\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,b_{i}}
for all
i\in[\lambda]
. Let
\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}_{\mathbf{b}}^{\mathsf{vk}}
be the state of the returned key conditioned on
\mathsf{E}_{\mathbf{b}}^{\mathsf{vk}}
.
Clearly, we have

\sum_{\mathsf{vk},\mathbf{b}}\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{\mathbf{b}}^{\mathsf{vk}}]\cdot\Tr(\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}^{\vk}\widetilde{\qdk}_{\mathbf{b}}^{\vk})=\epsilon.

(86)

Let
\mathtt{Good}
be a subset defined as

\mathtt{Good}\coloneqq\left\{(\mathsf{vk},\mathbf{b}):\Tr(\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}^{\vk}\widetilde{\qdk}_{\mathbf{b}}^{\vk})\geq\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right\}.

(87)

Then, by a standard averaging argument, it holds that

\displaystyle\sum_{(\mathsf{vk},\mathbf{b})\in\mathtt{Good}}\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{\mathbf{b}}^{\mathsf{vk}}]\geq\frac{\epsilon}{2}.

(88)

For
\mathsf{vk}=\{\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{dk}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
and
\mathbf{b}=b_{1}\|\ldots\|b_{\lambda}
, let
\Pi_{\neq\mathbf{b}}^{\mathsf{vk}}
be a projection defined as follows:

\displaystyle\Pi_{\neq\mathbf{b}}^{\mathsf{vk}}\coloneqq\sum_{\mathbf{b^{\prime}}\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}\setminus\{\mathbf{b}\}}\bigotimes_{i\in[\lambda]}\ket{b'_i}\ket{\cPKE.\dk_{i,b'_i}}\bra{b'_i}\bra{\cPKE.\dk_{i,b'_i}}.

(89)

Then, by the definition of
\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
, one can see that

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}=1]=\sum_{\mathsf{vk},\mathbf{b}}\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{\mathbf{b}}^{\mathsf{vk}}]\cdot\Tr\left(\Pi_{\neq\mathbf{b}}^{\mathsf{vk}}\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}_{\mathbf{b}}^{\mathsf{vk}}\right).

(90)

Then, we show the following proposition.

Proposition 5.5.

For any
(\mathsf{vk},\mathbf{b})\in\mathtt{Good}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\Tr\left(\Pi_{\neq\mathbf{b}}^{\mathsf{vk}}\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}_{\mathbf{b}}^{\mathsf{vk}}\right)\geq\frac{\epsilon}{4}-2^{-\lambda}.

(91)

Proof of
Proposition

5.5
.

By diagonalization, we can write

\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}_{\mathbf{b}}^{\mathsf{vk}}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}p_{j}\ket{\psi_j}\bra{\psi_j}

(92)

where
0<p_{j}\leq 1
,
\sum_{j=1}^{N}p_{j}=1
, and
\left|\bra{\psi_j}\ket{\psi_j}\right|=1
.
For each
j\in[N]
, it holds that

\displaystyle\Tr(\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}^{\vk}\ket{\psi_j}\bra{\psi_j})

(93)

\displaystyle=2^{-\lambda}\left\|\left(\sum_{\mathbf{b^{\prime}}\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}}\bigotimes_{i\in[\lambda]}\bra{b'_i}\bra{\cPKE.\dk_{i,b'_i}}\right)\ket{\psi_j}\right\|^{2}

(94)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-\lambda+1}\left(\left\|\left(\sum_{\mathbf{b^{\prime}}\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}\setminus\{\mathbf{b}\}}\bigotimes_{i\in[\lambda]}\bra{b'_i}\bra{\cPKE.\dk_{i,b'_i}}\right)\ket{\psi_j}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\left(\bigotimes_{i\in[\lambda]}\bra{b_i}\bra{\cPKE.\dk_{i,b_i}}\right)\ket{\psi_j}\right\|^{2}\right)

(95)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-\lambda+1}\left(\left(2^{\lambda}-1\right)\sum_{\mathbf{b^{\prime}}\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}\setminus\{\mathbf{b}\}}\left\|\left(\bigotimes_{i\in[\lambda]}\bra{b'_i}\bra{\cPKE.\dk_{i,b'_i}}\right)\ket{\psi_j}\right\|^{2}+1\right)

(96)

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\|\Pi_{\neq\mathbf{b}}^{\mathsf{vk}}\ket{\psi_j}\right\|^{2}+2^{-\lambda+1}

(97)

where the inequalities in the third and fourth lines follow from Cauchy–Schwarz inequality.

Then, it holds that

\displaystyle\Tr(\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}^{\vk}\widetilde{\qdk}_{\mathbf{b}}^{\vk})

(98)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}p_{j}\Tr(\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}^{\vk}\ket{\psi_j}\bra{\psi_j})

(99)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{N}p_{j}\left(2\left\|\Pi_{\neq\mathbf{b}}^{\mathsf{vk}}\ket{\psi_j}\right\|^{2}+2^{-\lambda+1}\right)

(100)

\displaystyle=2\Tr\left(\Pi_{\neq\mathbf{b}}^{\mathsf{vk}}\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}_{\mathbf{b}}^{\mathsf{vk}}\right)+2^{-\lambda+1}.

(101)

Since we assume
(\mathsf{vk},\mathbf{b})\in\mathtt{Good}
, it holds that
\Tr(\Pi_{\mathrm{vrfy}}^{\vk}\widetilde{\qdk}_{\mathbf{b}}^{\vk})\geq\frac{\epsilon}{2}
.
By combining the above,
Proposition

5.5
is proven.
∎

Then, we have

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}=1]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{vk},\mathbf{b}}\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{\mathbf{b}}^{\mathsf{vk}}]\cdot\Tr\left(\Pi_{\neq\mathbf{b}}^{\mathsf{vk}}\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}_{\mathbf{b}}^{\mathsf{vk}}\right)

(102)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{(\mathsf{vk},\mathbf{b})\in\mathtt{Good}}\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{\mathbf{b}}^{\mathsf{vk}}]\cdot\Tr\left(\Pi_{\neq\mathbf{b}}^{\mathsf{vk}}\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}_{\mathbf{b}}^{\mathsf{vk}}\right)

(103)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{(\mathsf{vk},\mathbf{b})\in\mathtt{Good}}\Pr[\mathsf{E}_{\mathbf{b}}^{\mathsf{vk}}]\cdot\left(\frac{\epsilon}{4}-2^{-\lambda}\right)

(104)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\epsilon}{2}\cdot\left(\frac{\epsilon}{4}-2^{-\lambda}\right)

(105)

where the second inequality follows from
Proposition

5.5
and the third inequality follows from Eq.
88
.
Recalling that
\epsilon=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=1]
, the above inequality implies
Lemma

5.4
.
∎

Lemma 5.6.

It holds that
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}=1]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
if
\mathsf{cPKE}
is OW-CPA secure.

Proof.

For clarity, we give the full description of
\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}
below.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}
:

It works as follows:

1.

The challenger chooses
i^{*}\leftarrow[\lambda]
and
b^{*}\in\{0,1\}
,
generates
(\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i,b},\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{dk}_{i,b})\leftarrow\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})
for
i\in[\lambda]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
,
sets

\mathsf{ek}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
and

\mathpzc{dk}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{i\in[\lambda]\setminus\{i^{*}\}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\ket{0}\ket{\cPKE.\dk_{i,0}}+\ket{1}\ket{\cPKE.\dk_{i,1}}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{DK}_{i}}}}\otimes(\ket{b^*}\ket{\cPKE.\dk_{i^*,b^*}})_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{DK}_{i^{*}}}}}

(106)

and sends
\mathsf{ek}
and
\mathpzc{dk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}
queries
\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}

to the verification oracle. The challenger returns
\top
to
\mathpzc{A}
as the response from the oracle.

3.

The challenger chooses
\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,b}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
for
i\in[\lambda]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
, generates

\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{i,b}\leftarrow\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i,b},\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,b})
for
i\in[\lambda]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
, and sends
\mathsf{ct}^{*}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs
\mathsf{m}^{\prime}=\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{1}\|\ldots\|\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{\lambda}
.
The challenger outputs
0
as the final output of the experiment if
\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{i}\notin\{\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,0},\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,1}\}
for some
i\in[\lambda]
.

5.

Otherwise, the challenger measures
\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}
in the computational basis, and let
\{\widetilde{b}_{i},\mathsf{cPKE}.\widetilde{\mathsf{dk}}_{i}\}_{i\in[\lambda]}
be the outcome.
If there is
i\in[\lambda]
such that
\mathsf{cPKE}.\widetilde{\mathsf{dk}}_{i}\neq\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{dk}_{i,\widetilde{b}_{i}}
, the challenger outputs
0
as the final outcome of this experiment.
Otherwise, define
\mathbf{b}=b_{1}\|\ldots\|b_{\lambda}\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
in such a way that
\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{i}=\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,b_{i}}
for
i\in[\lambda]
. Note that such
\mathbf{b}
must exist since this step is invoked only when the challenger does not output
0
in Step
4
.
19
19

19

If
\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,0}=\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i,1}
(which happens with a negligible probability), then we
set
b_{i}\coloneqq 0
.

If
\widetilde{b}_{i^{*}}\neq b_{i^{*}}
, the challenger outputs
1
and otherwise
0
as the final output of the experiment.

Suppose that we simulate
\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}
for
\mathpzc{A}
while embedding a problem instance of the OW-CPA security of
\mathsf{cPKE}
into
\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i^{*},b^{*}\oplus 1}
and
\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{i^{*},b^{*}\oplus 1}
. Remark that this is possible without knowing
\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{dk}_{i^{*},b^{*}\oplus 1}
.
Suppose that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}=1
occurs in the simulated execution. Then, we in particular have
\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{i^{*}}=\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i^{*},b_{i^{*}}}
,
\mathsf{cPKE}.\widetilde{\mathsf{dk}}_{i^{*}}=\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{dk}_{i^{*},\widetilde{b}_{i^{*}}}
, and
\widetilde{b}_{i^{*}}\neq b_{i^{*}}
.
We consider the following two sub-cases.

1.

If
b_{i^{*}}=b^{*}
, then we have

\widetilde{b}_{i^{*}}=b^{*}\oplus 1
. This implies
\mathsf{cPKE}.\widetilde{\mathsf{dk}}_{i^{*}}=\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{dk}_{i^{*},b^{*}\oplus 1}
. Then we can decrypt
\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i^{*},b^{*}\oplus 1}
by honestly running the decryption algorithm with
\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{dk}_{i^{*},b^{*}\oplus 1}
. This contradicts the OW-CPA security of
\mathsf{cPKE}
.

2.

If
b_{i^{*}}\neq b^{*}
, then we have
\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{i^{*}}=\mathsf{m}^{*}_{i^{*},b^{*}\oplus 1}
, which is the message encrypted in
\mathsf{cPKE}.\mathsf{ek}_{i^{*},b^{*}\oplus 1}
. This means that we can break the OW-CPA security of
\mathsf{cPKE}
.

Neither of them occurs with a non-negligible probability assuming the OW-CPA security of
\mathsf{cPKE}
.
Thus,
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}=1]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
. This completes the proof of
Lemma

5.6
. ∎

This completes the proof of
Theorem

5.2
.
∎

Remark
5.7 (On OMUR).

We can show that
\mathsf{SKL}
constructed above also satisfies OMUR. Since there is a generic conversion to add OMUR as shown in
Lemma

3.10
anyway, we only give a proof sketch.

We reduce OMUR to 1-key OW-KLA security. Suppose that there is an adversary that breaks OMUR, i.e., passes the verification twice. Then roughly speaking, we can use it to break 1-key OW-KLA security by sending one of them to the verification oracle and using the other one to decrypt the challenge message. There is an issue that the reduction algorithm may make only one verification query while the adversary against OMUR may make arbitrarily many verification queries. To resolve this issue, we can use a similar idea to that used in the proof of
Lemma

3.10
. The reduction algorithm guesses the first two queries to be accepted. Conditioned on that the guess is correct, the reduction algorithm can simulate the verification oracle by simply returning
\bot
to all queries except for the two queries that are guessed to be accepted until the adversary make the second guessed query.
The guess is correct with probability
\binom{Q}{2}^{-1}
where
Q
is the number of queries. Thus, the reduction works with a polynomial security loss.
Since we already proved that
\mathsf{SKL}
is 1-query OW-KLA secure (
Theorem

5.2
), the above reduction shows that it satisfies OMUR.

6
Attribute-Based Encryption with Secure Key Leasing

6.1
Definitions

Definition 6.1 (ABE with Secure Key Leasing).

An ABE-SKL scheme
\mathsf{ABE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}
is a tuple of six algorithms
(\mathsf{Setup},\mathpzc{KG},\mathsf{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Cert},\mathsf{Vrfy})
.
Below, let
\mathcal{X}=\{\mathcal{X}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda}
,
\mathcal{Y}=\{\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda}
, and
R=\{R_{\lambda}:\mathcal{X}_{\lambda}\times\mathcal{Y}_{\lambda}\to\{0,1\}\}_{\lambda}
be the ciphertext space, the key attribute space, and the associated relation of
\mathsf{ABE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}
, respectively.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{msk})
:

The setup algorithm takes a security parameter
1^{\lambda}
, and outputs a public key
\mathsf{pk}
and master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
.

\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},y)\rightarrow(\mathpzc{usk},\mathsf{vk})
:

The key generation algorithm takes a master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
and a key attribute
y\in\mathcal{Y}
, and outputs a user secret key
\mathpzc{usk}
and a verification key
\mathsf{vk}
.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},x,m)\rightarrow\mathsf{ct}
:

The encryption algorithm takes a public key
\mathsf{pk}
, a ciphertext attribute
x\in\mathcal{X}
, and a plaintext
m
, and outputs a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
.

\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathpzc{usk},x,\mathsf{ct})\rightarrow z
:

The decryption algorithm takes a user secret key
\mathpzc{usk}
, a ciphertext attribute
x
, and a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
and outputs a value
z\in\{\bot\}\cup\{0,1\}^{\ell}
.

\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime})\rightarrow\top/\bot
:

The verification algorithm takes a verification key
\mathsf{vk}
and a quantum state
\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime}
, and outputs
\top
or
\bot
.

Decryption correctness:

For every
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
y\in\mathcal{Y}
satisfying
R(x,y)=1
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathpzc{usk},x,\mathsf{ct})\allowbreak=m\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathpzc{usk},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},y)\\
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},x,m)\end{array}\right]=1-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Verification correctness:

For every
y\in\mathcal{Y}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{usk})=\top\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathpzc{usk},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},y)\\
\end{array}\right]=1-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Definition 6.2 (Adaptive Indistinguishability against Key Leasing Attacks).

We say that an ABE-SKL scheme
\mathsf{ABE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}
for relation
R:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\{0,1\}
is secure against adaptive indistinguishability against key leasing attacks (
Ada-IND-KLA
), if it satisfies the following requirement, formalized from the experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathpzc{A},\mathsf{ABE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})
between an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
and a challenger:

1.

At the beginning, the challenger runs
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})

and initialize the list
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
to be an empty set.
Throughout the experiment,
\mathpzc{A}
can access the following oracles.

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}(y)
:

Given
y
, it finds an entry of the form
(y,\mathsf{vk},V)
from
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
. If there is such an entry, it returns
\bot
.
Otherwise, it generates
(\mathpzc{usk},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},y)
, sends
\mathpzc{usk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
, and adds
(y,\mathsf{vk},\bot)
to
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
.

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}(y,\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime})
:

Given
(y,\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime})
, it finds an entry
(y,\mathsf{vk},V)
from
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
. (If there is no such entry, it returns
\bot
.)
It then runs
d\coloneqq\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime})
and returns
d
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
If
V=\bot
, it updates the entry into
(y,\mathsf{vk},d)
.

2.

When
\mathpzc{A}
sends
(x^{*},m_{0},m_{1})
to the challenger, the challenger checks if for any entry
(y,\mathsf{vk},V)
in
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
such that
R(x^{*},y)=1
, it holds that
V=\top
. If so, the challenger generates
\mathsf{ct}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},x^{*},m_{\mathsf{coin}})
and sends
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
to
\mathpzc{A}
. Otherwise, the challenger outputs
0
.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
continues to make queries to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}(\cdot)
and
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}(\cdot,\cdot)
.
However,
\mathpzc{A}
is not allowed to send a key attribute
y
such that
R(x^{*},y)=1
to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs a guess
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
for
\mathsf{coin}
. The challenger outputs
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
as the final output of the experiment.

For any QPT
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{ABE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[\expc{\ABESKL,\qA}{ada}{ind}{kla} (1^\secp,0) \ra 1] - \Pr[\expc{\ABESKL,\qA}{ada}{ind}{kla} (1^\secp,1) \ra 1] }\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(112)

Remark
6.3.

In
Definition

6.2
, the key generation oracle returns
\bot
if the same
y
is queried more than once. To handle the situation where multiple keys for the same attribute
y
are generated, we need to manage indices for
y
such as
(y,1,vk_{1},V_{1}),(y,2,vk_{2},V_{2})
. Although we can reflect the index management in the definition, it complicates the definition and prevents readers from understanding the essential idea.
Thus, we use the simplified definition above.

We also consider relaxed versions of the above security notion.

Definition 6.4 (Selective indistinguishability against key leasing attacks).

We consider selective indistinguishability against key leasing attacks (
Sel-IND-KLA
). For doing so, we consider the same security game as that for
Ada-IND-KLA
except that the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
should declare its target
x^{*}
at the beginning of the game (even before it is given
\mathsf{pk}
).
We then define the advantage
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{ABE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(\lambda)
for the selective case similarly. We say
\mathsf{ABE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}
is secure against selective indistinguishability against key leasing attack if for any QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
,
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{ABE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(\lambda)
is negligible.

We also consider the following security notion where we introduce additional restriction that the number of distinguishing keys that are issued (and eventually returned) before
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
is generated is bounded by some predetermined parameter
q
.
Here, distinguishing key refers to a key that can decrypt the challenge ciphertext if it is not returned.

Definition 6.5 (Bounded Distinguishing Key
Ada-IND-KLA
/
Sel-IND-KLA
for ABE).

For defining bounded distinguishing key
Ada-IND-KLA
security, we consider the same security game as that for
Ada-IND-KLA
(i.e.,
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathpzc{A},\mathsf{ABE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})
) except that we change the step
2
in
Definition

6.2
with the following:

2’

When
\mathpzc{A}
sends
(x^{*},m_{0},m_{1})
to the challenger, the challenger checks if there are at most
q
entries
(y,\mathsf{vk},V)
in
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
such that
R(x^{*},y)=1
and for all these entries,
V=\top
. If so, the challenger generates
\mathsf{ct}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},x^{*},m_{\mathsf{coin}})
and sends
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
to
\mathpzc{A}
. Otherwise, the challenger outputs
0
.

We then define the advantage
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{ABE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL},\mathpzc{A},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{q}}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(\lambda)
similarly to
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{ABE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(\lambda)
.
We say
\mathsf{ABE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL}
is
q
-bounded distinguishing key
Ada-IND-KLA
secure if for any QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
,
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{ABE}\textrm{-}\mathsf{SKL},\mathpzc{A},q}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(\lambda)
is negligible.
We also define
q
-bounded distinguishing key
Sel-IND-KLA
security analogously by enforcing the adversary to output its target
x^{*}
at the beginning of the game.

We emphasize that while the number of distinguishing keys that the adversary can obtain in the game is bounded by a fixed polynomial, the number of non-distinguishing keys (i.e., keys for
y
with
R(x^{*},y)=0
) can be unbounded.

6.2
1-Bounded Distinguishing Key Construction

We construct an ABE-SKL scheme
\mathsf{1ABE}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathpzc{KG},\mathsf{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Vrfy})
for relation
R:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\{0,1\}
with
1
-bounded distinguishing key
Ada-IND-KLA
/
Sel-IND-KLA
security whose message space is
\{0,1\}^{\ell}

by using the following building blocks.

•

IND-KLA secure PKE-SKL
\mathsf{SKL}.(\mathpzc{KG},\mathsf{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Vrfy})
.
Without loss of generality,
we assume that
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}}
and the randomness space used by
\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}
is
\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\mathsf{rand}}}
for some
\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}(\lambda)
and
\ell_{\mathsf{rand}}(\lambda)
.
We also assume that the message space of
\mathsf{SKL}
is
\{0,1\}^{\ell}
.

•

Adaptively/Selectively secure ABE
\mathsf{ABE}.(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KG},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
for relation
R
with message space
\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
.

•

A garbling scheme
\mathsf{GC}=(\mathsf{Grbl},\mathsf{GCEval})
.
Without loss of generality, we assume that the labels of
\mathsf{GC}
are in
\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
:

•

For
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
, run
(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,b},\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{msk}_{i,b})\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

•

Output
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{msk})\coloneqq(\{\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}],b\in\{0,1\}},\{\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{msk}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}],b\in\{0,1\}})
.

\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},y)
:

•

Generate
(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})
.

•

Run
\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}[i]},y)

for
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]
, where
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}[i]
denotes the
i
-th bit of the binary string
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}
.

•

Output
\mathpzc{usk}\coloneqq(\{\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk})
and
\mathsf{vk}\coloneqq\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}
.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},x,m)
:

•

Choose
\mathsf{R}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\mathsf{rand}}}
.

•

Construct circuit
E[m,\mathsf{R}]
, which is a circuit that takes as input an encryption key
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}
of
\mathsf{SKL}
and outputs
\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek},m;\mathsf{R})
.

•

Compute
(\{\mathsf{lab}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}],b\in\{0,1\}},\widetilde{E})\leftarrow\mathsf{Grbl}(1^{\lambda},E[m,\mathsf{R}])
.

•

Run
\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,b}\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,b},x,\mathsf{lab}_{i,b})

for
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
.

•

Output
\mathsf{ct}\coloneqq(\{\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}],b\in\{0,1\}},\widetilde{E})
.

\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathpzc{usk},x,\mathsf{ct})
:

•

Parse
\mathpzc{usk}=(\{\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk})
and
\mathsf{ct}=(\{\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}],b\in\{0,1\}},\widetilde{E})
.

•

Compute
\mathsf{lab}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{sk}_{i},x,\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}[i]})
for
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]
.

•

Compute
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}=\mathsf{GCEval}(\widetilde{E},\{\mathsf{lab}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]})
.

•

Compute and output
m^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct})
.

\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime})
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{vk}=\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}
and
\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime}=(\{\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{\prime},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk}^{\prime})
.

•

Compute and output
\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk}^{\prime})
.

We show that the scheme satisfies decryption correctness. To see this, we first observe that the decryption algorithm correctly recovers labels of
\widetilde{E}
corresponding to the input
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}
by the correctness of
\mathsf{ABE}
. Therefore,
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}
recovered by the garbled circuit evaluation equals to
\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek},m;\mathsf{R})
by the correctness of
\mathsf{GC}
. Then, the message
m
is recovered in the last step by the correctness of
\mathsf{SKL}
.
We can also see that the verification correctness follows from that of
\mathsf{SKL}
.

Theorem 6.6.

If
\mathsf{ABE}
is adaptively (resp., selectively) secure,
\mathsf{GC}
is secure, and
\mathsf{SKL}
is IND-KLA secure, then
\mathsf{1ABE}
above is
1
-bounded distinguishing key
Ada-IND-KLA
(resp.,
Sel-IND-KLA
) secure.

Proof of
Theorem

6.6
.

Here, we first focus on the proof for the case of
Ada-IND-KLA
and later mention the necessary modifications for the case of
Sel-IND-KLA
. Let
Q
be the upper bound on the number of key queries to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
before the challenge phase.
We define a sequence of hybrid games.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{1ABE},\mathpzc{A},1}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda},0)
. More specifically, it is as follows.

1.

The challenger generates
(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,b},\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{msk}_{i,b})\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})

for
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
and sends
\mathsf{pk}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,b}\}_{i,b}
to the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
.
The challenger then initializes the list
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
to be an empty set.

\mathpzc{A}
can access the following oracles.

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}(y^{(j)})
:

Given the
j
-th query
y^{(j)}
with
j\in[Q]
, if there is an entry of the form
(y^{(j)},\mathsf{vk},V)
, it outputs
\bot
. Otherwise, it generates
(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j)},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk}^{(j)},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}^{(j)})\leftarrow\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})
and
\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}^{(j)}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{msk}_{i,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j)}[i]},y^{(j)})
for
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]
,
where
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j)}[i]
is the
i
-th bit of the binary string
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j)}
.
It then sends
\mathpzc{usk}^{(j)}\coloneqq(\{\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}^{(j)}_{i}\}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk}^{(j)})
to
\mathpzc{A}
and adds
(y^{(j)},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}^{(j)},\bot)
to
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
.

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}(y,\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime})
:

Given
(y,\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime})
, it finds an entry
(y,\mathsf{vk},V)
from
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
and parse
\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime}=(\{\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime})
. (If there is no such entry, it returns
\bot
.)
It then parses
\mathsf{vk}=\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}

and returns
d\coloneqq\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk}^{\prime})
to
\mathpzc{A}
. It finally updates the entry into
(y,\mathsf{vk},d)
if
V=\bot
.

2.

When
\mathpzc{A}
sends
(x^{*},m_{0},m_{1})
to the challenger, the challenger checks whether there is at most one entry
(y,\mathsf{vk},V)
in
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
such that
R(x^{*},y)=1
and for that entry
V=\top
holds.
If so, the challenger generates
(\{\mathsf{lab}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}],b\in\{0,1\}},\widetilde{E})\leftarrow\mathsf{Grbl}(1^{\lambda},E[m_{0},\mathsf{R}])

and computes
\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,b}\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,b},x^{*},\mathsf{lab}_{i,b})
for
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
.
It then sends
\mathsf{ct}^{*}\coloneqq(\{\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,b}\}_{i,b},\widetilde{E})
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
Otherwise (i.e., if there are multiple entries with
R(x^{*},y)=1
or if there is an entry with
R(x^{*},y)=1
and
V=\bot
), it aborts the game and outputs
0
.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
continues to make queries to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}(\cdot)
and
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}(\cdot,\cdot)
.
However,
\mathpzc{A}
is not allowed to send a key attribute
y
such that
R(x^{*},y)=1
to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs a guess
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
for
\mathsf{coin}
. The challenger outputs
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
as the final output of the experiment.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
:

This game is the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
except that the challenger chooses random
\tilde{j}\leftarrow[Q]
at the beginning of the game.
Then, right before it computes the challenge ciphertext,
the challenger finds an index
j^{*}\in[Q]
such that
R(x^{*},y^{(j^{*})})=1
.
If there is no such a query, we define
j^{*}\coloneqq 1
.
20
20

20

Note that if there are multiple indices
j^{*}
satisfying the above, the challenger aborts and outputs
0
as specified in the previous game.
Therefore, there is at most one such
j^{*}
.

The challenger then checks whether
\tilde{j}=j^{*}
.
If so, the challenger continues the game until
\mathpzc{A}
outputs its guess.
Otherwise, it aborts the game and outputs
0
as the outcome of the game.

Since the choice of
\tilde{j}
is independent from the view of
\mathpzc{A}
and the outcome of the game is
1
only when
\tilde{j}=j^{*}
,
we can easily see that

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]/Q
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
:

This game is the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
except for the way
\{\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,b}\}_{i,b}
is generated.
Namely, we generate
\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,b}
as

\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,b}\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,b},\mathsf{lab}_{i,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})}[i]})

for
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
.

We observe that the labels being encrypted are changed only for positions of the form
(i,1\oplus\mathsf{lab}_{i,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})}[i]})
.
The adversary
\mathpzc{A}
cannot notice the change since it is not given any secret key that can decrypt the ABE ciphertexts for these positions.
To check this, recall that there is at most one index
j^{*}
such that
R(x^{*},y^{(j^{*})})=1
and for the corresponding key query, the adversary is given ABE secret keys for positions of the form
(i,\mathsf{lab}_{i,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})}[i]})
, but not for
(i,\mathsf{lab}_{i,1\oplus\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})}[i]})
.
Hence, we obtain
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hybi{1}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{2} =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
by the adaptive security of
\mathsf{ABE}
.
See
Lemma

6.7
for the detail.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
:

This game is the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
except for the way
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
is generated.
In particular, to generate
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
, we first run

(\{\mathsf{lab}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]},\widetilde{E})\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim.GC}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})},m_{0};\mathsf{R}))

and then compute

\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,b}\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,b},\mathsf{lab}_{i})

for
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
.

We claim that this game is indistinguishable from the previous one.
To see this, it suffices to show that

(\{\mathsf{lab}_{i,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})}[i]}\}_{i},\widetilde{E})

computed by
(\{\mathsf{lab}_{i,b}\}_{i,b},\widetilde{E})\leftarrow\mathsf{Grbl}(1^{\lambda},E[m_{0},\mathsf{R}])

and

(\{\mathsf{lab}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]},\widetilde{E})

computed by

(\{\mathsf{lab}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]},\widetilde{E})\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim.GC}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})},m_{0};\mathsf{R}))

are computationally indistinguishable.
This immediately follows from the security of the garbled circuit, since we have

E[m_{0},\mathsf{R}](\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})})=\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})},m_{0};\mathsf{R})

(113)

by the definition of
E
.
Hence, we obtain
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hybi{2}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{3} =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
:

This game is the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
except that the challenger
chooses
(\{\mathsf{lab}_{i}\}_{i},\widetilde{E})
by
(\{\mathsf{lab}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]},\widetilde{E})\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim.GC}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})},m_{1};\mathsf{R}))

instead of

(\{\mathsf{lab}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]},\widetilde{E})\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim.GC}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})},m_{0};\mathsf{R}))
.

To show that
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hybi{3}=1]-\Pr[\hybi{4} =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
, it suffices to show that
\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})},m_{0};\mathsf{R})
is indistinguishable from
\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})},m_{1};\mathsf{R})
for
\mathpzc{A}
, if it makes
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}
output
\top
on input
(y^{(j^{*})},\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime})
for some
\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime}

before the challenge ciphertext is given to
\mathpzc{A}
.
The indistinguishability follows from the security of
\mathsf{SKL}
,
since the fact that
\mathpzc{A}
passes the verification
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}
implies that
\mathpzc{A}
submitted
\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk}^{\prime}
such that
\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}^{(j^{*})},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk}^{\prime})=\top

before it is given the challenge ciphertext and therefore it has no longer the ability to decrypt the ciphertext.
To turn this intuition into a formal reduction, we have to embed the public key of
\mathsf{SKL}
into the answer to the
j^{*}
-th key generation query.
Since the reduction algorithm does not know
j^{*}
until
\mathpzc{A}
submits
(x^{*},m_{0},m_{1})
, it can only guess it.
The change in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
is introduced in order to incorporate the guess into the game so that the reduction is possible.
We refer to
Lemma

6.8
for the formal proof for
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hybi{3}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{4} =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{1ABE},\mathpzc{A},1}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda},1)
.

From the above discussion, we have

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{ Q \Pr[\hybi{3}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{0}=1]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q\absolutevalue{ \Pr[\hybi{3}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{1}=1]}

(114)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle Q\sum_{i\in[0,2]}\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hybi{i+1}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{i}=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

We then observe that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
(resp.,
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
) is the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
(resp.,
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
) except that
m_{1}
is used for the encryption instead of
m_{0}
. Therefore, we obtain

\absolutevalue{ Q \Pr[\hybi{4}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{5}=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)

analogously to Eq. (
114
) by considering similar sequence of the games with
m_{0}
being replaced by
m_{1}
in reverse order.
We therefore have

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\expc{\oneABE,\qA,1}{ada}{ind}{kla}(1^\secp,0)-\expc{\oneABE,\qA,1}{ada}{ind}{kla}(1^\secp,1)}

\displaystyle=\absolutevalue{ \Pr[\hybi{0}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{5}=1]}

\displaystyle\leq\absolutevalue{ \Pr[\hybi{0}=1] - Q \Pr[\hybi{3}=1]}+Q\cdot\absolutevalue{ \Pr[\hybi{3}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{4}=1]}+\absolutevalue{ \Pr[\hybi{5}=1] - Q \Pr[\hybi{4}=1]}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)

as desired.
It remains to prove
Lemmas

6.7
and
6.8
.

Lemma 6.7.

\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hybi{1}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{2} =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
if
\mathsf{ABE}
is adaptively secure.

Proof.

This can be reduced to the adaptive security of
\mathsf{ABE}
by a standard hybrid argument where we modify the way of generating
\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,1\oplus\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})}[i]}
for each
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]
one by one.
More precisely, the reduction works as follows.

We define additional hybrids
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.k}
for
k\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]
as follows.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.k}
:

This is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
except that
\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,1\oplus\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})}[i]}
is generated as

\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,1\oplus\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})}[i]}\leftarrow\begin{cases}\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,1\oplus\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})}[i]},\mathsf{lab}_{i,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})}[i]})&i<k\\
\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,1\oplus\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})}[i]},\mathsf{lab}_{i,1\oplus\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})}[i]})&i\geq k\end{cases}

(115)

for
i\in[\lambda]
.

Clearly, we have
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.1}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}+1}
. Thus, it suffices to prove that

\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.k+1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.k}=1]\right|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)

for all
k\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]
.
Remark that the only difference between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.k+1}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.k}
is the way of generating
\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{k,1\oplus\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})}[i]}
.
To show that
\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.k+1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.k}=1]\right|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
,
we construct
\mathpzc{B}
against adaptive security of
\mathsf{ABE}
as follows.

\mathpzc{B}(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{pk})
:

It works as follows.

1.

It chooses
\tilde{j}\leftarrow[Q]
and
(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j)},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk}^{(j)},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}^{(j)})\leftarrow\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})

for
j\in[Q]
.

2.

Generate
(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,b},\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{msk}_{i,b})\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{KG}(1^{\lambda})
for
(i,b)\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]\times\{0,1\}\backslash\{(k,1\oplus\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(\tilde{j})}[k])\}
.
Set

\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{k,1\oplus\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(\tilde{j})}[k]}\coloneqq\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{pk}

and send
\mathsf{pk}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,b}\}_{i,b}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

3.

\mathpzc{B}
initializes the list
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
to be an empty set and
simulates the following oracles for
\mathpzc{A}
.

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}(y^{(j)})
:

Given the
j
-th query
y^{(j)}
with
j\in[Q]
, if there is an entry of the form
(y^{(j)},\mathsf{vk},V)
, it outputs
\bot
. Otherwise, it generates
\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}^{(j)}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{msk}_{i,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j)}[i]},y^{(j)})
for
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]\backslash\{k\}
.
To simulate
\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}^{(j)}_{k}
,
\mathpzc{B}
preceeds as follows.
If
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j)}[k]=1\oplus\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(\tilde{j})}[k]
,
it queries
y^{(j)}
to its challenger.
The challenger runs

\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{msk},y^{(j)})

(116)

and returns it to
\mathpzc{B}
.

\mathpzc{B}
then sets
\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}^{(j)}_{k}\coloneqq\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}
.
Otherwise (i.e., if
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j)}[k]=\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(\tilde{j})}[k]
), it runs
\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}^{(j)}_{k}\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{msk}_{k,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j)}[k]},y^{(j)})
.
It then sends
\mathpzc{usk}^{(j)}\coloneqq(\{\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}^{(j)}_{i}\}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk}^{(j)})
to
\mathpzc{A}
and adds
(y^{(j)},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}^{(j)},\bot)
to
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
.

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}(y,\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime})
:

Given
(y,\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime})
, it finds an entry
(y,\mathsf{vk},V)
from
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
and parse
\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime}=(\{\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime})
. (If there is no such entry, it returns
\bot
.)
It then parses
\mathsf{vk}=\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}

and returns
d\coloneqq\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk}^{\prime})
to
\mathpzc{A}
. It finally updates the entry into
(y,\mathsf{vk},d)
if
V=\bot
.

4.

When
\mathpzc{A}
sends
(x^{*},m_{0},m_{1})
to the challenger,
\mathpzc{B}
checks whether there are multiple entries
(y,\mathsf{vk},V)
in
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
such that
R(x^{*},y)=1
or there is an entry
(y,\mathsf{vk},V)
in
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
with
R(x^{*},y)=1
and
V=\top
.
If so,
\mathpzc{B}
aborts the game and outputs
0
as its guess.
Otherwise,
\mathpzc{B}
defines
j^{*}\in[Q]
as in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
.
It then aborts and outputs
0
if
j^{*}\neq\tilde{j}
.
Otherwise,
\mathpzc{B}
computes
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
as follows.
It first chooses
\mathsf{R}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\mathsf{rand}}}
and
computes
(\{\mathsf{lab}_{i,b}\}_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}],b\in\{0,1\}},\widetilde{E})\leftarrow\mathsf{Grbl}(1^{\lambda},E[m_{0},\mathsf{R}])
.
It then computes

\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,b}

for
(i,b)\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]\times\{0,1\}\backslash\{(k,1\oplus\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})}[k])\}

as in
Equation

115
.

\mathpzc{B}
then submits
(\mathsf{lab}_{k,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})}[k]},\mathsf{lab}_{k,1\oplus\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})}[k]})
to its challenger.
Then, the challenger runs

\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{lab}_{\overline{\mathsf{coin}}\oplus\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})}[k]})

(117)

and gives
\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{ct}
to
\mathpzc{B}
,
where
\overline{\mathsf{coin}}\in\{0,1\}
is the coin chosen by the challenger.
Then,
\mathpzc{B}
sets
\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{k,1\oplus\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j^{*})}[k]}\coloneqq\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{ct}
and gives

\mathsf{ct}^{*}\coloneqq(\{\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,b}\}_{i,b},\widetilde{E})
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

5.

\mathpzc{A}
then continues to make queries to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}(\cdot)
and
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}(\cdot,\cdot)
.

\mathpzc{B}
answers the queries in the same manner as before the challenge query.

6.

\mathpzc{A}
finally outputs its guess.

\mathpzc{B}
outputs the same bit as its guess.

We first argue that
\mathpzc{B}
does not make any prohibited key query.
To see this, we first observe that for every key query
y
that
\mathpzc{B}
makes,
there exists
j
such that
y=y^{(j)}
.
We then observe that
R(x^{*},y^{(j)})=0
for
j\neq j^{*}
and
\mathpzc{B}
does not make a key query for
y^{(j^{*})}
in the above simulation.

We have

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{ABE},\mathpzc{B}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=2\left|\Pr[\mbox{ $\mathpzc{B}$ outputs $\overline{\mathsf{coin}}$ }]-\frac{1}{2}\right|

(118)

\displaystyle=\left|\Pr[\mbox{ $\mathpzc{B}$ outputs $1$ }|\overline{\mathsf{coin}}=0]-\Pr[\mbox{ $\mathpzc{B}$ outputs $1$ }|\overline{\mathsf{coin}}=1]\right|

(119)

\displaystyle=\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.k+1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.k}=1]\right|

(120)

where the probabilities are taken over the randomness used in the respective games. Thus,
\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.k+1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1.k}=1]\right|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
by the adaptive security of
\mathsf{ABE}
.
This completes the proof of
Lemma

6.7
.

∎

Lemma 6.8.

\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hybi{3}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{4} =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
if
\mathsf{SKL}
is IND-KLA secure.

Proof.

This can be reduced to the IND-KLA security security of
\mathsf{SKL}
.
To do so, we construct an adversary
\mathpzc{B}
against IND-KLA security of the scheme with advantage
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hybi{3}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{4} =1]}
as follows.

\mathpzc{B}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk})
:

It works as follows.

1.

It chooses
\tilde{j}\leftarrow[Q]
and
(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j)},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk}^{(j)},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}^{(j)})\leftarrow\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})

for
j\in[Q]\backslash\{\tilde{j}\}
.
It then sets
(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(\tilde{j})},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk}^{(\tilde{j})})\coloneqq(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk})
.
It then generates
(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,b},\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{msk}_{i,b})\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})

for
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
and sends
\mathsf{pk}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,b}\}_{i,b}
to the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

\mathpzc{B}
initializes the list
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
to be an empty set and
simulates the following oracles for
\mathpzc{A}
.

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}(y^{(j)})
:

Given the
j
-th query
y^{(j)}
with
j\in[Q]
, if there is an entry of the form
(y^{(j)},\mathsf{vk},V)
, it outputs
\bot
. Otherwise, it generates
\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}^{(j)}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{msk}_{i,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{(j)}[i]},y^{(j)})
for
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]
.
It then returns
\mathpzc{usk}^{(j)}\coloneqq(\{\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}^{(j)}_{i}\}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk}^{(j)})
to
\mathpzc{A}
and adds
(y^{(j)},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}^{(j)},\bot)
to
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
.

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}(y,\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime})
:

Given
(y,\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime})
, it finds an entry
(y,\mathsf{vk},V)
from
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
and parses
\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime}=(\{\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk}^{\prime})
. (If there is no such entry, it returns
\bot
.)
If
y=y^{(j)}
for
j\neq\tilde{j}
,
\mathpzc{B}
returns
d\coloneqq\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}^{(j)},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk}^{\prime})
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
Otherwise (i.e., if
y=y^{(\tilde{j})}
),
\mathpzc{B}
submits
\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk}^{\prime}
to its verification oracle.
Then,

d\coloneqq\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk}^{\prime})

(121)

is computed and returned to
\mathpzc{B}
.

\mathpzc{B}
then returns
d
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
It finally updates the entry into
(y,\mathsf{vk},d)
if
V=\bot
.

3.

When
\mathpzc{A}
sends
(x^{*},m_{0},m_{1})
to the challenger,

\mathpzc{B}
checks whether there are multiple entries
(y,\mathsf{vk},V)
in
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
such that
R(x^{*},y)=1
or there is an entry
(y,\mathsf{vk},V)
in
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
with
R(x^{*},y)=1
and
V=\top
.
If so,
\mathpzc{B}
aborts the game and outputs
0
as its guess.
Otherwise,
\mathpzc{B}
defines
j^{*}\in[Q]
as in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
.
It then aborts and outputs
0
if
j^{*}\neq\tilde{j}
.
Otherwise,
\mathpzc{B}
computes
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
as follows.
It first submits
(m_{0},m_{1})
to its challenger.
Then, the challenger runs

\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek},m_{\overline{\mathsf{coin}}})

(122)

and returns it to
\mathpzc{B}
, where
\overline{\mathsf{coin}}\in\{0,1\}
is the coin chosen by the challenger.

\mathpzc{B}
then runs
(\{\mathsf{lab}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]},\widetilde{E})\leftarrow\mathsf{Sim.GC}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct})
and computes

\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,b}\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,b},\mathsf{lab}_{i})

for
i\in[\ell_{\mathsf{ek}}]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
.
Then,
\mathpzc{B}
sets
\mathsf{ct}^{*}\coloneqq(\{\mathsf{abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,b}\}_{i,b},\widetilde{E})
and gives it to
\mathpzc{A}
.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
then continues to make queries to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}(\cdot)
and
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}(\cdot,\cdot)
.

\mathpzc{B}
answers the queries in the same manner as before the challenge query.

5.

\mathpzc{A}
finally outputs its guess.

\mathpzc{B}
outputs the same bit as its guess.

We then have

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{SKL},\mathpzc{B}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\left|\Pr[\mbox{ $\mathpzc{B}$ outputs $1$ }|\overline{\mathsf{coin}}=0]-\Pr[\mbox{ $\mathpzc{B}$ outputs $1$ }|\overline{\mathsf{coin}}=1]\right|

(123)

\displaystyle=\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=1]\right|

(124)

where the probabilities are taken over the randomness used in the respective games. Thus,
\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=1]\right|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
by the security of
\mathsf{SKL}
.
This completes the proof of
Lemma

6.8
.
∎

This completes the proof of
Theorem

6.6
for the case of adaptive security.

The proof for selective security.

The statement for selective security can be obtained immediately by considering the same sequence of games as adaptive security case with natural adaptations. In particular, we modify the reduction algorithm in
Lemma

6.7
so that it outputs
x^{*}
at the beginning of the game right after given
x^{*}
from
\mathpzc{A}
.

An alternative option is to consider a simpler proof that is tailored to selective setting. This is possible because the proof obtained by adapting the adaptive setting to the selective setting includes a redundant step.
In particular, we consider a sequence of games without
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
. The reason why
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
is not necessary is that in the selective setting, the reduction algorithm obtains
x^{*}
at the beginning of the game and can use this information throughout the game.
In particular, whenever
\mathpzc{A}
makes a key query
y^{(j)}
, the reduction algorithm can check whether
j^{*}=j
holds or not by computing the value of
R(x^{*},y^{(j)})
and there is no need to guess it.
By introducing this change, we can improve the reduction cost to be independent of
Q
.
∎

6.3
Q
-Bounded Distinguishing Key Construction

We construct an ABE-SKL scheme
\mathsf{qABE}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathpzc{KG},\mathsf{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Vrfy})
for relation
R:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\{0,1\}
with
q
-bounded distinguishing key
Ada-IND-KLA
(resp.,
Sel-IND-KLA
) security
from an ABE-SKL scheme
\mathsf{1ABE}=\mathsf{1ABE}.(\mathsf{Setup},\mathpzc{KG},\mathsf{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Vrfy})
for the same relation
R
with
1
-bounded distinguishing key
Ada-IND-KLA
(resp.,
Sel-IND-KLA
) security.
We note that the construction here is essentially the same as
[
ISV+17
]
, which converts a single collusion secure ABE scheme into a
q
-bounded collusion secure ABE.
However, our proof is more complex reflecting the fact that the adversary is allowed to make unbounded number of key queries (though the number of distinguishing keys is bounded).

The following construction uses parameters
v\coloneqq v(\lambda)
and
w\coloneqq w(\lambda)
.
We will set the parameters in
Theorem

6.9
.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
:

•

For
i\in[v]
and
j\in[w]
, run
(\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,j},\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{msk}_{i,j})\leftarrow\mathsf{1ABE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

•

Output
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{msk})\coloneqq(\{\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,j}\}_{i\in[v],j\in[w]},\{\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{msk}_{i,j}\}_{i\in[v],j\in[w]})
.

\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},y)
:

•

For
i\in[v]
, choose
j_{i}\leftarrow[w]
.

•

Run
(\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},\mathsf{1abe}.\mathpzc{usk}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{1ABE}.\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{msk}_{i,j_{i}},y)

for
i\in[v]
.

•

Output
\mathpzc{usk}\coloneqq\{j_{i},\mathsf{1abe}.\mathpzc{usk}_{i}\}_{i\in[v]}
and
\mathsf{vk}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{vk}_{i}\}_{i\in[v]}
.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},x,m)
:

•

Choose
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{v-1}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and set

\mu_{v}\coloneqq(\oplus_{i\in[v-1]}\mu_{i})\oplus m
, where
\oplus
denotes bit-wise XOR here.

•

Run
\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,j}\leftarrow\mathsf{1ABE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,j},x,\mu_{i})

for
i\in[v]
and
j\in[w]
.

•

Output
\mathsf{ct}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,j}\}_{i\in[v],j\in[w]}
.

\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathpzc{usk},x,\mathsf{ct})
:

•

Parse
\mathpzc{usk}\coloneqq\{j_{i},\mathsf{1abe}.\mathpzc{usk}_{i}\}_{i\in[v]}
and
\mathsf{ct}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,j}\}_{i\in[v],j\in[w]}
.

•

Compute
\mu^{\prime}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{1ABE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{1abe}.\mathpzc{usk}_{i},x,\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,j_{i}})
for
i\in[v]
.

•

Compute and output
m^{\prime}\coloneqq\oplus_{i\in[v]}\mu^{\prime}_{i}
.

\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime})
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{vk}=\{\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{vk}_{i}\}_{i\in[v]}
and
\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime}\coloneqq\{j_{i},\mathsf{1abe}.\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in[v]}
.

•

Compute
d_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{1ABE}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},\mathsf{1abe}.\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime}_{i})

for
i\in[v]
.

•

If
d_{i}=\top
for all
i\in[v]
, output
\top
. Otherwise, output
\bot
.

It is straightforward to see that the decryption correctness and the verification correctness of the above scheme follow from those of
\mathsf{1ABE}
.

Theorem 6.9.

Assuming
\mathsf{1ABE}
is
1
-bounded distinguishing key
Ada-IND-KLA
(resp.,
Sel-IND-KLA
) secure,
\mathsf{qABE}
is
q
-bounded distinguishing key
Ada-IND-KLA
(resp.,
Sel-IND-KLA
) secure if we set the parameters as follows:

•

For the adaptive case, we assume that the size of the ciphertext attribute space
|\mathcal{X}_{\lambda}|
is bounded by
2^{n(\lambda)}
for some polynomial function
n(\lambda)
.
We then set
v=2(\lambda+n)
and
w=q^{2}
.

•

For the selective case, we set
v=\lambda
and
w=q^{2}
.

Proof of
Theorem

6.9
.

Here, we first focus on the proof for the case of
q
-bounded distinguishing key
Ada-IND-KLA
and later mention the difference for the case of
q
-bounded distinguishing key
Sel-IND-KLA
.
We define a sequence of hybrid games.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{qABE},\mathpzc{A},q}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda},0)
. More specifically, it is as follows.

1.

The challenger generates
(\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,j},\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{msk}_{i,j})\leftarrow\mathsf{1ABE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})

for
i\in[v]
and
j\in[w]
and sends
\mathsf{pk}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,j}\}_{i,j}
to the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
.
The challenger then initializes the list
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
to be an empty set.

\mathpzc{A}
can access the following oracles.

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}(y^{(k)})
:

Given the
k
-th query
y^{(k)}
with
k\in[Q]
, if there is an entry of the form
(y^{(k)},\mathsf{vk},V)
, it outputs
\bot
. Otherwise, it chooses
j_{i}^{(k)}\leftarrow[w]
for
i\in[v]
and runs
(\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{vk}_{i}^{(k)},\mathsf{1abe}.\mathpzc{usk}_{i}^{(k)})\leftarrow\mathsf{1ABE}.\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{msk}_{i,j_{i}},y^{(k)})
for
i\in[v]
. It then returns
\mathpzc{usk}^{(k)}\coloneqq\{j_{i}^{(k)},\mathsf{1abe}.\mathpzc{usk}_{i}^{(k)}\}_{i\in[v]}
and
\mathsf{vk}^{(k)}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{vk}_{i}^{(k)}\}_{i\in[v]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
and adds
(y^{(k)},\mathsf{vk}^{(k)},\bot)
to
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
.

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}(y,\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime})
:

Given
(y,\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime})
, it finds an entry
(y,\mathsf{vk},V)
from
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
and parses
\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime}=\{j_{i},\mathpzc{usk}_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i}
. (If there is no such entry, it returns
\bot
.)
It then computes
d_{i}\coloneqq\mathsf{1ABE}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime}_{i})
for
i\in[v]
and checks if
d_{i}=\top
for all
i\in[v]
.
If so, it returns
d\coloneqq\top
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
Otherwise, it returns
d\coloneqq\bot
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
It finally updates the entry into
(y,\mathsf{vk},d)
if
V=\bot
.

2.

When
\mathpzc{A}
sends
(x^{*},m_{0},m_{1})
to the challenger, the challenger computes the set
K_{x^{*}}\coloneqq\{k\in[Q_{1}]:R(x^{*},y^{(k)})=1\}
, where
Q_{1}\leq Q
is the number of key queries made by
\mathpzc{A}
so far.
If we have
V=\top
for all entries of the form
(y^{(k)},\mathsf{vk},V)
in
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
with
k\in K_{x^{*}}
and
|K_{x^{*}}|\leq q
,
the challenger chooses
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{v-1}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, sets
\mu_{v}\coloneqq(\oplus_{i\in[v-1]}\mu_{i})\oplus m_{0}
, and computes
\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,j}\leftarrow\mathsf{1ABE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,j},x^{*},\mu_{i})
for
i\in[v]
and
j\in[w]
.
It then sends
\mathsf{ct}^{*}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,j}\}_{i,j}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
Otherwise (i.e., if
|K_{x^{*}}|>q
or if there is an entry of the form
(y^{(k)},\mathsf{vk},\bot)
for some
k\in K_{x^{*}}
), it aborts the game and outputs
0
.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
continues to make queries to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}(\cdot)
and
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}(\cdot,\cdot)
.
However,
\mathpzc{A}
is not allowed to send a key attribute
y
such that
R(x^{*},y)=1
to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs a guess
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
for
\mathsf{coin}
. The challenger outputs
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
as the final output of the experiment.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
:

This game is the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
except for the way
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
is generated.
In particular, when
\mathpzc{A}
submits
(x^{*},m_{0},m_{1})
, the challenger aborts the game and outputs
0
as the outcome of the game if there is no
i^{*}
such that
\{j_{i^{*}}^{(k)}\}_{k\in K_{x^{*}}}
are all distinct.
Otherwise, the challenger continues the game as specified in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
.

We observe that unless there is no such
i^{*}
, the game is the same as the previous one.
We bound the probability of this occuring.
Let us first consider the case where
\mathpzc{A}
fixes its target
x^{*}
at the beginning of the game (i.e., selective security setting).
In this case, by simple probability calculation, we can show that the probability that
i^{*}
does not exist is exponentially small in the parameter
v
.
However, in the adaptive case, the adversary can adaptively choose
x^{*}
dependent on the values of
\{j_{i}^{(k)}\}_{i\in[v],k\in[Q]}
and the proof for the selective case no longer works. To deal with the added flexibility given to the adversary, we use the
union bound over all
x\in\mathcal{X}
and then use the above bound for each fixed
x
. This requires the parameter
v
to grow dependent on the size of
\log|\mathcal{X}_{\lambda}|
so that the sum of the probabilities is still small enough even after taking the union bound.
Based on the above discussion, we can prove
\absolutevalue{ \Pr[\hybi{0}= 1] - \Pr[\hybi{1}= 1] }={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.
We refer to
Lemma

6.10
for the detail.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
:

This game is the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
except that the challenger chooses random
\tilde{i}\leftarrow[v]
at the beginning of the game. Then, right before the challenger computes
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
, it checks whether
\tilde{i}=i^{*}
, where
i^{*}
is the smallest index such that
\{j_{i^{*}}^{(k)}\}_{k\in K_{x^{*}}}
are all distinct.

21
21

21

Note that
i^{*}
is not defined until
\mathpzc{A}
chooses
x^{*}
.

If so, the challenger continues the game until
\mathpzc{A}
outputs its guess.
Otherwise, it aborts the game and outputs
0
as the outcome of the game.

Since the choice of
\tilde{i}
is independent from the view of
\mathpzc{A}
and the outcome of the game is
1
only when
\tilde{i}=i^{*}
,
we can easily see that
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]/v
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
except for how
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{v}
are generated.
In particular,
\mathpzc{A}
first chooses
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{v}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and discards
\mu_{i^{*}}
.
It then sets
\mu_{i^{*}}\coloneqq(\oplus_{i\in[v]\backslash\{i^{*}\}})\oplus m_{0}
.
It can be easily seen that the distribution of
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{v}
is unchanged from the previous game and thus we have
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
except that
\mu_{i^{*}}
is set as
\mu_{i^{*}}\coloneqq(\oplus_{i\in[v]\backslash\{i^{*}\}}\mu_{i})\oplus m_{1}
.

We claim that this change is not noticed by
\mathpzc{A}
by the security of the underlying
\mathsf{1ABE}
.
To show this, we first observe that the game differs from the previous one only in how
\{\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i^{*},j}\}_{j\in[w]}
are generated.
We then change each plaintext encrypted in
\{\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i^{*},j}\}_{j}
one by one by using the security of the underlying
\mathsf{1ABE}
.
This is possible since for each
\mathsf{1ABE}
instance with index
(i^{*},j)
,

\mathpzc{A}
is given only at most one distinguishing key by the change we introduced in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
and thus we can use the security of
\mathsf{1ABE}
for such instances.
We therefore have
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hybi{3}= 1] - \Pr[\hybi{4}= 1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.
We refer to
Lemma

6.11
for the detail.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{qABE},\mathpzc{A},q}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda},1)
.

From the above discussion, we have

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{ v \Pr[\hybi{3}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{0}=1]}=\absolutevalue{ \Pr[\hybi{1}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{0}=1]
}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(125)

We then observe that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
(resp.,
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
) is the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
(resp.,
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
) except that
m_{1}
is used for the encryption instead of
m_{0}
. Therefore, we obtain

\absolutevalue{ v \Pr[\hybi{4}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{5}=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)

analogously to Eq. (
125
) by considering similar sequence of games with
m_{0}
being replaced by
m_{1}
in a reverse order.
We therefore have

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\expc{\qABE,\qA,q}{ada}{ind}{kla}(1^\secp,0)-\expc{\qABE,\qA, q}{ada}{ind}{kla}(1^\secp,1)}

\displaystyle=\absolutevalue{ \Pr[\hybi{0}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{5}=1]}

\displaystyle\leq\absolutevalue{ \Pr[\hybi{0}=1] - v \Pr[\hybi{3}=1]}+v\cdot\absolutevalue{ \Pr[\hybi{3}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{4}=1]}+\absolutevalue{ \Pr[\hybi{5}=1] - v \Pr[\hybi{4}=1]}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)

as desired.
It remains to prove
Lemmas

6.10
and
6.11
.

Lemma 6.10.

\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hybi{0}= 1] - \Pr[\hybi{1}= 1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
holds both for selective and adaptive settings.

Proof.

We first show the statement for the selective case.
The proof for this case is the same as
[
ISV+17
, Lemma 1]
, but we provide the proof here for completeness.
In the selective case, the probability that
\{j_{i}^{(k)}\}_{k\in K_{x^{*}}}
are not all distinct for some fixed
i
is

1-\frac{w(w-1)\cdots(w-q+1)}{w^{q}}\leq 1-\left(1-\frac{q-1}{w}\right)^{q}.

(126)

Therefore, the probability that there is no
i^{*}
satisfying the requirement is at most

\left(1-\left(1-\frac{q-1}{w}\right)^{q}\right)^{v}

(127)

which is negligible when
v=\lambda
and
w=q^{2}
since

\left(1-\left(1-\frac{q-1}{w}\right)^{q}\right)^{v}\leq\left(1-e^{-1}\right)^{\lambda}=2^{-O(\lambda)}.

(128)

We then consider the adaptive case.
We have

\displaystyle\Pr[\mbox{$\{j_{i}^{(k)}\}_{k\in K_{x^{*}}}$ are not all distinct}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{\lambda}}\Pr[x^{*}=x\land\mbox{$\{j_{i}^{(k)}\}_{k\in K_{x}}$ are not all distinct}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{\lambda}}\Pr[\mbox{$\{j_{i}^{(k)}\}_{k\in K_{x}}$ are not all distinct}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{\lambda}}\left(1-\left(1-\frac{q-1}{w}\right)^{q}\right)^{v}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\mathcal{X}_{\lambda}|\left(1-e^{-1}\right)^{v}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{-\lambda},

where the probabilities are taken over all randomness used in the game.
In the above, third line follows from the same analysis as the selective case and the forth and the fifth lines follow from our parameter setting.
∎

Lemma 6.11.

If
\mathsf{1ABE}
is
1
-bounded distinguishing key
Ada-IND-KLA
,
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hybi{3}= 1] - \Pr[\hybi{4}= 1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

Proof.

This can be reduced to the
1
-bounded distinguishing key
Ada-IND-KLA
security of
\mathsf{1ABE}
by a standard hybrid argument, where we modify the plaintext encrypted in
\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i^{*},j}
for each
j\in[w]
one by one.
More precisely, the reduction works as follows.

We define additional hybrids
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3.k}
for
k\in[w]
as follows.
In the following, let
\xi_{b}\coloneqq(\oplus_{i\in[v]\backslash\{i^{*}\}}\mu_{i})\oplus m_{b}
for
b\in\{0,1\}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3.\tau}
:

This is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
except that
\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i^{*},j}
is generated as

\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i^{*},j}\leftarrow\begin{cases}\mathsf{1ABE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i^{*},j},\xi_{1})&j<\tau\\
\mathsf{1ABE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i^{*},j},\xi_{0})&j\geq\tau\end{cases}

(129)

for
j\in[\lambda]
.

Clearly, we have
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=\mathsf{Hyb}_{3.1}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=\mathsf{Hyb}_{3.w+1}
. Thus, it suffices to prove that

\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3.\tau+1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3.\tau}=1]\right|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)

for all
\tau\in[w]
.
Remark that the only difference between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3.\tau+1}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3.\tau}
is the way of generating
\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i^{*},\tau}
.
To show that
\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3.\tau+1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3.\tau}=1]\right|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
,
we construct
\mathpzc{B}
against the security of
\mathsf{1ABE}
as follows.

\mathpzc{B}(\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{pk})
:

It works as follows.

1.

It first chooses random
\tilde{i}\leftarrow[v]
.

2.

The challenger generates
(\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,j},\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{msk}_{i,j})\leftarrow\mathsf{1ABE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})

for
(i,j)\in([v]\times[w])\backslash\{(\tilde{i},k)\}
.
It then sets
\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{\tilde{i},k}\coloneqq\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{pk}

and sends
\mathsf{pk}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,j}\}_{i,j}
to the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
.
It then initializes the list
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
to be an empty set.

\mathpzc{B}
then simulates the following oracles for
\mathpzc{A}
.

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}(y^{(k)})
:

Given the
k
-th query
y^{(k)}
with
k\in[Q]
from
\mathpzc{A}
,
\mathpzc{B}
returns
\bot
to
\mathpzc{A}
if there is an entry of the form
(y^{(k)},\mathsf{vk},V)
. Otherwise, it chooses
j_{i}^{(k)}\leftarrow[w]
for
i\in[v]
and runs
(\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{vk}_{i}^{(k)},\mathsf{1abe}.\mathpzc{usk}_{i}^{(k)})\leftarrow\mathsf{1ABE}.\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{1ABE}.\mathsf{msk}_{i,j_{i}},y^{(k)})
for
i\in[v]\backslash\{\tilde{i}\}
.
If
j_{\tilde{i}}^{(k)}=\tau
,
it sends
y^{(k)}
to its key generation oracle and is given

\mathsf{1abe}.\mathpzc{usk}\leftarrow\mathsf{1ABE}.\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{msk},y^{(k)}).

(130)

Then, it sets
\mathsf{1abe}.\mathpzc{usk}^{(k)}_{\tilde{i}}\coloneqq\mathsf{1abe}.\mathpzc{usk}
.
Otherwise (i.e., if
j_{\tilde{i}}^{(k)}\neq\tau
), it runs

(\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{vk}_{\tilde{i}}^{(k)},\mathsf{1abe}.\mathpzc{usk}_{\tilde{i}}^{(k)})\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{1ABE}.\mathsf{msk}_{{\tilde{i}},j_{\tilde{i}}},y^{(k)})
by itself.
Finally,
\mathpzc{B}
returns
\mathpzc{usk}^{(k)}\coloneqq\{j_{i}^{(k)},\mathsf{1abe}.\mathpzc{usk}_{i}^{(k)}\}_{i\in[v]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
and adds
(y^{(k)},\mathsf{vk}^{(k)},\bot)
to
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
, where
\mathsf{vk}^{(k)}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{vk}_{i}^{(k)}\}_{i\in[v]}
.

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}(y,\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime})
:

Given
(y,\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime})
, it finds an entry
(y,\mathsf{vk},V)
from
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
and parses
\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime}=\{j_{i},\mathpzc{usk}_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i}
. (If there is no such entry, it returns
\bot
.)
It then computes
d_{i}\coloneqq\mathsf{1ABE}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime}_{i})
for
i\in[v]
.
If
j_{\tilde{i}}=\tau
,
\mathpzc{B}
makes a query to its own verification oracle to obtain

d_{\tilde{i}}\coloneqq\mathsf{1ABE}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime}_{\tilde{i}}).

(131)

Otherwise,
\mathpzc{B}
runs
d_{\tilde{i}}\coloneqq\mathsf{1ABE}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{vk}_{\tilde{i},\tau},\mathpzc{usk}^{\prime}_{\tilde{i}})
by itself.
Finally, it checks if
d_{i}=\top
for all
i\in[v]
.
If so, it returns
d\coloneqq\top
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
Otherwise, it returns
d\coloneqq\bot
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
It finally updates the entry into
(y,\mathsf{vk},d)
if
V=\bot
.

3.

When
\mathpzc{A}
sends
(x^{*},m_{0},m_{1})
to the challenger,

\mathpzc{B}
aborts and outputs
0

if either
|K_{x^{*}}|>q
or there is an entry of the form
(y^{(k)},\mathsf{vk},\bot)
for some
k\in K_{x^{*}}
.
It also aborts and outputs
0
if
i^{*}\neq\tilde{i}
