, which includes the case that there is no
i^{*}
satisfying the properties we defined in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
.
Otherwise, it chooses
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{v}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and sets

\xi_{0}\coloneqq(\oplus_{i\in[v]\backslash\{i^{*}\}}\mu_{i})\oplus m_{0}
and
\xi_{1}\coloneqq(\oplus_{i\in[v]\backslash\{i^{*}\}}\mu_{i})\oplus m_{1}
.
It then computes
\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,j}\leftarrow\mathsf{1ABE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{pk}_{i,j},x^{*},\mu_{i})
for
i\in[v]\backslash\{i^{*}\}
and
j\in[w]

and

\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i^{*},j}
for
j\in[w]\backslash\{\tau\}
as

Equation

129
.
It then submits
(\xi_{0},\xi_{1})
to its challenger.
Then,

\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{pk},\xi_{\overline{\mathsf{coin}}})

(132)

is run and
\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{ct}
is returned to
\mathpzc{B}
, where
\overline{\mathsf{coin}}
is the random bit chosen by
\mathpzc{B}
’s challenger.
Finally,
\mathpzc{B}
sets
\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i^{*},\tau}\coloneqq\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{ct}

and sends
\mathsf{ct}^{*}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{1abe}.\mathsf{ct}_{i,j}\}_{i,j}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
continues to make queries to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}(\cdot)
and
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}(\cdot,\cdot)
.
However,
\mathpzc{A}
is not allowed to send a key attribute
y
such that
R(x^{*},y)=1
to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
.

5.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs a guess
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
for
\mathsf{coin}
. The challenger outputs
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
as the final output of the experiment.

We first argue that
\mathpzc{B}
does not make more than two distinguishing key queries.
This is because
\mathpzc{B}
aborts and outputs
0
before it makes a challenge query if there is no
i^{*}
with the required conditions.
For such
i^{*}
, we have that
\{j^{(k)}_{i^{*}}\}_{k\in K_{x^{*}}}
are all distinct and thus in particular,
\mathpzc{B}
needs to simulate only single distinguishing key for the
(i^{*},\tau)
-th instance, to which the reduction algorithm embeds the
\mathsf{1ABE}
instance.

We then have

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{1ABE},\mathpzc{B},1}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\left|\Pr[\mbox{ $\mathpzc{B}$ outputs $1$ }|\overline{\mathsf{coin}}=0]-\Pr[\mbox{ $\mathpzc{B}$ outputs $1$ }|\overline{\mathsf{coin}}=1]\right|

(133)

\displaystyle=\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=1]\right|

(134)

where the probabilities are taken over the randomness used in the respective games. Thus,
\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=1]\right|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
by the adaptive security of
\mathsf{1ABE}
.
This completes the proof of
Lemma

6.11
.

∎

This completes the proof of
Theorem

6.9
for the case of adaptive security.

The proof for selective security.

The proof for selective security can be obtained immediately by considering the same sequence of games as adaptive security case with natural adaptations.
There are two main differences.
The proof for
Lemma

6.10
requires different parameters for selective and adaptive cases. We refer to the proof of the lemma for the detail.
Another difference is that we modify the reduction algorithm in
Lemma

6.11
so that it outputs
x^{*}
at the beginning of the game right after given
x^{*}
from
\mathpzc{A}
.
∎

6.4
Instantiations

Here, we explain new schemes that can be obtained by applying the conversions that we showed in
Sections

6.2
and
6.3
to existing IBE/ABE schemes.
Our constructions are fully generic and can upgrade almost all ABE schemes
22
22

22

Our conversion in
Section

6.3
for the adaptive security case poses the restriction that the size of the cipheretxt attribute space of the ABE should be bounded by
2^{{\mathrm{poly}}(\lambda)}
for some polynomial
{\mathrm{poly}}(\lambda)
.
This means that we cannot apply the conversion for adaptively secure ABE for DFA for example,
since the ciphertext attribute is of unbounded length and there is no such bound for the size of the ciphertext attribute space.
However, we do not know any concrete ABE scheme from standard assumptions for which we cannot apply our conversion.

into the one with the security against key leasing attacks with the help of IND-KLA secure PKE-SKL scheme, which can be instantiated from any (post quantum) PKE.
Here, we mention some instantiations, all of which are obtained from the standard LWE assumption.

•

If we start from selectively secure ABE scheme for circuits
[
GVW13
,
BGG+14
]
and apply the conversions in
Sections

6.2
and
6.3
,
we obtain an ABE-SKL scheme for circuits with
q
-bounded distinguishing key
Sel-IND-KLA
security for any
q={\mathrm{poly}}(\lambda)
.

•

If we start from adaptively secure ABE for inner products over the integer
[
KNYY20
]
and apply the conversions in
Sections

6.2
and
6.3
, we obtain an ABE-SKL scheme for the same predicate with
q
-bounded distinguishing key
Ada-IND-KLA
security for any
q={\mathrm{poly}}(\lambda)
.
We note that the conversion in
Section

6.3
for adaptive security case can be applied for the scheme, since the size of the ciphertext attribute space is bounded by
2^{{\mathrm{poly}}(\lambda)}
for the primitive.
Similar implications can be obtained for adaptively secure
t
-CNF formulae for
t=O(1)

[
Tsa19
]
and fuzzy IBE for small universe
[
KNYY20
]
.

•

If we start from adaptively (resp., selectively) secure IBE
[
ABB10
,
CHKP10
]
and apply the conversion in
Section

6.2
, we obtain IBE-SKL scheme with
1
-bounded distinguishing key
Ada-IND-KLA
(resp.,
Sel-IND-KLA
) security.
We note that
1
-bounded distinguishing key security for the case of IBE is a more natural security notion than that for the case of ABE with other relations since there is only one attribute that is eligible for decrypting a ciphertext in the case of IBE (i.e., the identity that is associated with the ciphertext), whereas there can be exponentially many such attributes in general.

7
Public-Key Functional Encryption with Secure Key Leasing

7.1
Definitions

Definition 7.1 (PKFE with Secure Key Leasing).

A PKFE-SKL scheme
\mathsf{PKFE\textrm{-}SKL}
is a tuple of six algorithms
(\mathsf{Setup},\mathpzc{KG},\mathsf{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Cert},\mathsf{Vrfy})
.
Below, let
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{Y}
, and
\mathcal{F}
be the plaintext, output, and function spaces of
\mathsf{PKFE\textrm{-}SKL}
, respectively.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{msk})
:

The setup algorithm takes a security parameter
1^{\lambda}
, and outputs a public key
\mathsf{pk}
and master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
.

\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},f)\rightarrow(\mathpzc{fsk},\mathsf{vk})
:

The key generation algorithm takes a master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
and a function
f\in\mathcal{F}
, and outputs a functional decryption key
\mathpzc{fsk}
and a verification key
\mathsf{vk}
.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},x)\rightarrow\mathsf{ct}
:

The encryption algorithm takes a public key
\mathsf{pk}
and a plaintext
x\in\mathcal{X}
, and outputs a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
.

\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathpzc{fsk},\mathsf{ct})\rightarrow\widetilde{x}
:

The decryption algorithm takes a functional decryption key
\mathpzc{fsk}
and a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
, and outputs a value
\tilde{x}
.

\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{fsk}^{\prime})\rightarrow\top/\bot
:

The verification algorithm takes a verification key
\mathsf{vk}
and a quantum state
\mathpzc{fsk}^{\prime}
, and outputs
\top
or
\bot
.

Decryption correctness:

For every
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
f\in\mathcal{F}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathpzc{fsk},\mathsf{ct})\allowbreak=f(x)\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathpzc{fsk},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},f)\\
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},x)\end{array}\right]=1-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Verification correctness:

For every
f\in\mathcal{F}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{fsk})=\top\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathpzc{fsk},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},f)\\
\end{array}\right]=1-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Remark
7.2.

Although Kitagawa and Nishimaki
[
KN22a
]
require SKFE-SKL to have classical certificate generation algorithm for deletion, we do not since it is optional.
If there exists a PKE-SKL scheme that has a classical certificate generation algorithm, our PKFE-SKL scheme in
Section

7.2
also has a classical certificate generation algorithm.

Definition 7.3 (Adaptive Indistinguishability against Key Leasing Attacks).

We say that a PKFE-SKL scheme
\mathsf{PKFE\textrm{-}SKL}
for
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
, and
\mathcal{F}
is an adaptively indistinguishable secure against key leasing attacks (Ada-IND-KLA), if it satisfies the following requirement, formalized from the experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathpzc{A},\mathsf{PKFE\textrm{-}SKL}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})
between an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
and a challenger:

1.

At the beginning, the challenger runs
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.
Throughout the experiment,
\mathpzc{A}
can access the following oracles.

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}(f)
:

Given
f
, it finds an entry
(f,\mathsf{vk},V)
from
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
. If there is such an entry, it returns
\bot
. Otherwise, it generates
(\mathpzc{fsk},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},f)
, sends
\mathpzc{fsk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
, and adds
(f,\mathsf{vk},\bot)
to
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
.

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}(f,\mathpzc{fsk}^{\prime})
:

Given
(f,\mathpzc{fsk}^{\prime})
, it finds an entry
(f,\mathsf{vk},V)
from
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
. (If there is no such entry, it returns
\bot
.) It computes
d\leftarrow\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{fsk}^{\prime})
and sends
d
to
\mathpzc{A}
. If
V=\top
, it does not update
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
. Else if
V=\bot
, it updates the entry by setting
V\coloneqq d
.

2.

When
\mathpzc{A}
sends
(x_{0}^{*},x_{1}^{*})
to the challenger, the challenger checks if for any entry
(f,\mathsf{vk},V)
in
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
such that
f(x_{0}^{*})\neq f(x_{1}^{*})
, it holds that
V=\top
. If so, the challenger generates
\mathsf{ct}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},x_{\mathsf{coin}}^{*})
and sends
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
to
\mathpzc{A}
. Otherwise, the challenger outputs
0
. Hereafter,
\mathpzc{A}
is not allowed to send a function
f
such that
f(x_{0}^{*})\neq f(x_{1}^{*})
to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs a guess
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
for
\mathsf{coin}
. The challenger outputs
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
as the final output of the experiment.

For any QPT
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{PKFE\textrm{-}SKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[\expc{\PKFESKL,\qA}{ada}{ind}{kla} (1^\secp,0) \ra 1] - \Pr[\expc{\PKFESKL,\qA}{ada}{ind}{kla} (1^\secp,1) \ra 1] }\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(140)

Remark
7.4.

Definition

7.3
assumes that the adversary does not get more than one decryption key for the same
f
for simplification as
Remark

6.3
.

7.2
Constructions

We describe our PKFE-SKL scheme in this section.
We construct a PKFE-SKL scheme
\mathsf{PKFE\textrm{-}SKL}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathpzc{KG},\mathsf{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Vrfy})
by using the following building blocks.

•

IND-KLA secure PKE-SKL
\mathsf{SKL}=\mathsf{SKL}.(\mathpzc{KG},\mathsf{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Vrfy})
.

•

Adaptively secure PKFE
\mathsf{FE}=\mathsf{FE}.(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KG},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
.

•

Adaptively single-ciphertext function private SKFE
\mathsf{SKFE}=\mathsf{SKFE}.(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KG},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
.

•

Pseudorandom-secure SKE
\mathsf{SKE}=\mathsf{SKE}.(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
.

•

Puncturable PRF
\mathsf{PRF}=(\mathsf{PRF}.\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{F},\mathsf{Puncture})
.

We set
\ell_{\mathsf{pad}}\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\skfe.\ct}-\absolutevalue{x}
and
\ell_{\mathsf{ske}}\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\ske.\ct}
, where
\absolutevalue{x}
is the input length of
\mathsf{PKFE\textrm{-}SKL}
,
\absolutevalue{\skfe.\ct}
is the ciphertext length of
\mathsf{SKFE}
, and
\absolutevalue{\ske.\ct}
is the ciphertext length of
\mathsf{SKE}
.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
:

•

Generate
(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

•

Output
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{msk})\coloneqq(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{msk})
.

\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},f)
:

•

Generate
(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})
.

•

Choose
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\mathsf{ske}}}
.

•

Construct a circuit
W[f,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}]
, which is described in
Figure

1
.

•

Generate
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{W}\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{msk},W[f,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}])
.

•

Output
\mathpzc{fsk}\coloneqq(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{W},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk})
and
\mathsf{vk}\coloneqq\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}
.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},x)
:

•

Choose
\mathsf{K}\leftarrow\mathsf{PRF}.\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
.

•

Compute
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{pk},(x\|0^{\ell_{\mathsf{pad}}},\bot,\mathsf{K}))
.

•

Output
\mathsf{ct}\coloneqq\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}
.

\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathpzc{fsk},\mathsf{ct})
:

•

Parse
\mathpzc{fsk}=(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk})
and
\mathsf{ct}=\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}
.

•

Compute
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct})
.

•

Compute and output
y\leftarrow\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct})
.

\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{fsk}^{\prime})
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{vk}=\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}
and
\mathpzc{fsk}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}^{\prime},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}^{\prime})
.

•

Compute and output
\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}^{\prime})
.

Function
W[f,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}](x^{\prime},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{K})

Constants:

Function
f
, PKE-SKL encryption key
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}
, SKE ciphertext
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}
.

Input:

Plaintext
x^{\prime}
, SKE key
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}
, PRF key
\mathsf{K}
.

1.

If
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}=\bot
, do the following:

•

Parse
x^{\prime}=x\|\overline{x}
such that
\absolutevalue{\barx}=\ell_{\mathsf{pad}}
.

•

Compute and output
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}\coloneqq\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek},f(x);\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}))
.

2.

If
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}\neq\bot
, do the following:

•

Compute
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct})
.

•

Compute and output
z\coloneqq\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk},x^{\prime})
.

Figure 1:
The description of
W[f,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}]

Correctness.

The decryption correctness of
\mathsf{PKFE\textrm{-}SKL}
follows from the correctness of
\mathsf{FE}
and the decryption correctness of
\mathsf{SKL}
.
The verification correcntess of
\mathsf{PKFE\textrm{-}SKL}
follows from the verification correcntess of
\mathsf{SKL}
.

7.3
Security Proofs

We prove the security of
\mathsf{PKFE\textrm{-}SKL}
.

Theorem 7.5.

If
\mathsf{PKFE}
is adaptively secure,
\mathsf{SKFE}
is adaptively single-ciphertext function private,
\mathsf{PRF}
is a secure punctured PRF, and
\mathsf{SKE}
has the ciphertext pseudorandomness, then
\mathsf{PKFE\textrm{-}SKL}
above is Ada-IND-KLA.

Theorem 7.6.

If
\mathsf{PKFE}
is
q
-bounded adaptively secure,
\mathsf{SKFE}
is adaptively single-ciphertext function private,
\mathsf{PRF}
is a secure punctured PRF, and
\mathsf{SKE}
has the ciphertext pseudorandomness, then
\mathsf{PKFE\textrm{-}SKL}
above is
q
-bounded Ada-IND-KLA.

The proof of
Theorem

7.6
is almost the same as that of
Theorem

7.5
. Hence, we focus on the proof of
Theorem

7.5
.
We can also consider a simulation-based security for
q
-bounded security as Kitagawa and Nishimaki
[
KN22a
]
and believe that we can achieve it using a similar technique. However, it is out of scope of this work.

Proof of
Theorem

7.5
.

In the proof, we embed an SKFE ciphertext
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},(x,\bot,\mathsf{K},0,\bot))
into the challenge ciphertext.
More specifically, we generate
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{PKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{pk},(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk},\bot))
and
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},T[f,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}]))
, where
T[f,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}]
is described in
Figure

2
.
By using this embedding, we can use the function privacy of
\mathsf{SKFE}
and can alter both plaintexts and functions in the proof.

Function
T[f,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}](x,x_{1},\mathsf{K},j,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast})

Constants:

Function
f
, encryption key of PKE-SKL
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}
.

Input:

Plaintext
x
and
x_{1}
, PRF key
\mathsf{K}
, index
j
, a PKE-SKL ciphertext
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
.
23
23

23

Although inputs
(x_{1},j,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast})
are not used in this function, we need them in hybrid games later.

1.

Compute and output
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}\coloneqq\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek},f(x);\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}))
.

Figure 2:
The description of
T[f,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}]

Let
q
be the total number of key queries to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
. In the collusion-resistant setting,
q
is an unbounded polynomial. Note that even if
q
is an unbounded polynomial, we need only
{\mathrm{poly}}(\lambda)
bits to describe
q
as an integer.
We assume that the adversary does not send the same
f
to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
more than once without loss of generality.
We define a sequence of hybrid games.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{PKFE\textrm{-}SKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda},0)
. More specifically, it is as follows.

1.

The challenger generates
(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and sends
\mathsf{pk}\coloneqq\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{pk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

\mathpzc{A}
can access the following oracles.

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}(f_{i})
:

Given
f_{i}
, it generates
(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})
,
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\mathsf{ske}}}
, and
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{W,i}\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{msk},W[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}])
, sends
\mathpzc{fsk}_{i}\coloneqq(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{W,i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{i})
to
\mathpzc{A}
, and adds
(f_{i},\mathsf{vk}_{i},\bot)
to
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
.

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}(f_{i},\mathpzc{fsk}^{\prime}_{i})
:

Given
(f_{i},\mathpzc{fsk}^{\prime}_{i})
, it finds an entry
(f_{i},\mathsf{vk}_{i},V_{i})
from
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
and parse
\mathpzc{fsk}^{\prime}_{i}=(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}^{\prime}_{i})
. (If there is no such entry, it returns
\bot
.) It returns
d\coloneqq\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}^{\prime})
. If
V_{i}=\top
, it does not update the entry. Otherwise, it updates the entry by setting
V_{i}\coloneqq d
.

2.

When
\mathpzc{A}
sends
(x_{0}^{*},x_{1}^{*})
to the challenger, the challenger checks if for any entry
(f,\mathsf{vk},V)
in
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
such that
f(x_{0}^{*})\neq f(x_{1}^{*})
, it holds that
V=\top
. If so, the challenger generates
\mathsf{K}\leftarrow\mathsf{PRF}.\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
and
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{pk},(x_{\mathsf{coin}}^{\ast}\|0^{\ell_{\mathsf{pad}}},\bot,\mathsf{K}))
and sends
\mathsf{ct}^{*}\coloneqq\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
to
\mathpzc{A}
. Otherwise, the challenger outputs
0
. Hereafter,
\mathpzc{A}
is not allowed to send a function
f
such that
f(x_{0}^{*})\neq f(x_{1}^{*})
to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs a guess
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
for
\mathsf{coin}
. The challenger outputs
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
as the final output of the experiment.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
except that for all
i\in[q]
, we generate
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i})
, where
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},T[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}])
and
(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
. Note that the SKE secret key
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}
never appears in the view of
\mathpzc{A}
. Hence, we obtain
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hybi{0}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{1} =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
by the security of
\mathsf{SKE}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
except that we generate
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{PKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{pk},(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk},\bot))
, where
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},(x_{0}^{\ast},\bot,\mathsf{K},0,\bot))
. By the definition of
W
described in
Figure

1
, if we decrypt
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
by
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}
, we obtain

•

\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},f(x_{0}^{\ast});\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}))
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
since the plaintext in
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
is
(x_{0}^{\ast}\|0^{\ell_{\mathsf{pad}}},\bot,\mathsf{K})
,

•

z_{i}=\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i},\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast})
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
since
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}
is a ciphertext of
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}
and the plaintext in
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
is
(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk},\bot)
, where
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}=\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},(x_{0}^{\ast},\bot,\mathsf{K},0,\bot))
. By the correctness of
\mathsf{SKFE}
,
z_{i}=\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},f(x_{0}^{\ast});\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}))
.

That is, for all
i\in[q]
, it holds that
W[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}](x_{0}^{\ast}\|0^{\ell_{\mathsf{pad}}},\bot,\mathsf{K})=W[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}](\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk},\bot)
. Hence, we can use the security of
\mathsf{PKFE}
and obtain
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hybi{1}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{2} =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
. See
Lemma

C.1
for the detail.

After this game, we can focus on
\mathsf{SKFE}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
except that we generate
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},(x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast},\mathsf{K},0,\bot))
and
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},T_{\mathsf{hyb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i])
, where
T_{\mathsf{hyb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i]
is described in
Figure

3
. Since
i\in[q]
, it holds that
T_{\mathsf{hyb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i](x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast},\mathsf{K},0,\bot)=T[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}](x_{0}^{\ast},\bot,\mathsf{K},0,\bot)
for all
i\in[q]
.
Hence, by the adaptively single-ciphertext function privacy of
\mathsf{SKFE}
, we obtain
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hybi{2}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{3} =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
. See
Lemma

C.2
for the detail.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}^{j}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
except that we generate
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},(x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast},\mathsf{K},j,\bot))
. Apparently,
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}^{0}
is the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
. We show it holds that
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hybij{3}{j-1}=1] - \Pr[\hybij{3}{j} =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
for
j\in[q]
in
Lemma

7.7
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}^{q}
except that we generate
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},T[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}])
and
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},(x_{1}^{\ast},\bot,\mathsf{K},0,\bot))
.
Recall that in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}^{q}
, we use
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},T_{\mathsf{hyb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i])
and
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},(x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast},\mathsf{K},q,\bot))
. By the definition of
T_{\mathsf{hyb}}
and
T
, it holds that for all
i\in[q]
,

\displaystyle T_{\mathsf{hyb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i](x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast},\mathsf{K},q,\bot)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},f_{i}(x_{1}^{\ast});\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}))

(141)

\displaystyle=T[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}](x_{1}^{\ast},\bot,\mathsf{K},0,\bot).

(142)

Hence, we can use the adaptively single-ciphertext function privacy of
\mathsf{SKFE}
and obtain
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hybij{3}{q}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{4} =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
. See
Lemma

C.3
for the detail.

Now, we use
x_{1}^{\ast}
instead of
x_{0}^{\ast}
and erased
x_{0}^{\ast}
in the challenge ciphertext. Hence, we focus on
\mathsf{PKFE}
again and undo the changes from
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
and from
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
except that we generate
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{PKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{pk},(x_{1}^{\ast}\|0^{\ell_{\mathsf{pad}}},\bot,\mathsf{K}))
. This is the reverse transition from
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
, so we obtain
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hybi{4}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{5} =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
by the security of PKFE as the proof of
Lemma

C.1
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
except that we generate
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
. As the transition from
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
, we obtain
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hybi{5}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{6} =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
by the ciphertext pseudorandomness of SKE.
It is easy to see that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
is the same as
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{PKFE\textrm{-}SKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(1^{\lambda},1)
.

Function
T_{\mathsf{hyb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i](x_{0},x_{1},\mathsf{K},j,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast})

Constants:

Function
f_{i}
, PKE-SKL encryption key
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}
, index
i
.

Input:

Plaintext
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
, PRF key
\mathsf{K}
, index
j
, a PKE-SKL ciphertext
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
.

1.

If
i\leq j
, compute and output
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}\coloneqq\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},f_{i}(x_{1});\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}))
.

2.

If
i>j
, compute and output
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}\coloneqq\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},f_{i}(x_{0});\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}))
.

Figure 3:
The description of
T_{\mathsf{hyb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i]

If we prove
Lemma

7.7
, we complete the proof of
Theorem

7.5
.
∎

Lemma 7.7.

For all
j\in[q]
, it holds that
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hybij{3}{j-1}=1] - \Pr[\hybij{3}{j} =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
if
\mathsf{SKFE}
is fully function private,
\mathsf{SKL}
is IND-KLA, and
\mathsf{PRF}
is a puncturable PRF.

Proof.

We define a sequence of hybrid games.

\mathsf{G}_{0}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}^{j-1}
. That is,
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},(x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast},\mathsf{K},j-1,\bot))
and
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},T_{\mathsf{hyb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i])
.

\mathsf{G}_{1}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{G}_{0}
except that we generate
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},(x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast},\mathsf{K},j,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}))
, where
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{j},f_{j}(x_{0}^{\ast});\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{j}))
and
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},T_{\mathsf{emb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i])
, where
T_{\mathsf{emb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i]
is described in
Figure

4
.
By the definitions of
T_{\mathsf{hyb}}
and
T_{\mathsf{emb}}
, it holds that

T_{\mathsf{hyb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i](x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast},\mathsf{K},j-1,\bot)=T_{\mathsf{emb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i](x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast},\mathsf{K},j,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast})

(143)

for all
i\in[q]
since
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
is an encryption of
f_{j}(x_{0}^{\ast})
.
Hence, by the adaptively single-ciphertext function privacy of
\mathsf{SKFE}
, we obtain
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\sfG_0 =1] - \Pr[\sfG_1 =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
. See
Lemma

C.4
for the detail.

\mathsf{G}_{2}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{G}_{1}
except that we use a punctured PRF key
\mathsf{K}_{\neq\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{j}}=\mathsf{Puncture}(\mathsf{K},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{j})
. By the functionality of punctured PRF keys, it holds that

T_{\mathsf{emb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i](x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast},\mathsf{K},j,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast})=T_{\mathsf{emb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i](x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast},\mathsf{K}_{\neq\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{j}},j,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast})

(144)

for all
i\in[q]
. Note that
T_{\mathsf{emb}}
directly uses
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
instead of computing
\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{j},f_{j}(x_{0}^{\ast});\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{j}))
, so
\mathsf{K}_{\neq\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{j}}
is sufficient for the functional equivalence. The only difference between the two games is whether the PRF key is
\mathsf{K}
or
\mathsf{K}_{\neq\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{j}}
. Hence, we can use the adaptively single-ciphertext function privacy of
\mathsf{SKFE}
and obtain
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\sfG_1 =1] - \Pr[\sfG_2 =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
. We omit the proof since it is easy.

\mathsf{G}_{3}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{G}_{2}
except that we generate
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{j},f_{j}(x_{0}^{\ast}))
. That is, we use uniform randomness for generating
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
. By the punctured pseudorandomness of
\mathsf{PRF}
, we obtain
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\sfG_2 =1] - \Pr[\sfG_3 =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
. We omit the proof since it is easy.

\mathsf{G}_{4}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{G}_{3}
except that we generate
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{j},f_{j}(x_{1}^{\ast}))
.
We consider two cases.

•

If
(f_{j},\mathsf{vk}_{j},\bot)
is recorded in
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
, that is, valid
\mathpzc{fsk}_{j}
is not returned, it must hold that
f_{j}(x_{0}^{\ast})=f_{j}(x_{1}^{\ast})
by the requirement of Ada-IND-KLA security. In this case, the distribution of
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{j},f_{j}(x_{0}^{\ast}))
is trivially the same as that of
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{j},f_{j}(x_{1}^{\ast}))
. Hence, we obtain
\Pr[\mathsf{G}_{3}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{G}_{4}=1]
.

•

If
(f_{j},\mathsf{vk}_{j},\top)
is recorded in
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
, that is, it is certified that the adversary returned valid
\mathpzc{fsk}_{j}
, it could hold that
f_{j}(x_{0}^{\ast})\neq f_{j}(x_{1}^{\ast})
by the requirement of Ada-IND-KLA security. In this case, we use IND-KLA security of
\mathsf{SKL}
since
\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{j}
was returned. We have that
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{j},f_{j}(x_{0}^{\ast}))
is computationally indistinguishable from
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{j},f_{j}(x_{1}^{\ast}))
. Hence, we obtain
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\sfG_3 =1] - \Pr[\sfG_4 =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
in this case. See
Lemma

C.5
for the detail.

Hence, we obtain
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\sfG_3 =1] - \Pr[\sfG_4 =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
in either cases.

\mathsf{G}_{5}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{G}_{4}
except that we undo the change in
\mathsf{G}_{3}
. That is, we use
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{j})
for the randomness of
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
. We obtain
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\sfG_4 =1] - \Pr[\sfG_5 =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
by the punctured pseudorandomness of
\mathsf{PRF}
. We omit the proof since it is easy.

\mathsf{G}_{6}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{G}_{5}
except that we undo the change in
\mathsf{G}_{2}
. That is, we use a unpunctured PRF key
\mathsf{K}
. We obtain
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\sfG_1 =1] - \Pr[\sfG_2 =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
by the adaptively single-ciphertext function privacy as the transition from
\mathsf{G}_{1}
to
\mathsf{G}_{2}
. So, we omit the proof.

\mathsf{G}_{7}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{G}_{6}
except that we undo the change in
\mathsf{G}_{1}
, but the index is still
j
. That is, we use
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},(x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast},\mathsf{K},j,\bot))
and
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},T_{\mathsf{hyb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i])
. We obtain
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\sfG_6 =1] - \Pr[\sfG_7 =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
by the adaptively single-ciphertext function privacy of
\mathsf{SKFE}
. The proof is similar to that of
Lemma

C.4
. So, we omit the proof.

Function
T_{\mathsf{emb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i](x_{0},x_{1},\mathsf{K},j,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast})

Constants:

Function
f_{i}
, encryption key of PKE-SKL
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}
, index
i
.

Input:

Plaintext
x_{0}
,
x_{1}
, PRF key
\mathsf{K}
, index
j
, an SKL ciphertext
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
.

1.

If
i=j
, output
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
.

2.

If
i<j
, compute and output
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}\coloneqq\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},f_{i}(x_{1});\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}))
.

3.

If
i>j
, compute and output
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}\coloneqq\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},f_{i}(x_{0});\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}))
.

Figure 4:
The description of
T_{\mathsf{emb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i]

It is easy to see that
\mathsf{G}_{7}
is the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}^{j}
. Therefore, we complete the proof.
∎

By

Corollary 7.8.

If there exists IND-CPA secure PKE, there exists

Corollary 7.9.

If there exists single-key selective-message-function secure

The adversary must select the target plaintext pair and function at the beginning of the game. This is the same as weakly selective security by Garg and Srinivasan

and weakly compact PKFE for

Appendix A
SDE Implies PKE-SKL

In this section, we discuss the relationship between SDE and PKE-SKL.
There are many incomparable security definitions for SDE in the literature.
Coladangelo et al.
[
CLLZ21
]
defined two incomparable security definitions called
CPA-style anti-piracy
and
random challenge anti-piracy
.
25
25

25

They actually also defined stronger variants of them called strong anti-piracy security and strong anti-piracy against random plaintexts. See
[
CLLZ21
, Definition 6.11 and D.4 in the full version]
for the detail.
All constructions of SDE in
[
CLLZ21
]
are shown to satisfy both CPA-style anti-piracy and random challenge anti-piracy.
Georgiou and Zhandry
[
GZ20
]
defined yet another security definition, which is similar to but slightly different from CPA-style anti-piracy of
[
CLLZ21
]
.
26
26

26

We note that
[
GZ20
]
appeared before
[
CLLZ21
]
.
Though we do not see any relationships between security notions in
[
GZ20
]
and
[
CLLZ21
]
, it seems possible to prove that the construction given in
[
GZ20
]
satisfies both CPA-style anti-piracy and random challenge anti-piracy of
[
CLLZ21
]
because it is very similar to one of the schemes given in
[
CLLZ21
]
.
27
27

27

Here, we are referring to the construction of SDE based on one-shot signatures and extractable witness encryption in
[
GZ20
, Section 5]
.
For proving that the scheme satisfies the security notions of
[
CLLZ21
]
, we will need to go trough the “strong” variants of them similarly to
[
CLLZ21
]
.

In the following, we show that SDE with random challenge anti-piracy implies IND-KLA secure PKE-SKL. This means that all known constructions of SDE can be used to construct PKE-SKL.

The definitions of SDE and its random challenge anti-piracy are given below.
The syntax of SDE is identical to that of PKE except that the key generation and decryption algorithms are quantum and the decryption key is quantum.

Definition A.1 (Single-Decryptor Encryption).

A single-decryptor encryption (SDE) scheme
\mathsf{SDE}
is a tuple of three algorithms
(\mathpzc{KG},\mathsf{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec})
.
Below, let
\mathcal{X}
be the message space of
\mathsf{SDE}
.

\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{ek},\mathpzc{dk})
:

The key generation algorithm takes a security parameter
1^{\lambda}
, and outputs an encryption key
\mathsf{ek}
and a decryption key
\mathpzc{dk}
.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{m})\rightarrow\mathsf{ct}
:

The encryption algorithm takes an encryption key
\mathsf{ek}
and a message
\mathsf{m}\in\mathcal{X}
, and outputs a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
.

\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathpzc{dk},\mathsf{ct})\rightarrow\tilde{\mathsf{m}}
:

The decryption algorithm takes a decryption key
\mathpzc{dk}
and a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
, and outputs a value
\tilde{\mathsf{m}}
.

Correctness:

For every
\mathsf{m}\in\mathcal{X}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathpzc{dk},\mathsf{ct})\allowbreak=\mathsf{m}\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{ek},\mathpzc{dk})\leftarrow\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{m})\end{array}\right]=1-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

In the following definition of random challenge anti-piracy, we use the notion of quantum programs with classical inputs and outputs as defined in
Definition

4.7
.

Definition A.2 (Random Challenge Anti-Piracy).

We say that an SDE scheme
\mathsf{SDE}
with the message space
\mathcal{X}
satisfies random-challenge anti-piracy, if it satisfies the following requirement, formalized from the experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{SDE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{rand}\mbox{-}\mathsf{chal}}(1^{\lambda})
between an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
and a challenger
\mathpzc{C}
:

1.

\mathpzc{C}

runs
(\mathsf{ek},\mathpzc{dk})\leftarrow\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})
and sends
\mathsf{ek}
and
\mathpzc{dk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}
sends two (possibly entangled) quantum programs
(\mathpzc{D}_{0},\mathpzc{D}_{1})
with classical inputs and outputs to
\mathpzc{C}
.

3.

For
b\in\{0,1\}
,

\mathpzc{C}

chooses
\mathsf{m}^{*}_{b}\leftarrow\mathcal{X}
, generates
\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{b}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{m}^{*}_{b})
, and runs
\mathpzc{D}_{b}
on input
\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{b}
to obtain an output
\mathsf{m}_{b}
.

\mathpzc{C}
outputs
1
if
\mathsf{m}_{b}=\mathsf{m}^{*}_{b}
for
b\in\{0,1\}
and otherwise outputs
0
as the final output of the experiment.

For any QPT
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{SDE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{rand}\mbox{-}\mathsf{chal}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{SDE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{rand}\mbox{-}\mathsf{chal}}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow 1]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(147)

We prove the following theorem.

Theorem A.3.

If there exists an SDE scheme that satisfies random challenge anti-piracy, there exists an IND-KLA secure PKE-SKL scheme.

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{SDE}=(\mathsf{SDE}.\mathpzc{KG},\mathsf{SDE}.\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{SDE}.\mathpzc{Dec})
be an SDE scheme that satisfies random challenge anti-piracy.
By
Theorem

3.9
, it suffices to construct a one-query OW-KLA secure PKE-SKL scheme.
We construct a one-query OW-KLA secure PKE-SKL scheme
\mathsf{SKL}=(\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{KG},\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Dec},\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Vrfy})
as follows.

\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})
:

Run
(\mathsf{sde}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{sde}.\mathpzc{dk})\leftarrow\mathsf{SDE}.\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})

and output
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}\coloneqq\mathsf{sde}.\mathsf{ek}
,
\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk}\coloneqq\mathsf{sde}.\mathpzc{dk}
, and
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}\coloneqq\mathsf{sde}.\mathsf{ek}
.

\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{m})
:

This is identical to
\mathsf{SDE}.\mathsf{Enc}
.

\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathpzc{dk},\mathsf{ct})
:

This is identical to
\mathsf{SDE}.\mathpzc{Dec}
.

\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{skl}.\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}})
:

Parse
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}=\mathsf{sde}.\mathsf{ek}
,
choose
\mathsf{m}^{*}\leftarrow\mathcal{X}
,
run
\mathsf{ct}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{SDE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{sde}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{m}^{*})
and
\mathsf{m}\leftarrow\mathsf{SDE}.\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathsf{skl}.\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}},\mathsf{ct}^{*})
,
and output
\top
if and only if
\mathsf{m}=\mathsf{m}^{*}
.

Suppose that
\mathsf{SKL}
is not one-query OW-KLA secure. Let
\mathpzc{A}
be a QPT adversary that breaks the one-query OW-KLA security of
\mathsf{SKL}
. We construct a QPT adversary
\mathpzc{B}
that breaks the random challenge anti-piracy of
\mathsf{SDE}
as follows.

\mathpzc{B}(\mathsf{sde}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{sde}.\mathpzc{dk})
:

Set
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}\coloneqq\mathsf{sde}.\mathsf{ek}
,
\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk}\coloneqq\mathsf{sde}.\mathpzc{dk}
, and
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}\coloneqq\mathsf{sde}.\mathsf{ek}
and sends
(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{dk},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk})
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
When
\mathpzc{A}
makes a verification query
\mathsf{skl}.\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}}
,
\mathpzc{B}
returns
1
to
\mathpzc{A}
as the response from the oracle. Let
\mathpzc{D}_{0}
be the quantum program with classical inputs and outputs that takes
\mathsf{ct}
as input and outputs
\mathsf{m}\leftarrow\mathsf{SDE}.\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathsf{skl}.\widetilde{\mathpzc{dk}},\mathsf{ct})
.
When
\mathpzc{A}
sends
\mathsf{RequestChallenge}
, let
\mathpzc{D}_{1}
be the quantum program with classical inputs and outputs, in which
\mathpzc{A}
’s internal state is hardwired, that takes
\mathsf{ct}
as input, runs the rest of
\mathpzc{A}
on the challenge ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
, and outputs
\mathpzc{A}
’s output
\mathsf{m}
.
Output
(\mathpzc{D}_{0},\mathpzc{D}_{1})
.

By the construction of
\mathpzc{B}
and the deferred measurement principle, it is immediate to see that

\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{SDE},\mathpzc{B}}^{\mathsf{rand}\mbox{-}\mathsf{chal}}(\lambda)=\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{SKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ow}\mbox{-}\mathsf{kla}}(\lambda)
. Thus,
\mathpzc{B}
breaks the random challenge anti-piracy of
\mathsf{SDE}
, which is contradiction. Therefore,
\mathsf{SKL}
is one-query OW-KLA secure.
∎

Remark
A.4 (On CPA-Style Anti-Piracy).

We do not know if SDE with CPA-style anti-piracy implies PKE-SKL. On the other hand, it seems possible to show that SDE with the “strong” variant of CPA-style anti-piracy (called strong anti piracy
[
CLLZ21
, Definition 6.11 in the full version]
) implies PKE-SKL.
In the single-bit encryption setting, the security roughly means that the adversary given one decryption key cannot generate two “good” distinguishers that distinguish encryptions of
0
and
1
. Then our idea is to construct a PKE-SKL scheme whose verification algorithm accepts if a returned decryption key gives a “good” distinguisher. Then the strong anti piracy ensures that if the adversary passes the verification, then it cannot keep a “good” distinguisher, which in particular means that it cannot distinguish encryptions of
0
and
1
. Thus, the PKE-SKL scheme is one-query IND-KLA secure.

Appendix B
OW-CPA from CoIC-KLA

We show the following lemma.

Lemma B.1.

If a PKE scheme with a super-polynomial-size message space is CoIC-KLA secure, then it is OW-CPA secure.

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{PKE}=(\mathsf{KG},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
be a CoIC-KLA secure PKE scheme with the message space
\mathcal{X}
such that
|\mathcal{X}|
is super-polynomial in
\lambda
. Toward contradiction, suppose that it is not OW-CPA secure. Let
\mathpzc{A}
be an adversary that breaks OW-CPA security of
\mathsf{PKE}
. Then we construct
\mathpzc{B}
that breaks CoIC-KLA security of
\mathsf{PKE}
as follows.

\mathpzc{B}(\mathsf{ek}_{0},\mathsf{ek}_{1},\mathpzc{dk})
:

Measure
\mathpzc{dk}
to get
(\beta,\mathsf{dk}_{\beta})
for
\beta\in\{0,1\}
.
Choose
(\mathsf{m}_{0}^{*},\mathsf{m}_{1}^{*})\leftarrow\mathcal{X}^{2}
and send
(\mathsf{m}_{0}^{*},\mathsf{m}_{1}^{*})
to the challenger (without making any oracle query). Upon receiving
(\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{0},\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{1})
from the challenger,
run
\mathsf{m}_{\beta}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{dk}_{\beta},\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{\beta})
and
\mathsf{m}_{\beta\oplus 1}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathpzc{A}(\mathsf{ek}_{\beta\oplus 1},\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{\beta\oplus 1})
and output
0
if
\mathsf{m}_{0}^{\prime}=\mathsf{m}_{1}^{\prime}
and
1
otherwise.

Note that the challenger implicitly chooses
a,b\leftarrow\{0,1\}
and generates

\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{0}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek}_{0},\mathsf{m}^{*}_{a})
and
\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek}_{1},\mathsf{m}^{*}_{a\oplus b})
.

\mathpzc{B}
’s goal is to guess
b
.

If
b=0
,
by the correctness of
\mathsf{PKE}
, we have
\Pr[\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{\beta}=\mathsf{m}^{*}_{a}]=1-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.
By the assumption that
\mathpzc{A}
breaks OW-CPA security,
\Pr[\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{\beta\oplus 1}=\mathsf{m}^{*}_{a}]
is non-negligible.
In particular,
\Pr[\mathpzc{B}(\mathsf{ek}_{0},\mathsf{ek}_{1},\mathpzc{dk})\rightarrow 0|b=0]
is non-negligible.
If
b=1
,
by the correctness of
\mathsf{PKE}
, we have
\Pr[\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{\beta}=\mathsf{m}^{*}_{a+\beta}]=1-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.
On the other hand,
\mathsf{ct}^{*}_{\beta\oplus 1}
contains no information of
\mathsf{m}^{*}_{a\oplus\beta}
. Therefore,
\Pr[\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{\beta\oplus 1}=\mathsf{m}^{*}_{a\oplus\beta}]\leq 1/|\mathcal{X}|={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.
Thus,
\Pr[\mathpzc{B}(\mathsf{ek}_{0},\mathsf{ek}_{1},\mathpzc{dk})\rightarrow 0|b=1]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.
Thus,
|2\Pr[\mathpzc{B}(\mathsf{ek}_{0},\mathsf{ek}_{1},\mathpzc{dk})\rightarrow b]-1|=|\Pr[\mathpzc{B}(\mathsf{ek}_{0},\mathsf{ek}_{1},\mathpzc{dk})\rightarrow 0|b=0]-\Pr[\mathpzc{B}(\mathsf{ek}_{0},\mathsf{ek}_{1},\mathpzc{dk})\rightarrow 0|b=1]|
is non-negligible. This contradicts the assumed CoIC-KLA security. Thus,
\mathsf{PKE}
is OW-CPA secure.
∎

Appendix C
Deferred Proofs for PKFE-SKL

In this section, we present the deferred proofs in
Section

7
.

Lemma C.1.

If
\mathsf{PKFE}
is adaptively secure, it holds that
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hybi{1}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{2} =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

Proof.

We construct an adversary
\mathpzc{B}
for
\mathsf{PKFE}
by using the distinguisher
\mathpzc{D}
for these two games.

1.

\mathpzc{B}
is given
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{pk}
and sends
\mathsf{pk}\coloneqq\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{pk}
to
\mathpzc{D}
.
\mathpzc{B}
also generates
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

When
\mathpzc{D}
sends
f_{i}
to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
,
\mathpzc{B}
generates
(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})
,
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},V[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}])
, and
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i})
. Then,
\mathpzc{B}
sends
W[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}]
to its challenger and receives
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{W,i}\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{msk},W[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}])
.
\mathpzc{B}
returns
\mathpzc{fsk}_{i}\coloneqq(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{W,i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{i})
to
\mathpzc{D}
and adds
(f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},\bot)
to
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
.

3.

When
\mathpzc{D}
sends
(f_{i},\mathpzc{fsk}_{i}^{\prime})
to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}
,
\mathpzc{B}
finds an entry
(f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},V_{i})
from
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
and parses
\mathpzc{fsk}_{i}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}^{\prime},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{i}^{\prime})
.
\mathpzc{B}
returns
d\coloneqq\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{i}^{\prime})
. If
V_{i}=\top
,
\mathpzc{B}
does not update the entry. Otherwise,
\mathpzc{B}
updates the entry by setting
V_{i}\coloneqq d
.

4.

When
\mathpzc{D}
sends
(x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast})
,
\mathpzc{B}
generates
\mathsf{K}\leftarrow\mathsf{PRF}.\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
,
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},(x_{0}^{\ast},\bot,\mathsf{K},0,\bot))
.
\mathpzc{B}
sets
X_{0}^{\ast}\coloneqq(x_{0}^{\ast}\|0^{\ell_{\mathsf{pad}}},\bot,\mathsf{K})
and
X_{1}^{\ast}\coloneqq(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk},\bot)
, sends
(X_{0}^{\ast},X_{1}^{\ast})
to its challenger, and receives
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
.
\mathpzc{B}
passes
\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\coloneqq\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
to
\mathpzc{D}
.

5.

\mathpzc{B}
outputs what
\mathpzc{D}
outputs.

By the definition of
W
described in
Figure

1
, if we decrypt
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
by
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{W,i}
, we obtain

•

\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},f(x_{0});\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}))
if
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
is generated from
X_{0}^{\ast}
,

•

z_{i}=\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i},\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast})
if
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
is generated from
X_{1}^{\ast}
since
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}
is a ciphertext of
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}
, where
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}=\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},(x_{0}^{\ast},\bot,\mathsf{K},0,\bot))
. By the correctness of
\mathsf{SKFE}
and the definition of
T[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}]
, it holds that
z_{i}=\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},f(x_{0}^{\ast});\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}))
.

That is, for all
i\in[q]
, it holds that
W[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}](X_{0}^{\ast})=W[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}](X_{1}^{\ast})
, and
\mathpzc{B}
is a valid adversary of PKFE.

It is easy to see that if
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
is an encryption of
X_{0}^{\ast}
and
X_{1}^{\ast}
,
\mathpzc{B}
perfectly simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
, respectively. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma C.2.

If
\mathsf{SKFE}
is adaptively single-ciphertext function private, it holds that
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hybi{2}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{3} =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

Proof.

We construct an adversary
\mathpzc{B}
for
\mathsf{SKFE}
by using the distinguisher
\mathpzc{D}
for these two games.

1.

\mathpzc{B}
generates
(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
, and sends
\mathsf{pk}\coloneqq\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{pk}
to
\mathpzc{D}
.

2.

When
\mathpzc{D}
sends
f_{i}
to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
,
\mathpzc{B}
generates
(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})
, sends a key query
(F_{0,i},F_{1,i})\coloneqq(T[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}],T_{\mathsf{hyb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i])
to its challenger, and receives
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}
.
\mathpzc{B}
also generates
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i})
and
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{W,i}\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{msk},W[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}])
.
\mathpzc{B}
returns
\mathpzc{fsk}_{i}\coloneqq(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{W,i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{i})
to
\mathpzc{D}
and adds
(f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},\bot)
to
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
.

3.

When
\mathpzc{D}
sends
(f_{i},\mathpzc{fsk}_{i}^{\prime})
to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}
,
\mathpzc{B}
finds an entry
(f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},V_{i})
from
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
and parses
\mathpzc{fsk}_{i}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}^{\prime},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{i}^{\prime})
.
\mathpzc{B}
returns
d\coloneqq\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{i}^{\prime})
. If
V_{i}=\top
,
\mathpzc{B}
does not update the entry. Otherwise,
\mathpzc{B}
updates the entry by setting
V_{i}\coloneqq d
.

4.

When
\mathpzc{D}
sends
(x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast})
,
\mathpzc{B}
generates
\mathsf{K}\leftarrow\mathsf{PRF}.\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
, sets
X_{0}^{\ast}\coloneqq(x_{0}^{\ast},\bot,\mathsf{K},0,\bot)
and
X_{1}^{\ast}\coloneqq(x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast},\mathsf{K},0,\bot)
, sends an encryption query
(X_{0}^{\ast},X_{1}^{\ast})
to its challenger, and receives
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
.
\mathpzc{B}
also generates
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{pk},(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk},\bot))
and passes
\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\coloneqq\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
to
\mathpzc{D}
.

5.

\mathpzc{B}
outputs what
\mathpzc{D}
outputs.

Since
i\in[q]
, it holds that
T_{\mathsf{hyb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i](x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast},\mathsf{K},0,\bot)=T[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}](x_{0}^{\ast},\bot,\mathsf{K},0,\bot)
for all
i\in[q]
. That is,
F_{0,i}(X_{0}^{\ast})=F_{1,i}(X_{1}^{\ast})
for all
i\in[q]
and
\mathpzc{B}
is an valid adversary for SKFE.

If
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
is an encryption of
X_{0}^{\ast}
and
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}
is a functional decryption key for
F_{0,i}
,
\mathpzc{B}
perfectly simulate
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
. If
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
is an encryption of
X_{1}^{\ast}
and
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}
is a functional decryption key for
F_{1,i}
,
\mathpzc{B}
perfectly simulate
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma C.3.

If
\mathsf{SKFE}
is adaptively single-ciphertext function private, it holds that
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hybij{3}{q}=1] - \Pr[\hybi{4} =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

Proof.

We construct an adversary
\mathpzc{B}
for
\mathsf{SKFE}
by using the distinguisher
\mathpzc{D}
for these two games.

1.

\mathpzc{B}
generates
(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
, and sends

.

\mathpzc{D}
sends
f_{i}
to

,

generates
(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})
, sends a key query
(F_{0,i},F_{1,i})\coloneqq(T_{\mathsf{hyb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i],T[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}])
to its challenger, and receives
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}
.
\mathpzc{B}
also generates
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i})

returns
\mathpzc{fsk}_{i}\coloneqq(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{W,i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{i})
to
\mathpzc{D}
and adds
(f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},\bot)
to
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
.

3.

When
\mathpzc{D}
sends
(f_{i},\mathpzc{fsk}_{i}^{\prime})
to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}
,
\mathpzc{B}
finds an entry
(f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},V_{i})
from
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
and parses
\mathpzc{fsk}_{i}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}^{\prime},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{i}^{\prime})
.
\mathpzc{B}
returns
d\coloneqq\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{i}^{\prime})
. If
V_{i}=\top
,
\mathpzc{B}
does not update the entry. Otherwise,
\mathpzc{B}
updates the entry by setting
V_{i}\coloneqq d
.

4.

When
\mathpzc{D}
sends
(x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast})
,
\mathpzc{B}
generates
\mathsf{K}\leftarrow\mathsf{PRF}.\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
, sets
X_{0}^{\ast}\coloneqq(x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast},\mathsf{K},q,\bot)
and
X_{1}^{\ast}\coloneqq(x_{1}^{\ast},\bot,\mathsf{K},0,\bot)
, sends an encryption query
(X_{0}^{\ast},X_{1}^{\ast})
to its challenger, and receives
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
.
\mathpzc{B}
also generates
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{pk},(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk},\bot))
and passes
\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\coloneqq\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
to
\mathpzc{D}
.

5.

\mathpzc{B}
outputs what
\mathpzc{D}
outputs.

By the definition of
T_{\mathsf{hyb}}
and
T
, it holds that for all
i\in[q]
,

\displaystyle T_{\mathsf{hyb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i](x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast},\mathsf{K},q,\bot)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},f_{i}(x_{1}^{\ast});\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}))

(148)

\displaystyle=T[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}](x_{1}^{\ast},\bot,\mathsf{K},0,\bot).

(149)

That is,
F_{0,i}(X_{0}^{\ast})=F_{1,i}(X_{1}^{\ast})
for all
i\in[q]
and
\mathpzc{B}
is an valid adversary for SKFE.

If
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
is an encryption of
X_{0}^{\ast}
and
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}
is a functional decryption key for
F_{0,i}
,
\mathpzc{B}
perfectly simulate
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}^{q}
. If
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
is an encryption of
X_{1}^{\ast}
and
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}
is a functional decryption key for
F_{1,i}
,
\mathpzc{B}
perfectly simulate
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma C.4.

If
\mathsf{SKFE}
is adaptively single-ciphertext function private, it holds that
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\sfG_0 =1] - \Pr[\sfG_1 =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

Proof.

We construct an adversary
\mathpzc{B}
for
\mathsf{SKFE}
by using the distinguisher
\mathpzc{D}
for these two games.

1.

\mathpzc{B}
generates
(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
, and sends
\mathsf{pk}\coloneqq\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{pk}
to
\mathpzc{D}
.

2.

When
\mathpzc{D}
sends
f_{i}
to

,

generates
(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})
, sends a key query
(F_{0,i},F_{1,i})\coloneqq(T_{\mathsf{hyb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i],T_{\mathsf{emb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i])
to its challenger, and receives
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}
.
\mathpzc{B}
also generates
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i})
and
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{W,i}\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{msk},W[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}])

returns
\mathpzc{fsk}_{i}\coloneqq(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{W,i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{i})
to
\mathpzc{D}
and adds
(f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},\bot)
to

.

3.

When
\mathpzc{D}
sends
(f_{i},\mathpzc{fsk}_{i}^{\prime})
to

,
\mathpzc{B}
finds an entry
(f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},V_{i})
from

and parses
\mathpzc{fsk}_{i}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}^{\prime},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{i}^{\prime})
.
\mathpzc{B}
returns
d\coloneqq\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{i}^{\prime})
. If
V_{i}=\top
,
\mathpzc{B}
does not update the entry. Otherwise,
\mathpzc{B}
updates the entry by setting
V_{i}\coloneqq d
.

4.

When
\mathpzc{D}
sends
(x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast})
,
\mathpzc{B}
generates
\mathsf{K}\leftarrow\mathsf{PRF}.\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
and
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{j},f_{j}(x_{0}^{\ast});\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{j}))
, sets
X_{0}^{\ast}\coloneqq(x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast},\mathsf{K},j-1,\bot)
and
X_{1}^{\ast}\coloneqq(x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast},\mathsf{K},j,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast})
, sends an encryption query
(X_{0}^{\ast},X_{1}^{\ast})
to its challenger, and receives
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
.
\mathpzc{B}
generates
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{pk},(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk},\bot))
and passes
\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\coloneqq\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
to

5.

\mathpzc{B}
outputs what
\mathpzc{D}
outputs.

By the definitions of
T_{\mathsf{hyb}}
and
T_{\mathsf{emb}}
, it holds that

\displaystyle T_{\mathsf{hyb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i](x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast},\mathsf{K},j-1,\bot)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},f_{i}(x_{0}^{\ast});\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}))

(150)

\displaystyle=T_{\mathsf{emb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i](x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast},\mathsf{K},j,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast})

(151)

for all
i\in[j,q]
since
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
is an encryption of
f_{j}(x_{0})
.
it also holds that

\displaystyle T_{\mathsf{hyb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i](x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast},\mathsf{K},j-1,\bot)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},f_{i}(x_{1}^{\ast});\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i}))

(152)

\displaystyle=T_{\mathsf{emb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i](x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast},\mathsf{K},j,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast})

(153)

for all
i\in[1,j-1]
. Hence, for all
i\in[q]
, it holds that
F_{0,i}(X_{0}^{\ast})=F_{1,i}(X_{1}\ast)
and
\mathpzc{B}
is an valid adversary for SKFE. If
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
is an encryption of
X_{0}^{\ast}
and
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}
is a functional decryption key for

,

perfectly simulate
\mathsf{G}_{0}
. If
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
is an encryption of
X_{1}^{\ast}
and
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}
is a functional decryption key for
F_{1,i}
,
\mathpzc{B}
perfectly simulate
\mathsf{G}_{1}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma C.5.

If
\mathsf{SKL}
IND-KLA, it holds that
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\sfG_3 =1] - \Pr[\sfG_4 =1]}={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

Proof.

We focus on the case where the adversary returns a valid
\mathpzc{fsk}_{j}=(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{W,j},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{j})
, which is the answer to the
j
-th key query, since
f_{j}(x_{0}^{\ast})=f_{j}(x_{1}^{\ast})
must hold if
\mathpzc{fsk}_{j}
is not returned.
Hence
f_{j}(x_{0}^{\ast})\neq f_{j}(x_{1}^{\ast})
is allowed in this case.

We construct an adversary
\mathpzc{B}
for
\mathsf{SKL}
by using the distinguisher
\mathpzc{D}
for these two games.

1.

\mathpzc{B}
is given
(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{\ast},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}^{\ast})
and sets
(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{j},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{j})\coloneqq(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}^{\ast},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}^{\ast})
.

2.

\mathpzc{B}
generates
(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
,
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
, and
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
, and sends
\mathsf{pk}\coloneqq\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{pk}
to
\mathpzc{D}
.

3.

When
\mathpzc{D}
sends the
i
-th query
f_{i}
to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
, if
i\neq j
,
\mathpzc{B}
generates
(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{KG}(1^{\lambda})
. For all
i\in[q]
,
\mathpzc{B}
generates
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},T_{\mathsf{emb}}[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},i])
,
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i})
, and
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{W,i}\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{msk},\allowbreak W[f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{i},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{ct}_{i}])
, and returns
\mathpzc{fsk}_{i}\coloneqq(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{W,i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{i})
to
\mathpzc{D}
. Note that
\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{j}=\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}
is given from the challenger. If
i\neq j
,
\mathpzc{B}
adds
(f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},\bot)
to
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
.
If
i=j
,
\mathpzc{B}
adds
(f_{j},\bot,\bot)
to
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
.

4.

When
\mathpzc{D}
sends
(f_{i},\mathpzc{fsk}_{i}^{\prime})
to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}
,
\mathpzc{B}
finds an entry
(f_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},V_{i})
from
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
and parses
\mathpzc{fsk}_{i}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{i}^{\prime},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{i}^{\prime})
.

•

If
f_{i}\neq f_{j}
,
\mathpzc{B}
returns
d\coloneqq\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}_{i},\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{i}^{\prime})
since
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{vk}_{i}\neq\bot
. If
V_{i}=\top

\mathpzc{B}
does not update the entry. Otherwise,
\mathpzc{B}
updates the entry by setting
V_{i}\coloneqq d
.

•

Else if
f_{i}=f_{j}
,
\mathpzc{B}
sends
\mathsf{skl}.\mathpzc{sk}_{j}^{\prime}
to its challenger (
O_{\mathtt{\mathsf{SKL}.\mathpzc{Vrfy}}}
of IND-KLA), receives the result
d_{j}
, and passes
d_{j}
to
\mathpzc{D}
. If
V_{j}=\top
,
\mathpzc{B}
does not update the entry. Otherwise,
\mathpzc{B}
updates the entry by setting
V_{j}\coloneqq d_{j}
.

5.

When
\mathpzc{D}
sends
(x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast})
,
\mathpzc{B}
generates
\mathsf{K}\leftarrow\mathsf{PRF}.\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
and
\mathsf{K}_{\neq\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{j}}=\mathsf{Puncture}(\mathsf{K},\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{j})
, sends
(x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast})
to its challenger, and receives
\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{j},f_{j}(x_{\mathsf{coin}}^{\ast}))
.
\mathpzc{B}
generates
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{msk},\allowbreak(x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast},\mathsf{K}_{\neq\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ek}_{j}},j,\mathsf{skl}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}))
and
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{pk},(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{sk},\bot))
and passes
\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\coloneqq\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
to
\mathpzc{D}
.

6.

\mathpzc{B}
outputs what
\mathpzc{D}
outputs.

It is easy to see that
\mathpzc{B}
perfectly simulates
\mathsf{G}_{3}
and
\mathsf{G}_{4}
if
\mathsf{coin}=0
and
\mathsf{coin}=1
, respectively.
This completes the proof.
∎
